And then independently you go into science and then you put them together. I don't know if that is really the case, or if it's really something that can be built from inside, scientific philosophy. I think your section 15 goes well on those lines. It's just we need to develop a bit more. So why might be a better explanation, like give some actual arguments. You mentioned some authors like Aristotle, and some others, like Leibniz, Aquinas, and so on. So there must be a real ground. I don't think they were stupid or they were not logical enough to actually give a good reason that a naturalist would necessarily not be able to reply. Or at least the replication process could go indefinitely. Because if it goes to one definitive answer, then we know what's the correct one. Obviously we don't know because we don't know the answer. But as I said, there should be reasons to come up with different ontological realities. So yeah, I think the idea of a strongest case position would be nice.


Yes. And I think your request changes the task in an important way: **you don't want me to defend theism by finding gaps in naturalism; you want me to construct the strongest serious theistic argument and see whether it actually earns the extra ontology.**

I also accept your use of **faith**. You're using it in a broad epistemic sense: trust or commitment based on reasons, experience, testimony, inference, etc., rather than “believing without evidence.” That's a perfectly legitimate usage. In fact, philosophy of religion explicitly distinguishes fideism—the view that faith should operate independently of reason—from traditions of *natural theology*, which try to give rational arguments for religious belief. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

And I think we should be very strict here:

> **If theism is going to beat naturalism, it needs positive reasons of its own. “Science can't answer this” is not enough.**

So let's make the best case.

---

# 1. First, let's distinguish the positions properly

There's a danger in saying “naturalism vs. theism” because **naturalism itself comes in several forms**.

At minimum:

### Methodological naturalism

> When doing science, we restrict ourselves to natural causes and entities.

This is almost uncontroversial as a description of scientific practice. It does **not** entail atheism. A Christian physicist can do perfectly ordinary physics without putting God into the equations. The philosophical literature explicitly distinguishes this from ontological naturalism. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

### Ontological/metaphysical naturalism

> Reality contains nothing beyond the natural/physical order.

That's the much stronger position.

And **that's** the position that theism has to challenge.

This distinction matters because:

> “Science doesn't use God”

doesn't imply:

> “God doesn't exist.”

That's not a scientific inference.

---

# 2. The strongest theistic argument is not really about mathematics

After everything we've discussed, I actually think we should **set the mathematics argument aside for a moment**.

Your criticisms of it are good.

The argument from mathematical beauty or effectiveness is vulnerable to:

* abstraction,
* compression,
* selection effects,
* mathematics developing in response to physics,
* mathematics exploring enormous spaces of possible structures,
* and our selectively celebrating the structures that happen to be physically useful.

So I would **not** try to make mathematics the primary argument.

Instead, I'd start somewhere much more fundamental:

> **Why is there a contingent, intelligible reality at all?**

That's the territory of Aristotle, Ibn Sina, Aquinas and Leibniz, although they formulate the problem differently. The contemporary literature still treats contingency, necessity, grounding and the Principle of Sufficient Reason as the central issues in cosmological arguments. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][3])

---

# 3. The argument begins with something you already accept

You don't need God to get started.

Look around you.

There are things that **exist but could have failed to exist**.

You exist.

I exist.

This table exists.

The Earth exists.

The particular arrangement of matter in the universe exists.

You don't think:

> “The Earth could not possibly have failed to exist.”

You think:

> “It exists, but it didn't have to.”

That's what philosophers mean by **contingency**.

A contingent thing is, roughly:

> Something whose existence is not necessary; it could have been otherwise.

A necessary thing, by contrast, is something that cannot fail to exist.

And here's where the theistic argument begins.

---

# 4. The key intuition: contingent reality doesn't contain the reason for its own existence

Suppose we find a completely ordinary contingent thing:

> A tree.

Why does the tree exist?

Because of:

* the seed,
* the previous tree,
* sunlight,
* water,
* biological processes,
* etc.

Those things explain the tree's existence.

Now take the whole causal chain.

You can say:

> The tree exists because of the seed.

Then:

> Why the seed?

Because of the previous tree.

And so on.

Now imagine, just for argument's sake, that this chain goes back indefinitely.

The theist asks:

> **Have I actually explained why there is a chain of contingent things at all?**

This is where the argument becomes much more interesting.

It isn't primarily:

> “Everything needs a cause.”

That's a weak version, because then someone immediately asks:

> “What caused God?”

Instead:

> **Why is there a contingent reality rather than no contingent reality?**

That is Leibniz's move.

Leibniz explicitly formulated the Principle of Sufficient Reason as the principle that contingent truths have a sufficient reason why they are true rather than otherwise, and argued that the sufficient reason for the entire contingent series must lie beyond that series. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][4])

---

# 5. Now comes your strongest objection

You say:

> **Why can't I just say that the contingent universe is a brute fact?**

And here's the crucial point:

### You absolutely can.

Nothing in logic immediately prevents you.

That's why I don't think the cosmological argument is:

> “Brute facts are logically impossible.”

It isn't.

The real argument is:

> **Brute contingency is explanatorily inferior to a necessary ground.**

That's an abductive argument—an inference to the best explanation—not a deduction from pure logic.

And now we can actually compare the two hypotheses.

---

# 6. Hypothesis A: brute naturalism

You say:

> Reality exists.

Why?

> No reason.

Why these laws?

> No reason.

Why this mathematical structure?

> No reason.

Why is there something rather than nothing?

> No reason.

Why is reality regular?

> That's just a fundamental fact.

This is internally coherent.

And there is something very attractive about it:

### It's extremely parsimonious.

You don't add God.

You don't add another realm.

You don't add a supernatural substance.

You just stop.

That's a **real strength** of naturalism.

---

# 7. Hypothesis B: theism

The theist says:

> Contingent reality exists because it is grounded in a necessary reality.

And then identifies that necessary reality with God.

Now you ask:

> Why does God exist?

And the answer is:

> God isn't contingent. God is necessary.

And here we need to be extremely careful.

“Necessary” doesn't mean:

> “Something that we don't feel like explaining.”

It means something much stronger:

> **There is no possible world in which this being fails to exist.**

That's a modal claim.

So the theist isn't supposed to say:

> “God exists because we can't think of a reason for God.”

They're saying:

> **God's non-existence is metaphysically impossible.**

That is a very strong claim.

And now you can attack it.

---

# 8. Your immediate objection is exactly the right one

You can say:

> Why can't the physical universe itself be necessary?

This is one of the most important objections.

And the theist has to demonstrate that the physical universe **is contingent**.

But that's difficult.

We know that the particular contents of our universe could have been different.

We can imagine:

* different particle masses,
* different constants,
* different initial conditions,
* different structures.

But does that establish that **there could have been nothing physical whatsoever?**

No.

That's a much stronger modal claim.

And contemporary discussions recognize that moving from the contingency of individual things to the contingency of the whole universe is nontrivial. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][3])

So the naturalist can reasonably say:

> “Maybe the fundamental physical order is necessary.”

If that's coherent, the theist has lost one of the easiest routes to God.

---

# 9. But now the theist can make a subtler argument

The theist can say:

> Even if the physical laws were necessary, why would *those particular laws* constitute the necessary structure?

Suppose there are many mathematically coherent structures:

$$
S_1, S_2, S_3, \ldots
$$

Our universe instantiates \(S_{17}\).

Why \(S_{17}\)?

The naturalist can say:

> “Because that's the structure that exists.”

Again, that's coherent.

But the theist says:

> “You're treating a contingent selection as brute.”

And this is where Leibniz's principle becomes powerful.

The question isn't:

> “Can you imagine a reason?”

It's:

> **Is there a principled reason why one contingent possibility is actual rather than another?**

If the answer is no, then ultimate reality contains an irreducible brute selection.

The theist regards that as metaphysically unsatisfactory.

---

# 10. But—and this is important—you have a devastating reply

You can say:

> **Why should I care?**

Not psychologically.

Philosophically.

You can say:

> “You've demonstrated that I cannot give a further reason. You haven't demonstrated that there must be one.”

Exactly.

This is where the **Principle of Sufficient Reason** carries the entire argument.

If PSR is true:

> Contingent facts require sufficient explanation.

Then brute naturalism is in serious trouble.

If PSR isn't true:

> There can simply be unexplained contingent facts.

Then the cosmological argument loses its primary engine.

And this is why Hume's and Kant's criticisms of cosmological arguments matter so much. Historically, they attacked precisely the assumptions concerning causation, contingency and explanatory necessity that such arguments rely upon. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][3])

---

# 11. So why would anyone believe the Principle of Sufficient Reason?

Now we're getting somewhere.

There are several possible arguments.

### Argument 1: Rational inquiry presupposes it

Whenever you encounter something unexplained, you naturally ask:

> Why?

Science itself is essentially the systematic search for explanations.

If we discovered a phenomenon that had no apparent reason whatsoever, we'd normally continue investigating.

So perhaps the commitment to explanation isn't merely psychological—it is constitutive of rational inquiry.

But you can reply:

> Science searches for explanations where explanations are available. That doesn't prove reality guarantees them.

Correct.

So this argument doesn't establish PSR.

---

# 12. Argument 2: Brute contingency is inherently arbitrary

This is stronger.

Suppose:

> X exists contingently.

There is no reason whatsoever why X exists rather than not-X.

Then the difference between:

> “X exists”

and

> “X doesn't exist”

has no grounding whatsoever.

The theist argues that such ultimate arbitrariness is metaphysically problematic.

This is very close to Leibniz's intuition.

But again, you can say:

> “Why is arbitrariness incoherent?”

And the theist has to answer.

It isn't obviously logically incoherent.

So again: **the argument is not deductive.**

---

# 13. Argument 3: denying PSR creates an arbitrary stopping point

Here's perhaps the strongest version.

You already accept explanation at ordinary levels:

> Why did the rock fall?

Gravity.

> Why does gravity behave that way?

General relativity.

> Why that fundamental structure?

At some point, you stop.

The theist asks:

> **Why is that stopping point legitimate?**

If your answer is:

> “Because that's where we currently stop,”

that's obviously temporary.

But if you say:

> “Because there is nothing further to explain,”

you've made a metaphysical claim.

And the theist says:

> “Fine. Defend that claim.”

This is where the dispute becomes philosophical rather than scientific.

---

# 14. But naturalism has an excellent response

It can say:

> **Explanation is a relation between facts, not an obligation imposed on reality.**

This is a very powerful thought.

The universe doesn't have a duty to provide an explanation.

Maybe:

> Some facts explain other facts.

But eventually:

> There are fundamental facts that explain nothing further.

That's not irrational.

Indeed, **every explanatory system has to terminate somewhere**, unless you accept an infinite regress.

So why is the naturalist stopping point worse than the theist's?

This is the point where the theist has to do real work.

---

# 15. And here I think Aquinas is actually more interesting than the simplified “first cause” version

People often present Aquinas as:

> Everything has a cause → therefore first cause → God.

That's not really the sophisticated Thomistic argument.

Aquinas is interested in **ontological dependence**, not merely temporal beginnings.

Imagine a lamp illuminating a room.

The light's existence depends continuously on the lamp.

You don't solve the dependence problem by saying:

> “Maybe the chain of lamps goes back infinitely.”

An infinite collection of dependent things is still dependent.

That's the Thomistic intuition.

So Aquinas's argument is closer to:

> **If reality consists entirely of things whose existence is derivative/dependent, then the whole structure remains derivative/dependent.**

Therefore:

> There must be something whose existence isn't derivative.

This is sometimes called a **vertical** or *per se* causal argument, rather than a historical chain going backward in time.

The contemporary literature explicitly distinguishes these grounding/dependence versions from temporal cosmological arguments. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][3])

---

# 16. Now the naturalist's best response

And it's a very good one:

> **Why can't the fundamental physical laws simply be the non-dependent reality?**

That's exactly the right response.

If the naturalist says:

> “Quantum fields/laws/whatever are fundamental and not dependent on anything deeper,”

then the Thomist needs to show:

> **Why is that metaphysically impossible?**

And that is much harder.

You cannot simply reply:

> “But they're contingent.”

You have to establish their contingency.

This is why I would not tell you that Aquinas has *proven* God.

He hasn't.

He has identified a profound metaphysical problem:

> **Can an entirely dependent reality exist without something non-dependent grounding it?**

That's a serious question.

But whether the answer must be “God” is another question.

---

# 17. And this is where theism has another problem

Suppose we establish:

> There is a necessary ground.

We've still got an enormous distance to:

> **The God of classical theism.**

We need to establish things like:

* necessity,
* unity,
* simplicity,
* intelligence,
* intentionality,
* causal power,
* perhaps goodness,
* perhaps omniscience,
* perhaps omnipotence.

That's a huge inferential chain.

The cosmological argument by itself doesn't automatically give you Christianity, Judaism or Islam.

At most, in its strongest versions, it gets you toward something like:

> **a necessary, non-dependent ground of contingent reality.**

Then you need further arguments.

This is why natural theology is an entire philosophical field rather than one proof.

---

# 18. So where does faith legitimately enter?

This connects directly to your correction.

Suppose someone has:

1. philosophical arguments for a necessary ground,
2. arguments from consciousness,
3. arguments from morality,
4. religious experience,
5. historical evidence,
6. personal religious experience,
7. testimony,
8. theological coherence.

They may then have a **cumulative case**.

Their faith isn't:

> “I arbitrarily believe God.”

It's more like:

> **“Given the totality of my evidence and experience, I judge the theistic worldview to be true.”**

That is perfectly compatible with rationality.

The philosophical literature on religious epistemology explicitly considers evidential, experiential, rationalistic and other models of religious belief; faith need not mean fideism. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][5])

And this actually makes your question more interesting.

You don't need to demand:

> “Give me a scientific proof of God.”

You can instead ask:

> **“Is there a cumulative body of evidence and argument that makes theism more rationally credible than naturalism?”**

That's a much more sophisticated question.

---

# 19. And there is one thing I want to correct from our earlier conversation

You said:

> “Maybe there is some knowledge inaccessible to science.”

I think we should be careful.

You **don't need to establish some mysterious second faculty of knowledge** in order to defend theism.

You could simply say:

> Science is one epistemic method, optimized for empirical investigation of the natural world.

Then philosophy can reason about:

* logic,
* modality,
* causation,
* ontology,
* mathematics,
* epistemology,
* values,
* consciousness,
* metaphysical explanation.

Whether those forms of reasoning ultimately succeed is another matter.

But this is not an ad hoc escape hatch for religion.

Philosophy was doing this **before modern science existed**, and contemporary philosophy of science itself is concerned with questions that cannot simply be settled by experiments. Scientific methodology involves observation and experiment but also reasoning, model construction, conceptual analysis and theoretical interpretation. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][6])

So the theist doesn't need to say:

> “There is secret non-scientific knowledge.”

They can say:

> **“Science is not the whole of rational inquiry.”**

That's a much weaker and more defensible claim.

---

# 20. Now: can the theist actually beat the naturalist?

Here is my honest assessment.

If by “beat” you mean:

> **Prove that naturalism is logically impossible and God necessarily exists**

—I don't think the classical arguments accomplish that.

But if you mean:

> **Give a positive, rationally serious reason for preferring theism to brute naturalism**

—yes, there is a real argument.

And I would formulate the strongest version something like this:

> **1. There are contingent realities.**
>
> **2. Contingent realities do not contain within themselves the sufficient reason for their existence.**
>
> **3. If all reality were contingent, then the existence of reality as a whole would remain unexplained.**
>
> **4. It is more rational to posit a necessary ground than to accept ultimate contingent existence as brute.**
>
> **5. A necessary ground capable of accounting for contingent reality is better understood as something fundamentally different from the contingent physical order.**
>
> **6. Further philosophical arguments can then be used to argue that such a ground is unitary, non-material, intelligent, etc.**
>
> **7. This gives us a route toward classical theism.**

The key vulnerability is **premise 4**.

That's where you should attack.

And interestingly, I think **you have already identified the attack**:

> “Why is brute contingency actually unacceptable?”

If the naturalist can answer:

> “It isn't. Ultimate reality simply has fundamental facts, and there is no reason to believe every fact requires an explanation,”

then the cosmological argument has not defeated naturalism.

But if the theist can establish independently that **reality is intelligible through and through**, and that ultimate brute contingency is incompatible with the rational structure we already rely on, then the balance starts to shift.

That's the battle.

---

## 21. And there's an even deeper possibility

I think your discussion has now reached a point where **“theism vs. naturalism” is actually premature**.

There are at least three serious possibilities:

**A. Brute naturalism**

> There is a fundamental physical reality. Full stop.

**B. Necessary structural reality**

> The fundamental mathematical/physical structure is not contingent but necessary.

**C. Theistic metaphysics**

> The contingent physical order is grounded in a necessary rational reality.

And there is a fourth:

**D. Skepticism**

> We don't currently have enough evidence to know which, if any, is correct.

I actually think **D is a completely respectable position** while we investigate the arguments.

You don't have to choose naturalism merely because the theistic argument hasn't yet convinced you.

---

# 22. And this gives us a much better experiment

Rather than me continuing to tell you why theism *might* work, I suggest we now do something more rigorous:

### **Take the strongest naturalist position you have just articulated and attack it from the theistic side.**

In particular, we should examine four questions:

1. **Can there coherently be an ultimate brute fact?**
2. **Can an infinite regress of contingent/dependent explanations actually explain anything?**
3. **Can the physical universe itself be necessary?**
4. **If a necessary ground exists, why should it be personal/intelligent rather than an impersonal mathematical or metaphysical structure?**

Those four questions would take us directly through **Aquinas → Leibniz → Hume → Kant → contemporary analytic philosophy**, without giving either side an easy ride.

And I think the fourth is especially important for you, because even if the theist wins the argument for **“there is something necessary beyond the contingent physical world,”** that still doesn't get them anywhere close to **God as a mind**. That is where the strongest naturalist objection is waiting.

[1]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/fideism/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Fideism (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/naturalism/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Naturalism (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[3]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/cosmological-argument/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Cosmological Argument (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[4]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/leibniz/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[5]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/philosophy-religion/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Philosophy of Religion (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[6]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/scientific-method/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Scientific Method (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"


Ok let's do that. And then I would like to expand in point 6 of how you would reasonably withing a philosofical, metaphysical and teologically consistent manner into deducing or defending that the necessary entity would deserved to be called god


Yes. And I think we can now make the discussion considerably more rigorous.

I want to do two things:

1. **Take the four challenges seriously from the naturalist side and then give the strongest theistic response to each.**
2. Then follow the theistic argument all the way from **“there is a necessary ground” → “that ground has properties that warrant calling it God.”**

And I want to be explicit about something: **the second step is not automatic.** Aquinas himself recognized this. His “Five Ways” don't by themselves establish everything we mean by God; he thinks they establish a starting point from which further metaphysical arguments establish simplicity, immateriality, perfection, eternity, knowledge, unity, etc. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

So let's build the case rather than smuggle God into the conclusion.

---

# Part I — Can naturalism stop with brute facts?

## 1. Can there coherently be an ultimate brute fact?

Yes.

I think we should concede this immediately.

There is no logical contradiction in saying:

> “Reality has some fundamental facts, and there is no further reason why those facts obtain.”

For example:

> The electron has charge \(e\).

Perhaps that's simply a fundamental fact.

Or:

> The fundamental laws have this mathematical structure.

Perhaps that's simply a fundamental fact.

Or even:

> There exists a physical reality with these fundamental properties.

Perhaps that's simply a fundamental fact.

**Nothing in formal logic rules that out.**

This is important because it means that the theist cannot say:

> “Brute facts are impossible.”

The interesting claim has to be weaker:

> **Ultimate brute contingency is a worse metaphysical explanation than necessary reality.**

That's an *abductive* claim rather than a logical proof.

And this is essentially where contemporary versions of the cosmological argument live: they debate whether contingent reality requires grounding/explanation and whether a necessary reality provides a better stopping point. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

---

# 2. So why might brute facts be unsatisfactory?

Here I think we can formulate the theistic intuition much more precisely than “humans don't like unanswered questions.”

Consider two worlds:

### World A

There is no universe.

### World B

There is a universe with exactly these fundamental laws, constants and structures.

Suppose both are genuinely possible.

Then ask:

> Why is B actual rather than A?

The brute-fact naturalist says:

> There is no answer.

The Leibnizian says:

> That's an arbitrary difference between possibilities, and reality should not contain an unexplained arbitrary selection.

That's the **Principle of Sufficient Reason** (PSR).

Leibniz's version says, roughly, that there is a sufficient reason why every fact is so rather than otherwise, even if we cannot discover that reason. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][3])

Now, notice what has happened.

The theist is **not** saying:

> “I personally don't like brute facts.”

They're proposing a substantive metaphysical principle:

> **Reality is intelligible through and through.**

That's a serious principle.

---

# 3. But is PSR true?

This is the real battlefield.

And here I think the naturalist has a formidable case.

They can say:

> “Why should every contingent fact have a sufficient reason?”

We observe that:

* many physical events have explanations;
* science is extraordinarily successful at finding them;
* regularity is overwhelmingly confirmed.

But that doesn't establish:

> **Every fact whatsoever has an explanation.**

That's an enormous extrapolation.

The Principle of Sufficient Reason is explicitly recognized as a **powerful but controversial** philosophical principle. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][3])

And there is a particularly interesting problem:

### If you demand a reason for everything, you eventually need a reason for the PSR itself.

Why should reality be completely intelligible?

If you answer:

> “Because otherwise reality would be arbitrary,”

the naturalist can respond:

> “And why is ultimate arbitrariness impossible?”

You haven't established it.

So **PSR is not a theorem of rationality**.

It's a metaphysical commitment.

---

# 4. But here's the strongest theistic counterargument

The theist can turn the tables:

> “You are already relying on the assumption that reality is intelligible.”

Think about science.

Science doesn't merely assume:

> “Sometimes things are regular.”

Its entire enterprise assumes that:

> **There are discoverable reasons why things behave as they do.**

When physicists encounter an unexplained anomaly, they don't normally say:

> “Perhaps this is an irreducible brute fact; let's stop.”

They investigate.

So the theist asks:

> Why should the intelligibility we trust everywhere else suddenly disappear at the foundation of reality?

This doesn't prove PSR, but it creates a philosophical tension:

### Naturalist:

> Most things have explanations, but perhaps the ultimate facts don't.

### Theist:

> Why should explanatory intelligibility suddenly terminate at precisely the deepest level?

And I think that's a genuinely powerful theistic challenge.

---

# 5. The naturalist's answer is nevertheless quite good

They can say:

> Because explanation has to terminate somewhere.

That's actually compelling.

Imagine:

A explained by B
B explained by C
C explained by D
D explained by E...

If there is no terminus, have we explained anything?

The naturalist says:

> “Eventually we arrive at fundamental reality. That's precisely what ‘fundamental’ means.”

And there's nothing obviously incoherent about that.

The theist replies:

> “Then why can't God simply be your arbitrary stopping point?”

And the naturalist says:

> “Exactly.”

This is probably the single most important objection to the cosmological argument.

---

# 6. Why doesn't “God” just become an arbitrary stopping point?

This is where the distinction between **contingent** and **necessary** becomes important.

Suppose I say:

> “God exists, but God could have failed to exist.”

Then God doesn't solve the problem.

You've merely replaced:

> contingent universe

with:

> contingent God + universe.

But classical theism says something radically different:

> **God is not contingent. God is necessary.**

Meaning:

> God cannot fail to exist.

And therefore the proposed explanatory structure is:

**contingent reality**

↓ depends upon

**necessary reality**

rather than:

**contingent reality**

↓ depends upon

**another contingent thing**

↓ depends upon

**another contingent thing**

forever.

This is why the necessary/contingent distinction matters.

The contemporary literature makes precisely this point: a necessary being is not supposed to be “self-explanatory” in the sense of having an explanation for its existence; rather, its existence is taken as ultimate and non-derivative. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

---

# 7. But here's your devastating response again

You can say:

> **Why can't fundamental physical reality itself be necessary?**

Exactly.

And I think this is the hardest objection to the cosmological argument.

Suppose the naturalist says:

> “The fundamental laws aren't contingent. They are necessary.”

Then:

> Why God?

The theist cannot simply answer:

> “Because contingent things require explanations.”

Because you've just denied that the fundamental physical reality is contingent.

So now the argument has to establish:

> **Physical reality cannot itself be necessary.**

And that's much harder.

---

# 8. Can we establish that?

There are some arguments.

One is:

> We can conceive of different physical laws.

But that's not sufficient.

Because:

> “I can conceive of \(L_1\) and \(L_2\)”

doesn't establish:

> “\(L_1\) and \(L_2\) are both metaphysically possible.”

The naturalist can say:

> “You don't know what the ultimate laws are. Perhaps the fundamental structure of reality is necessary, and alternative mathematical structures simply aren't physically possible.”

That's perfectly coherent.

So **the cosmological argument cannot simply assume the physical world is contingent**.

It needs to argue for it.

And contemporary discussions explicitly identify the contingency of the universe and the concept of necessary being as central controversial premises. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

---

# 9. What about an infinite regress?

Now let's take the second question.

Suppose every contingent thing is explained by another contingent thing:

$$
A \leftarrow B \leftarrow C \leftarrow D \leftarrow \cdots
$$

Does an infinite regress solve the problem?

This depends enormously on what kind of explanation we're talking about.

If we're talking about **temporal causation**, an infinite past is one question.

But Aquinas's argument is more interesting because he is concerned with a **simultaneous dependence structure**.

Think about this:

> A hand moves a stick.
> The stick moves a stone.

The stick doesn't possess the relevant causal power independently; its causal activity is derivative from the hand.

Now imagine:

> A → B → C → D

where every member's causal power is entirely derivative from something else.

Even if there are infinitely many members, the Thomist asks:

> **Where does the derivative causal power ultimately come from?**

This is why Aquinas's argument is not fundamentally about the universe having a beginning in time. His “first cause” is better understood as a **first in ontological dependence**, not necessarily first chronologically. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

---

# 10. But can an infinite regress of dependence exist?

Again, maybe.

The naturalist can say:

> “Perhaps every layer is dependent on another layer, and there simply is no independent layer.”

The Thomist says:

> “Then nothing in the system has non-derivative existence.”

And asks:

> How can there be anything at all if everything derives its existence from something else?

That's a deeper argument than:

> “Infinite things are impossible.”

Aquinas isn't necessarily arguing that an infinite number of causes is mathematically impossible.

He's asking:

> **Can there be an infinite hierarchy of entirely derivative reality without something non-derivative?**

And that's a much more interesting question.

---

# 11. The naturalist can again say: yes

They might simply reject the metaphysical principle:

> “Every dependent reality requires something independent.”

Or they could say:

> “The entire infinite structure is fundamental.”

And again we're back to brute facts.

This is becoming a pattern.

And that's actually revealing.

### Naturalism ultimately says:

> At some point, reality is just fundamental.

### Theism says:

> The fundamental reality is not the physical universe but a necessary ground.

So the real question becomes:

> **Which stopping point is better justified?**

And that brings us directly to your final question.

---

# Part II — Suppose we grant a necessary ground. Why call it God?

This is where things get really interesting.

Because **“necessary being” ≠ “God”**.

Suppose I grant:

> There is a necessary foundation of contingent reality.

You have not yet established:

> It is conscious.

Or:

> It is intelligent.

Or:

> It has a will.

Or:

> It is good.

Or:

> It is omniscient.

Or:

> It is omnipotent.

So how does classical theism get there?

There is actually a remarkably systematic route.

And this is where Aquinas is much more sophisticated than the popular presentation of his arguments.

---

# 12. Step 1: The necessary ground must be independent

Suppose \(N\) is the ultimate ground.

If \(N\) depends on something else for its existence, then \(N\) isn't ultimate.

So:

> **Necessary ground → non-dependent existence.**

This is the basic starting point.

---

# 13. Step 2: It cannot be composed of more fundamental parts

Suppose God were composed of:

$$
A + B.
$$

Then we can ask:

> Why are A and B united in this particular way?

If the existence of the whole depends on the parts and their combination, then the whole isn't metaphysically ultimate.

This leads classical theism toward **divine simplicity**.

God isn't supposed to be a very complicated object made out of metaphysical components.

Rather:

> **God is metaphysically simple: not composed of more fundamental constituents.**

Aquinas explicitly develops this after his initial arguments for the first cause. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

This is important because it answers one of your earlier objections:

> “Why doesn't God just add another unexplained layer?”

Classical theism's answer is:

> **God isn't another object within the explanatory hierarchy. God is supposed to be the non-composite ground of the hierarchy itself.**

Whether you buy that is another matter.

---

# 14. Step 3: It cannot be material

Matter is characterized by things like:

* composition,
* change,
* spatial extension,
* dependence upon physical conditions.

But if the necessary ground were material, the theist asks:

> Why this particular material configuration?

A material object is normally something composed of parts and capable of being otherwise.

So classical theism reasons toward:

> **The ultimate necessary reality is immaterial.**

This is one reason the classical God is not imagined as:

> “a very powerful invisible object somewhere outside the universe.”

That's a fundamentally different conception.

God is supposed to be **ontologically prior to physical objects altogether**.

---

# 15. Step 4: It must be uncaused

This one follows naturally.

If something causes God to exist, God isn't ultimate.

Therefore:

> **Necessary ground → uncaused.**

But be careful:

> “Uncaused”

doesn't mean:

> “random.”

It means:

> **Its existence is not derived from another cause.**

---

# 16. Step 5: Why only one?

Suppose there are two necessary beings:

$$
N_1,\quad N_2.
$$

What distinguishes them?

There must be some property possessed by \(N_1\) but not \(N_2\).

But then we have:

> \(N_1\) has X
> \(N_2\) lacks X.

Where does that difference come from?

If the difference is something fundamental, then there are now two ultimate principles.

Classical theism argues that genuine ultimate simplicity leaves no room for such differentiating composition.

Therefore:

> **There cannot be two absolutely ultimate, simple necessary beings.**

So:

> Necessary + simple → unique.

This is part of Aquinas's later progression toward divine unity. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

---

# 17. Now something surprising happens

If the necessary reality is:

* uncaused,
* non-composite,
* immaterial,
* unique,
* independent,
* eternal,

we are already quite far from “the universe has fundamental laws.”

But we're **still not at God**.

We need intelligence.

And this is where things become much more interesting.

---

# 18. Step 6: Why would the necessary ground be intelligent?

There are several possible routes.

The most Leibnizian route begins with **possibility**.

Suppose many possible physical worlds are mathematically/metaphysically possible:

$$
W_1,W_2,W_3,\ldots
$$

But only one becomes actual.

If the necessary ground is responsible for the existence of one contingent world rather than another, then something has to account for the selection.

Leibniz says:

> The necessary ground must have **understanding**, because it comprehends the possibilities.

And it must have **will**, because it actualizes one possibility rather than another.

And it must have **power**, because it is capable of actualizing it.

This is essentially Leibniz's own progression: ultimate reason → understanding of possible worlds → will/choice → power to actualize. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][4])

This is a much stronger argument than:

> “The universe is orderly, therefore someone designed it.”

It is instead:

> **If the contingent world is grounded in a necessary reality, and multiple contingent possibilities exist, the grounding relation looks less like a blind mechanical cause and more like an act of selection.**

---

# 19. But the naturalist has a very good answer

They can say:

> “Why assume there are multiple genuinely possible worlds?”

Perhaps there is only one metaphysically possible fundamental structure.

Then there is no selection.

The necessary structure simply entails the universe.

This would make the ultimate reality:

> **necessary mathematics/structure**

rather than:

> **a divine intellect choosing a world.**

That's a powerful naturalist alternative.

So the theist needs to establish that:

> **The contingent physical world could genuinely have been otherwise.**

If they can't, the move to divine intelligence becomes much weaker.

---

# 20. There is another route to intelligence

Aquinas takes a different path.

The world contains things that are:

* ordered toward ends,
* structured to produce particular effects,
* governed by regularities.

Modern science explains those regularities mechanically, but Aquinas asks a deeper question:

> **Why does non-conscious matter systematically behave in ways that realize determinate ends?**

His famous example is essentially:

> An arrow reaches its target because an archer directs it.

The modern naturalist says:

> Evolution, selection, physical laws, self-organization, etc.

And that's a completely legitimate response at the scientific level.

But Aquinas is asking something more metaphysical:

> Why are there stable causal dispositions at all?

Why does:

$$
H_2O
$$

have these dispositions?

Why does gravity systematically produce certain behaviors?

Why do physical systems have determinate powers and tendencies?

His answer is ultimately:

> Their directedness is grounded in an intelligence.

This is the background of Aquinas's Fifth Way.

---

# 21. But this is where modern naturalism has probably improved the most

Darwin makes the teleological argument much harder.

We can explain enormous amounts of apparent biological purpose without invoking an intelligence.

So:

> “Living organisms look designed”

is **not** sufficient.

A serious Thomist doesn't need to deny evolution, however.

They can move the argument downward:

> Evolution explains biological adaptation, but it doesn't explain why there is a world possessing stable causal powers, laws, mathematical structures and evolutionary mechanisms in the first place.

That moves the argument from:

> biological design

to:

> metaphysical intelligibility.

Whether that move succeeds is controversial, but it's much stronger.

---

# 22. Step 7: Why intelligence rather than merely abstract mathematics?

This is probably the hardest point.

Imagine:

> Necessary mathematical structure \(M\)

and

> Necessary divine mind \(G\).

Why prefer \(G\)?

The theist can argue:

> Mathematical structures are abstract. They don't normally cause concrete things.

A mathematical truth such as:

$$
2+2=4
$$

doesn't physically produce four apples.

So if the necessary ground is supposed to **actualize** a physical universe, an abstract mathematical structure may not be enough.

You need something with **causal efficacy**.

And now the theist proposes:

> **A necessary mind/intellect with causal power.**

That's a real philosophical distinction:

### Platonism

Mathematical reality is necessary but abstract.

### Theism

The necessary ground is necessary **and causally productive**.

That is a significant advantage for the theistic model if you accept the premise that the physical world's existence requires an external ground.

---

# 23. But again, naturalism has an escape route

It can say:

> “Why assume mathematical objects are causally inert?”

Or:

> “Maybe the distinction between abstract structure and physical reality is misguided.”

Or simply:

> “The physical structure itself is fundamental.”

And we're back to the competing stopping points.

So again, the theist hasn't mathematically forced the conclusion.

They have developed a **metaphysical model** that potentially has greater explanatory unity.

---

# 24. Step 8: From intelligence to omniscience

Suppose the necessary ground is an intellect.

Why omniscient?

If it is the ultimate ground of all reality, then:

> Every truth about reality ultimately falls within the scope of what it grounds.

Classical theism therefore argues that the divine intellect knows all truths.

But again, this requires care.

You can't simply say:

> “God is the ultimate cause, therefore God knows everything.”

You need the intermediate metaphysical argument.

Aquinas's own account of divine knowledge is extraordinarily elaborate; he argues that God knows things by knowing his own essence as the source of all created reality. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

So:

> **Ultimate causal ground → intellect → comprehensive knowledge**

is a philosophical chain, not a single inference.

---

# 25. Step 9: From necessary reality to goodness

Classical theism then makes another move.

If God is the ultimate source of all actuality, then God is understood as **pure actuality** rather than a mixture of actuality and unrealized potentiality.

And from there Aquinas connects:

> actuality → perfection → goodness.

This gives the classical formula:

> **God is not merely one good thing. God is the ultimate source and standard of goodness.**

That's how the argument eventually reaches omnibenevolence.

Again, this is specifically **classical theism**, particularly Thomistic metaphysics.

It isn't contained in the bare cosmological argument.

---

# 26. So now look at what we've actually achieved

Starting from:

> **There is contingent reality.**

we have potentially built:

### 1. Contingent reality

↓

### 2. Necessary ground

↓

### 3. Non-dependent

↓

### 4. Uncaused

↓

### 5. Simple

↓

### 6. Immaterial

↓

### 7. Unique

↓

### 8. Necessary

↓

### 9. Potentially intellective

↓

### 10. Knowing

↓

### 11. Powerful

↓

### 12. Good

And now **“God” isn't being introduced arbitrarily at the beginning.**

We're using “God” as the name for the entity possessing the collection of properties reached through the metaphysical argument.

That is exactly the sort of project Aquinas undertakes after the Five Ways: he proceeds from the initial causal/necessary ground toward simplicity, perfection, goodness, infinity, immutability, eternity, unity and knowledge. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

---

# 27. But—and this is crucial—the chain has weak links

I don't want to oversell it.

The hardest transitions are probably:

### **Contingency → necessary ground**

Depends on some version of PSR/grounding.

### **Necessary ground → God**

Doesn't follow automatically.

### **Necessary ground → simplicity**

Requires a substantive metaphysics of dependence and composition.

### **Necessary ground → immateriality**

Requires an argument about matter and contingency.

### **Necessary ground → intelligence**

Probably the hardest transition.

### **Intelligence → omniscience/goodness**

Requires still more classical metaphysics.

So a person could accept:

> necessary ground

while rejecting:

> personal God.

That is a perfectly coherent position.

---

# 28. And this produces a very interesting spectrum

We're not dealing with:

**Atheism ←→ Christianity**

but something more like:

**Brute naturalism**

↓

**Necessary physical reality**

↓

**Necessary mathematical/structural reality**

↓

**Necessary metaphysical ground**

↓

**Necessary immaterial ground**

↓

**Necessary intelligent ground**

↓

**Classical theism**

↓

**A particular revealed religion**

Each arrow needs additional argument.

That's philosophically much cleaner.

---

# 29. And I think this answers your original concern about “bringing God from somewhere else”

You were worried that the procedure might be:

> I already believe in God for religious reasons → I notice that physics doesn't explain everything → I put God into the unexplained space.

That would indeed be philosophically weak.

But **classical natural theology is attempting something quite different**:

> Start with features of reality that are available independently of revelation → derive metaphysical consequences → identify the resulting ultimate reality with what classical traditions call God.

Whether the derivation succeeds is debatable.

But the *method* is not circular.

And historically, this is exactly what Aquinas, Leibniz and other natural theologians were trying to do. The contemporary philosophical literature still treats these as arguments from contingency, grounding, necessity, and explanation rather than as mere religious appeals. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

---

# 30. Where I think the real philosophical confrontation now lies

After all our discussion, I would reduce the entire dispute to **three enormous questions**.

### Question 1 — Is ultimate brute contingency acceptable?

If **yes**, naturalism gains a major advantage.

If **no**, theism or some other necessary-ground metaphysics becomes attractive.

### Question 2 — Can physical reality itself be necessary?

If **yes**, the cosmological argument may stop before God.

If **no**, the theist has a stronger case for something beyond physical reality.

### Question 3 — If there is a necessary ground, must it be a mind?

This is the hardest step toward God.

If **yes**, classical theism becomes much more plausible.

If **no**, you could have something like:

> necessary impersonal metaphysical structure.

And that would be a very interesting middle position.

---

## My provisional assessment

If I were trying to convince **you specifically**, I would **not** lead with the fine-tuning argument, mathematical beauty, religious experience, or “science can't explain everything.”

Your objections to those are too strong for them to carry the argument.

I'd build the strongest case in this order:

> **contingency → grounding → necessity → independence → simplicity → immateriality → unity → intellect → will → goodness**

And I'd concentrate especially on the first and last parts.

Because the whole argument ultimately stands or falls on two questions:

> **Why isn't brute contingent reality an acceptable ultimate explanation?**

and then:

> **Why should the necessary ground be a mind rather than an impersonal necessary structure?**

Those are the two places where I think the naturalist has the strongest position—and therefore the two places where a genuinely convincing theistic argument has to do its hardest work.

And there's a beautiful historical progression here: **Aristotle gives us the unmoved mover; Aquinas develops a metaphysics of act, potency and dependence; Leibniz shifts the emphasis toward sufficient reason and contingency; and contemporary analytic philosophy reformulates these ideas using grounding, modality and possible worlds.** ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

The next step I'd take is therefore **not** to pile on more arguments for God. I'd take **Question 1 and Question 3 and really attack them from both sides**:

> **(A)** Is “the universe is just a brute fact” actually a stable philosophical position, or does the very notion of a *contingent brute fact* contain a deep problem?

> **(B)** Suppose we grant the necessary ground. Can we genuinely get **mind/intellect/will** from metaphysics, or does the argument illegitimately jump from “necessary foundation” to “God”?

If we can get through those two, we'll know whether the route to God is genuinely substantive—or whether naturalism has successfully stopped the argument before it gets there.

[1]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/aquinas/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Thomas Aquinas (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/cosmological-argument/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Cosmological Argument (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[3]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/sufficient-reason/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Principle of Sufficient Reason (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[4]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/leibniz/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"


The fact that a principle is not stated as "i don't like unanswered explations" doesn't mean that this is not where the metaphysical alternative really comes from ultimately since it is human statement.

Why should we take science as assuming that all reality is intelligible rather than just trying to complete everything that is intelligible. Recognizing that somes thingsay not be be intelligible doesn't mean we should stop trying. We may have even a dense det of intelligible things inside a non intelligible reality (analogue of rationals inside of reals).

I have to say that at some points the naturalist questioning sounds a bit psychotic and certainly lazy. Not wrong, but I get the rejection more from a human perspective than a logical one, because it seems to make knowledge impossible or irrational, that's why I am also saying that it could be all reduced to human preferences. And I would say that being able to conceive universes with different fundamental laws/constants is a good argument for contingency even if not a definitive proof since we will never know what the actual fundsmental laws are.


Where does the possibility of multiple universes come into all of this? Because some of these arguments seem to rely on the existence of only one reality. How do these argument have to be modofied or what are the natural consequences of them? Also in leibniz argument I don't see why randomness would not be a possible explanation for the choice


Have Aquinas/leibniz etc arguments being developed further in more modern philosophy? Seems like you tool naturalism to more recent arguments but for theism you stayed at those. I also think even their arguments could be developed further such the argument of god knowing its own essence or why only goodness when badness also exist (and you have to define good and bad anyway)

When I say bringing evidence of god from elsewhere is not aboout circularity, is just that if we try to explain the universe you would need some evidence from somewhere even if it is not traditional science. I don't care about specific religions.

I am also happy for you to take those two suggested questions further.



Yes. I think your objections substantially sharpen the issue, and I want to revise two things from my previous answer.

First, **I was too quick to treat the naturalist's brute-fact position as philosophically neutral**. It is logically coherent, but that does not mean it is explanatorily or epistemically neutral.

Second, **I was too conservative about the modern development of these arguments**. There is a substantial contemporary literature that takes Aquinas/Leibniz-type ideas and reformulates them using modality, grounding, explanatory dependence, counterfactuals, simplicity, causal powers, etc. Contemporary philosophy has not simply left Aquinas and Leibniz in the Middle Ages. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

Let me work through your points and then do the two questions we identified.

---

# 1. You're right about the “human preference” issue

You made an important distinction:

> A philosopher can formulate “I don't like unexplained things” as a sophisticated metaphysical principle, but that doesn't mean the principle isn't ultimately motivated by a human preference for explanation.

Exactly.

And this is one of the deepest issues in the entire debate.

Suppose someone says:

> **PSR:** Every contingent fact has a sufficient reason.

We can ask:

**Why believe PSR?**

Possible answer:

> Because otherwise reality contains arbitrary facts.

But then:

**Why is arbitrary reality unacceptable?**

And eventually you may reach:

> “Because rational thought aims at intelligibility.”

And then:

> **Why should reality itself conform to the requirements of rational thought?**

That is the crucial question.

So I agree with your suspicion that **there is a possible psychological genealogy of metaphysical principles**:

$$
\text{humans dislike unexplained things}
$$

→

$$
\text{we develop a norm of seeking explanations}
$$

→

$$
\text{we generalize that norm to reality}
$$

→

$$
\text{we formulate PSR}.
$$

But here's the interesting counterargument:

The fact that a principle has a psychological origin **doesn't establish that it is merely psychological**.

Arithmetic also has a psychological history. Logic has a psychological history. Our concept of causation has a psychological history.

The relevant question isn't:

> “Did humans invent the principle?”

but:

> **“Is there something about the principle that makes it objectively true, rather than merely useful or psychologically compelling?”**

That's the same distinction you were making earlier about mathematical intuition.

And here, I think the theist has to do considerably more than say:

> “PSR feels right.”

---

# 2. And I agree with your correction about science

I previously gave too much weight to the argument:

> Science presupposes universal intelligibility.

That is too strong.

Your formulation is much better:

> **Science investigates whatever parts of reality are intelligible to scientific investigation.**

There is no contradiction whatsoever in:

$$
\text{some intelligible domain} \subset \text{a larger unintelligible reality}.
$$

Your analogy with

$$
\mathbb Q \subset \mathbb R
$$

is actually very useful.

The fact that there are enormous amounts of rationally tractable structure inside the reals doesn't imply that every real number has some simple rational representation. Likewise:

> The fact that science has discovered vast regularities doesn't establish that **reality as a whole is exhaustively intelligible**.

And this is important for our earlier discussion of science.

Scientific methodology gives us excellent evidence for:

> “Many things are intelligible.”

It does **not** by itself give us:

> “Everything is intelligible.”

So the move:

> “Science works → therefore PSR is true”

is much too quick.

There are contemporary defenders of the connection between explanatory principles and scientific practice—for example, Alexander Pruss argues that rejecting such principles creates tension with scientific inquiry—but critics respond precisely that this establishes at most a methodological principle, not necessarily an ontological one. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

I think your formulation is stronger:

> **Science gives us evidence that reality contains a large, extraordinarily rich intelligible structure. It doesn't tell us whether reality is intelligible all the way down.**

That distinction should stay.

---

# 3. Your “psychotic naturalist” reaction is actually pointing at something philosophically interesting

I understand what you mean.

The naturalist says:

> “Maybe this whole thing just exists for no reason.”

And you're thinking:

> “Fine, that's logically possible, but you're making an enormous concession. You're basically saying that at the deepest level there is no reason to believe reality is rationally structured.”

And that can feel like it undermines the very rational enterprise by which you arrived at the position.

But we need to distinguish two claims:

### Claim A

> “There are some brute facts.”

This seems harmless.

### Claim B

> “At the ultimate level, reality may be entirely brute.”

That's much more radical.

Because if the fundamental structure is brute, then the fact that it has:

* mathematical regularity,
* causal structure,
* logical coherence,
* stable laws,
* discoverable patterns,

is itself ultimately unexplained.

That doesn't make science impossible.

It means:

> **Science can map the structure without explaining why there is that structure.**

That's perfectly coherent.

So I wouldn't call naturalism irrational.

But I agree with you that **it is not obviously philosophically cost-free**.

And this is exactly where contemporary defenders of cosmological arguments press the issue: they argue that saying “the universe is a brute fact” isn't merely stopping an investigation; it is adopting a substantive view about what counts as an adequate ultimate explanation. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

---

# 4. I also agree that conceivable alternative laws are evidence of contingency

I would make your position slightly more precise.

You said:

> If we can conceive of universes with different fundamental laws/constants, that is a good argument for contingency, although not definitive.

Yes.

I would formulate it:

> **Modal conceivability is defeasible evidence for metaphysical possibility, but it isn't proof of metaphysical possibility.**

For example, we can imagine:

$$
G = 5
$$

instead of the actual gravitational constant.

That strongly suggests:

> “There is nothing conceptually contradictory about a world with different gravitational behavior.”

But it doesn't establish:

> “Such a world is metaphysically possible.”

There might be deeper constraints we don't know.

Nevertheless, **if a naturalist claims the fundamental physical structure is necessary**, the burden becomes interesting.

They need some account of why:

$$
\neg P
$$

is impossible when \(P\) is some fundamental physical fact.

Simply saying:

> “It's fundamental”

doesn't establish necessity.

It establishes **epistemic termination**:

> “We've reached the bottom.”

It doesn't establish **metaphysical necessity**:

> “The bottom could not have been otherwise.”

That distinction is extremely important.

---

# 5. Now let's bring the multiverse into the picture

This is a very good question because there are actually **several different things people call “multiverse.”**

We should distinguish them.

## A. Many physical universes

There are multiple causally disconnected regions with different initial conditions or perhaps different effective constants.

Call them:

$$
U_1,U_2,U_3,\ldots
$$

Then the question becomes:

> Why does the multiverse exist?

You haven't automatically solved the cosmological problem.

You've moved from:

$$
\text{Why does } U_1 \text{ exist?}
$$

to:

$$
\text{Why does } \{U_1,U_2,U_3,\ldots\} \text{ exist?}
$$

The totality can still be contingent.

And this is exactly why contemporary cosmological arguments don't simply collapse when a multiverse is introduced. The relevant object for the argument can become the **totality of contingent realities**, rather than our local universe. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

---

# 6. But the multiverse can nevertheless help naturalism

Absolutely.

Suppose the theory says:

> There is a necessary generative mechanism that produces infinitely many universes.

Then perhaps:

$$
\text{necessary multiverse-generating structure}
\rightarrow
\text{many universes}
$$

and our universe is just one realization.

This can potentially explain why we observe apparently fine-tuned parameters without requiring a designer.

But notice something interesting:

**The multiverse hasn't necessarily eliminated metaphysics.**

You still have to ask:

> Why does that generative structure exist?

And:

> Why does it have those laws?

And:

> Why does it generate this space of possibilities?

And perhaps:

> Why is that possibility-space mathematically structured in the way it is?

So the multiverse can push the explanatory question upward.

That doesn't mean it's illegitimate.

It means:

> **It is not automatically an ultimate explanation.**

---

# 7. Now something even more interesting: the “all possible worlds” idea

Leibniz himself gives us a much more radical possibility.

Suppose **every genuinely possible world exists** in some sense.

Then the question:

> “Why this universe rather than another?”

becomes less compelling.

Because every possible universe exists.

Our universe is simply one of them.

But this creates a huge ontological price.

You have now moved from:

> one physical universe

to:

> an enormous—or perhaps maximal—realm of possible worlds.

This is related to contemporary modal realism, although Leibniz's metaphysics is not identical to David Lewis's modern modal realism.

And it raises exactly the question you're asking:

> **Does multiplying realities actually explain anything, or merely relocate the brute fact?**

That's one of the deepest issues here.

---

# 8. And now your objection to Leibniz is exactly right: why not randomness?

Suppose there are possible worlds:

$$
W_1,W_2,W_3,\ldots
$$

and one becomes actual.

Leibniz says:

> There must be a sufficient reason why \(W_i\) is actual rather than \(W_j\).

But why couldn't the answer be:

> **Random selection?**

This is a serious objection.

Leibniz's response would be that “random” doesn't constitute an ultimate explanation.

Suppose:

$$
P(W_i)=0.2.
$$

You can explain the outcome statistically:

> “The probability distribution assigned a 20% probability to \(W_i\), and \(W_i\) happened.”

But then ask:

> Why that probability distribution?

If that's also brute, randomness hasn't eliminated the ultimate unexplained fact.

So randomness can explain **why an outcome occurred given a probability structure**, but it doesn't necessarily explain **why that probability structure exists**.

This is a crucial distinction:

$$
\text{probabilistic explanation}
\neq
\text{ultimate explanation}.
$$

The naturalist can respond:

> “I don't require ultimate explanation.”

And we're back at the central dispute.

So randomness doesn't refute Leibniz's argument; it **relocates the point at which you reject PSR**.

---

# 9. This actually produces three increasingly radical naturalist stopping points

### Naturalism 1 — Brute universe

> This universe simply exists.

### Naturalism 2 — Brute multiverse

> This multiverse simply exists.

### Naturalism 3 — Necessary possibility structure

> The space of possible realities exists necessarily, and our universe is one realization.

The third is much more sophisticated.

But notice how interesting it becomes.

It starts looking surprisingly close to metaphysics traditionally associated with theism:

> necessary reality
> possibility
> actuality
> explanation
> modal structure.

The disagreement becomes:

> **Is the necessary structure fundamentally mathematical/impersonal, or fundamentally intellectual/personal?**

And *that* is a much deeper question than “God vs science.”

---

# 10. Now let's update the modern philosophical landscape

You were absolutely right to call me out here.

There is a substantial modern development.

A particularly important transition is:

### Medieval language

> cause
> substance
> act/potency
> essence/existence

↓

### Leibniz

> sufficient reason
> necessity/contingency
> possible worlds

↓

### Contemporary metaphysics

> grounding
> metaphysical dependence
> modal necessity
> explanatory relations
> counterfactual dependence
> fundamentality.

The contemporary literature on **grounding** is particularly important here. Grounding asks questions such as:

> In virtue of what does this fact obtain?

rather than merely:

> What caused this event?

That distinction is extremely close to the concerns of Aristotle and Aquinas, but expressed in modern metaphysical vocabulary. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][2])

So modern analytic philosophy has essentially reopened some of the ancient questions using a new conceptual toolkit.

---

# 11. There are contemporary arguments that are much closer to what you're asking

For example, **Alexander Pruss** develops contemporary versions of cosmological arguments around PSR and contingency.

**Robert Koons** develops arguments based on causal/explanatory principles.

**Timothy O'Connor** develops arguments concerning necessary being and purposive agency.

**Joshua Rasmussen** has developed contemporary arguments for necessary existence and the nature of fundamental reality.

And importantly, there are sophisticated critics such as **Graham Oppy**, who takes these arguments seriously while arguing that naturalistic alternatives can be at least as good.

The contemporary debate therefore isn't:

> Aquinas said X 800 years ago.

It's:

> **Pruss/Rasmussen/Koons/O'Connor vs Oppy and other critics, using contemporary modal and metaphysical machinery.**

The Stanford Encyclopedia's contemporary survey explicitly discusses O'Connor's argument that the necessary being is better understood as a purposive agent, Koons's attempts to derive divine attributes, and Oppy's objections to those derivations. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

That's much closer to the debate you want.

---

# 12. Now let's return to Question 1 properly

## Is brute contingency actually acceptable?

I think we can now formulate the issue much more sharply.

The naturalist has this position:

> **There is no logical contradiction in ultimate brute facts.**

I agree.

But the theist asks:

> **Is “there is no contradiction” enough to establish that the position is metaphysically satisfactory?**

No.

And here's why.

Suppose the ultimate fact is:

$$
F = \text{“A physical reality with structure S exists.”}
$$

There are two possibilities:

### Option A

\(F\) is necessary.

Then:

$$
\Box F
$$

### Option B

\(F\) is contingent.

Then:

$$
\Diamond \neg F
$$

If B is true, the theist asks:

> Why \(F\) rather than \(\neg F\)?

If there is no answer:

> brute contingency.

The theist says:

> “That's an unexplained modal asymmetry.”

And that is a much more precise criticism than:

> “I don't like unexplained things.”

---

# 13. But here's the strongest naturalist response

And I think we should take it very seriously.

The naturalist can say:

> **There is no such thing as an unexplained modal asymmetry requiring justification.**

Perhaps:

$$
F
$$

is simply true.

The fact that:

$$
\neg F
$$

was possible doesn't generate an obligation to explain why \(F\) obtained.

That's a coherent metaphysical position.

And this is where I think the debate becomes genuinely philosophical.

There is no experiment that can settle it.

The issue is about what **fundamental explanation** means.

---

# 14. But here's where I think the theist gets a genuinely strong argument

The theist can say:

> “Fine. But you're using ‘fundamental’ in two different senses.”

You're saying:

> “This is fundamental because there is nothing deeper.”

But that doesn't tell us:

> **Why this is the kind of thing that can occupy the fundamental position.**

Compare:

> “This axiom is primitive.”

with:

> “This axiom is necessarily true.”

Those aren't the same.

Likewise:

> “The laws of physics are fundamental”

doesn't entail:

> “The laws of physics are necessary.”

So the naturalist who says:

> “Reality bottoms out in physics”

has not yet answered the modal question.

They have merely specified where they stop.

That is, in my view, one of the strongest points available to the theist.

---

# 15. But now we hit the really hard Question 3

Suppose the theist wins:

> **There is a necessary ground.**

Why should it be a **mind**?

This is where I think the argument needs to become much more sophisticated than Aquinas's Fifth Way.

I would divide the possible routes into three.

---

## Route A — From contingency to choice

If multiple possible worlds are genuinely possible:

$$
W_1,W_2,W_3,\ldots
$$

and the necessary ground produces one:

$$
N \rightarrow W_i
$$

then why \(W_i\)?

If \(N\)'s nature doesn't necessitate \(W_i\), something must account for the selection.

The Leibnizian answer:

> intellect apprehends possibilities; will selects one.

That's a route to **mind**.

But as you correctly pointed out:

> Why can't selection be random?

The answer is:

> Randomness can explain an outcome conditionally, but not necessarily why the probability structure or possibility space exists.

Still, this doesn't prove mind.

---

# 16. Route B — The necessary ground must contain reasons

This is more interesting.

Suppose the ground explains:

$$
W_i
$$

rather than merely causing it.

Then the explanation might take the form:

> “\(W_i\) obtains because it instantiates property P.”

But if P is itself something abstract, you haven't necessarily explained why it became actual.

A mind can provide a fundamentally different explanatory relation:

> **representation → intention → actualization.**

This is why O'Connor's contemporary version is interesting: he argues that a necessary being is more plausibly understood as a purposive agent with desires, intentions and beliefs, rather than simply an impersonal necessary mechanism. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

That's a genuine modern development of the old argument.

---

# 17. Route C — The necessary ground must be a source of actuality, not merely possibility

This is where Thomistic metaphysics becomes relevant again.

Mathematical objects can be necessary.

For example:

$$
2+2=4
$$

may be necessary.

But:

> \(2+2=4\)

doesn't cause four apples.

So we distinguish:

### Necessary truth

from

### Necessary causal reality.

If the ultimate ground must actually **produce or sustain concrete existence**, then a merely abstract mathematical structure may not be enough.

You need something that can actualize.

And classical theism says:

> **Pure actuality.**

This is one reason the Thomistic God is not a mathematical object.

---

# 18. But here's a fascinating naturalist alternative

The naturalist could say:

> **Perhaps the distinction between mathematical structure and physical existence is itself artificial.**

Maybe reality simply *is* a mathematical structure.

Then there is no transition:

$$
\text{mathematics} \rightarrow \text{physical reality}.
$$

There is simply:

$$
\text{mathematical structure} = \text{reality}.
$$

This is roughly the kind of possibility associated with mathematical structural realism and, in a much stronger form, Tegmark's Mathematical Universe Hypothesis.

And now we're back to your original intuition:

> Perhaps the universe simply is mathematical.

This is a **much stronger naturalist alternative** than the crude “physics just happens” position.

And notice what it does:

It eliminates the need for a divine chooser because:

> there was never a choice.

The necessary structure simply exists.

---

# 19. And that gives us the deepest version of the disagreement

At this point we have:

### Theistic metaphysics

$$
\text{Necessary reality}
\rightarrow
\text{intellect}
\rightarrow
\text{possibilities}
\rightarrow
\text{will}
\rightarrow
\text{actual world}.
$$

versus:

### Structural naturalism

$$
\text{Necessary mathematical structure}
=
\text{reality}.
$$

And now I think your original mathematical intuition becomes relevant again.

You were previously saying:

> “Why introduce God if the mathematical structure can simply be what reality is?”

**Exactly.**

That's probably the strongest naturalist position we have encountered.

The theist now needs to explain why:

> **necessary mathematical structure**

is metaphysically insufficient.

And that's much harder than simply saying:

> “But why does mathematics exist?”

Because the naturalist can answer:

> “There is no additional mathematical existence over and above the structure itself.”

---

# 20. Now your question about good and evil becomes crucial

You correctly noticed that saying:

> “God is goodness”

doesn't magically solve the problem.

Because:

> **What is goodness?**

And:

> **Why does badness exist?**

Classical theism has a very sophisticated answer that is much stranger than “God is nice.”

For Aquinas, evil isn't a positive substance competing with goodness.

Rather:

> **Evil is a privation of a due good.**

Blindness is evil *for a creature that ought to see*.

Disease is evil *because the organism's proper functioning is impaired*.

Injustice is evil because a due order between persons is violated.

So evil isn't a second metaphysical principle:

$$
Good \quad vs \quad Evil.
$$

Instead:

$$
Good = actuality/perfection
$$

and

$$
Evil = privation/defect\ of\ due\ actuality.
$$

This lets classical theism maintain:

> God is the ultimate source of being and goodness

without making God the creator of an independent “evil substance.”

But this generates enormous further problems—especially **why a good God permits horrendous suffering**.

And contemporary philosophy has developed theodicies and skeptical-theist responses far beyond Aquinas.

So again, you're right: simply saying “God is goodness” is nowhere near enough.

---

# 21. And this connects to your point about evidence

I think I now understand your earlier objection more precisely.

You weren't saying:

> “The theist is circular.”

You were saying:

> **If we're going to posit an entity beyond physics, what is our evidence for positing it?**

That's exactly right.

And here's the important answer:

### There is no requirement that all evidence be scientific evidence.

But there **is** a requirement that the evidence be epistemically respectable.

For example:

* philosophical arguments,
* modal intuitions,
* metaphysical arguments,
* logical consequences,
* inference to the best explanation,
* consciousness,
* moral experience,
* religious experience,
* historical testimony,

could potentially all count.

The question becomes:

> **Does the total evidence make theism more probable than its alternatives?**

That's the epistemological question.

And I think that's ultimately a better formulation than:

> “Can science prove God?”

Science may be completely silent about God while philosophy still has something to say.

---

# 22. So let's now make the strongest possible naturalist case

I think it should be:

> **Reality contains a fundamental structure.**
>
> We don't know whether that structure is contingent or necessary.
>
> If contingent, there is no logical contradiction in it being a brute fact.
>
> If necessary, no further explanation is needed.
>
> Mathematical and physical structures may be fundamentally identical rather than requiring an external creator.
>
> Science demonstrates that enormous portions of reality are intelligible, but does not establish universal PSR.
>
> Therefore we have no sufficient reason to introduce a transcendent mind.
>
> Adding God creates additional metaphysical commitments that aren't independently required.

That's a **very strong position**.

And notice that I no longer think it's fair to characterize it merely as:

> “Humans don't like explanations.”

It's a principled position:

> **Don't posit an entity unless the explanatory gain exceeds the ontological cost.**

That's essentially a version of theoretical parsimony.

---

# 23. Now the strongest theistic reply

And I think it is this:

> **You're treating the ultimate physical/mathematical structure as exempt from the very explanatory principles you use everywhere else, without demonstrating that it is metaphysically necessary.**

You know that:

$$
\text{physical structure S}
$$

could apparently have been different.

If so:

$$
\Diamond \neg S.
$$

Then:

> Why S?

If you answer:

> “No reason.”

you have accepted brute contingency.

The theist says:

> “That is possible, but metaphysically costly.”

Then:

> “If there is a necessary ground capable of explaining why a contingent reality exists, that is a better stopping point.”

Then comes the second half:

> “If the necessary ground must actualize one of several possibilities, an intellect/will provides a better account of this than an impersonal structure.”

And finally:

> “A necessary, simple, immaterial, intelligent, purposive, causally efficacious ground is very close to what classical philosophy means by God.”

That's, in my view, the strongest cumulative case.

---

# 24. But I would *not* say we've defeated naturalism yet

Because the naturalist can still say:

> **Perhaps there is no ultimate explanation.**

or:

> **Perhaps physical reality is necessary.**

or:

> **Perhaps mathematical structure is identical to physical reality.**

or:

> **Perhaps necessity itself generates the actual structure without any choice.**

And those aren't stupid objections.

The interesting thing is that we're now getting down to **very deep metaphysics**, rather than religion versus science.

---

# 25. Where I think we should go next

I would propose we now take the argument one level deeper and actually **formalize the two competing metaphysical pictures**.

### Naturalist picture

$$
\boxed{
