\text{Necessary/Brute Fundamental Structure}
\rightarrow
\text{Physical Reality}
}
$$

### Theist picture

$$
\boxed{
\text{Necessary Simple Ground}
\rightarrow
\text{Intellect}
\rightarrow
\text{Possibilities}
\rightarrow
\text{Will}
\rightarrow
\text{Contingent Reality}
}
$$

And then ask, one by one:

**1. What exactly does “necessary” mean?**
Logical necessity? Metaphysical necessity? Nomological necessity?

**2. Can physical reality really be metaphysically necessary?**

**3. Can a mathematical structure cause anything?**

**4. Does a necessary ground need to explain why it produces *this* world?**

**5. Does explanation of a contingent world require something like a mind?**

**6. Is randomness genuinely an alternative to intention, or does it merely move the brute fact?**

**7. Does divine simplicity actually solve the “who explains God?” problem, or does it just redefine God as the stopping point?**

**8. And finally: if we reach a necessary, simple, immaterial, intelligent ground, do we really have sufficient grounds to call that “God,” or have we merely discovered an unfamiliar metaphysical entity?**

That last question is particularly important. **“Necessary being” → “God” is not one inference. It's an entire research program.** Contemporary philosophers explicitly disagree about whether the divine attributes can actually be derived from cosmological arguments; O'Connor and Koons attempt such derivations, while Oppy is skeptical of them. ([Enciclopedia Stanford de Filosofía][1])

And I think we should tackle it that way: **not as apologetics, but almost as if we're constructing competing theories of ultimate reality and comparing their explanatory power, ontological commitments, and internal coherence.**

That would also let us finally address something you raised at the beginning of this whole conversation: whether the mathematical intelligibility of reality is actually evidence for something beyond the physical—or whether, once properly analyzed, it simply becomes another feature of the fundamental structure.

[1]: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/spr2021/entries/cosmological-argument/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Cosmological Argument (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy/Spring 2021 Edition)"
[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/grounding/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Metaphysical Grounding (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"



Does A not imply B under most logical settings? Namely if there are some brute facts, those would naturally be an ultimate level of reality, can there be brute facts and a fundamental reality that is not a brute fact at the same time?

With multiple universes you could still ask why we're in this one and other questions along those line, but not sure if those are equally fundamental

Sometimes you say metaphysically possible/satisfactory/necessary/sufficient. What those really mean?

I'm interested in O'connors version. You barely skimmed it. Similarly with other modern theists, you gave a review of current authors without explaining the arguments. And do any of them or previous one have an answer to why necessary mathematical structure is metaphysically insufficient? As well as for the problems of good as source of goodness

Go further in 21, what are the epistemologically serious sources of theism?

Would be great if you continuing answering the questions that you suggested as well



Yes. And I think your first question exposes a distinction I blurred before. Let me start there, because it affects almost everything else.

## 1. Do brute facts necessarily constitute fundamental reality?

**Not quite, but very nearly—and we need to distinguish two senses of “fundamental.”**

Suppose there is a brute fact \(B\):

> \(B\) is true, and there is no explanation of why \(B\) is true.

If there were something more fundamental \(F\) that *explained* \(B\), then \(B\) wouldn't be brute **with respect to reality**. It might be unexplained *by us*, but it wouldn't be metaphysically brute.

So if by **brute fact** we mean:

> a fact that has no metaphysical explanation whatsoever,

then yes:

$$
\boxed{\text{brute} \Rightarrow \text{fundamental}}
$$

in the explanatory sense.

But the converse doesn't hold:

$$
\boxed{\text{fundamental} \not\Rightarrow \text{brute}}
$$

because something could be fundamental while being necessary and therefore not requiring an explanation of the ordinary contingent sort.

For example, a theist might say:

> God is fundamental, but God is not a brute fact because God's existence is necessary.

So we have three possibilities:

|                            | Explained?                         | Necessary? |
| -------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | ---------- |
| Ordinary contingent fact   | Yes                                | No         |
| Fundamental necessary fact | No *external* explanation required | Yes        |
| Fundamental brute fact     | No                                 | No         |

That third category is what the theist objects to.

And there's an important subtlety: **“necessary” itself does not necessarily mean “has an explanation.”** A necessary truth such as \(2+2=4\) isn't necessarily “explained” by something deeper. Rather, its non-truth is impossible. The theist's claim is that *necessity itself* is a superior metaphysical terminus to contingent brute fact.

That is much more precise than “God explains everything.”

---

# 2. And this makes your criticism of the naturalist position sharper

The naturalist can say:

> “There is a fundamental physical reality, and it is brute.”

That's perfectly coherent.

But they can alternatively say:

> “There is a fundamental physical reality, and it is necessary.”

Those are **very different positions**.

The first says:

$$
\text{It happens to be this way.}
$$

The second says:

$$
\text{It could not have been otherwise.}
$$

And this is why I think your earlier argument from conceivability is relevant.

If we can coherently imagine:

* different constants,
* different laws,
* different dimensionality,
* different particle structures,
* perhaps no physical universe at all,

then we have at least **prima facie evidence of contingency**.

Not proof, as you correctly said. But it puts pressure on someone claiming:

> “The physical structure is necessary.”

They need an account of *why* those alternatives aren't metaphysically possible.

That is a real philosophical burden.

---

# 3. Let's clean up the vocabulary

You asked something very important: what exactly do we mean by **metaphysically possible, necessary, sufficient, satisfactory**?

These terms are often abused.

### Logical possibility

Something is logically possible if it doesn't contradict the relevant logical rules.

For example:

> “There are blue swans.”

is logically possible.

Whereas:

> “There is a married bachelor.”

is logically impossible.

---

### Metaphysical possibility

This is stronger.

It means roughly:

> **Could reality genuinely have been that way?**

For example, after Kripke, philosophers distinguish logical conceivability from metaphysical possibility. “Water is not H₂O” may be conceivable in some epistemic sense, but if water is essentially H₂O, it isn't metaphysically possible for water to have a different chemical composition.

So:

$$
\text{metaphysically possible}
$$

is intended to capture the genuine space of ways reality could be.

But—and this matters enormously—**we don't have an infallible window into metaphysical possibility**.

That's why arguments such as:

> “I can conceive of another universe”

are evidence, not demonstrations.

---

### Metaphysical necessity

$$
\Box P
$$

means:

> \(P\) is true in every genuinely possible way reality could be.

So:

$$
\Box P
$$

is stronger than:

$$
P.
$$

A necessary being is therefore one that **could not fail to exist**.

But O'Connor makes a very interesting distinction: he doesn't equate the necessity of God with ordinary logical necessity. His “absolute necessity” is supposed to arise from the metaphysical explanatory structure of reality. The Stanford Encyclopedia summarizes his position as a being that is “absolutely invulnerable to nonexistence,” while emphasizing that O'Connor thinks its necessity is not simply established by logical consistency. ([Plato][1])

---

### Sufficient

This is easier.

A condition is **sufficient** for \(P\) if its obtaining guarantees \(P\).

$$
A \rightarrow B
$$

So \(A\) is sufficient for \(B\).

But it doesn't necessarily mean that \(A\) is necessary for \(B\).

For example:

> Being a mammal is sufficient for being an animal.

but not necessary.

---

### Metaphysically satisfactory

This one is **not a technical term**.

And I should have avoided using it without qualification.

It usually means something like:

> “A philosophical account that we regard as sufficiently explanatory, coherent, non-arbitrary, parsimonious, etc.”

But those criteria themselves are disputable.

So when we argue:

> “Brute contingency is metaphysically unsatisfactory,”

we should really unpack that into something like:

> “Brute contingency fails to provide an explanation of a contingent fact, and I regard explanation of contingent facts as a genuine epistemic/metaphysical requirement.”

That makes the premise visible instead of hiding it behind “satisfactory.”

---

# 4. Now: O'Connor properly

This is where I think my previous answer really was inadequate.

O'Connor is interesting precisely because he tries to solve the problem **after** the standard Leibnizian argument has been sharpened with contemporary modal metaphysics.

His project is in *Theism and Ultimate Explanation: The Necessary Shape of Contingency* (2008), and he subsequently develops the identification stage in “And This All Men Call God.” ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][2])

His argument has roughly two stages:

$$
\boxed{\text{Existence stage}}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{\text{Identification stage}}
$$

The first asks:

> Why is there contingent reality?

The second asks:

> What sort of thing could be its ultimate ground?

And **O'Connor explicitly thinks the second question is often neglected**. ([Tim O'Connor][3])

---

# 5. O'Connor's first important move: modality is already indispensable

O'Connor's starting point is something like:

We already rely on modal notions constantly.

We distinguish:

> what actually happens

from:

> what could happen

from:

> what couldn't happen.

Physics itself constantly makes modal claims:

> Given these laws and initial conditions, this outcome could occur.

or:

> This process cannot occur under these constraints.

So O'Connor argues that we cannot simply eliminate metaphysical modality from serious explanation.

His project therefore isn't:

> “Let's arbitrarily introduce God.”

It's:

> **Let's take seriously the modal structure already implicit in our explanatory practices and ask what ultimately grounds contingent reality.**

His book explicitly develops a “modalized, broadly neo-Aristotelian metaphysics” and argues that this changes how the cosmological question should be approached. ([PhilPapers][4])

---

# 6. The core O'Connor argument

Very roughly:

### Premise 1

There are contingent beings/events.

### Premise 2

Contingent reality requires an ultimate explanatory ground.

### Premise 3

An infinite regress of contingent explanatory dependencies doesn't provide an ultimate ground.

### Premise 4

The ultimate ground therefore cannot itself be contingent.

### Conclusion

There exists an **absolutely necessary being**.

But notice how O'Connor differs from a simplistic cosmological argument.

He's not primarily saying:

> “Everything has a cause.”

He's asking:

> **What kind of reality could terminate the explanatory dependence of contingent reality?**

That's much closer to the question you've been pushing toward.

O'Connor explicitly characterizes the target as a transcendent necessary being that is the source and basis of the ultimate explanation of contingent beings and their interconnected histories. ([OUP Academic][5])

---

# 7. But then O'Connor asks the question you have been asking me

Suppose we have established:

$$
N = \text{necessary being}.
$$

Why should \(N\) be God?

This is where O'Connor gets interesting.

He argues that the necessary being cannot simply be:

> the universe as a giant physical object.

Why?

Because the universe contains **internal differentiation and contingency**.

Likewise, the collection of elementary particles doesn't work because:

> their particular distributions and relations appear contingent.

The SEP summarizes O'Connor's argument as saying that a necessary being cannot be a derivative emergent property; nor can the universe, as a mereological complexity of contingent beings, simply be identified with the necessary being. ([Plato][1])

So O'Connor pushes toward something that is:

* necessary,
* non-derivative,
* transcendent,
* and capable of producing contingent reality.

And then comes his distinctive move:

> **The best candidate is a purposive agent.**

---

# 8. Why an agent?

Here's the core intuition.

Suppose the necessary reality \(N\) is sufficient to ground contingent reality.

But there are many possible contingent arrangements:

$$
W_1,W_2,W_3,\ldots
$$

If \(N\)'s nature **necessitated** \(W_1\), then:

$$
N \rightarrow W_1
$$

necessarily.

But then \(W_1\) wouldn't really be contingent.

So if:

> \(N\) is necessary

and

> \(W\) is contingent,

then there cannot simply be a deterministic entailment:

$$
N \models W.
$$

Something has to account for why **this contingent outcome** occurs.

O'Connor's answer is **agent causation**.

A person can have:

* beliefs,
* desires,
* reasons,
* intentions,

and then act.

The reason doesn't mechanically necessitate the action.

This allows:

$$
N \quad \text{necessary}
$$

while:

$$
W \quad \text{contingent}.
$$

The necessary being **freely wills** a contingent effect.

That is the really interesting part of O'Connor's argument.

The contemporary SEP describes his proposed necessary being precisely as a purposive agent possessing desires, intentions and beliefs whose activity is guided, but not determined, by goals. ([Plato][1])

---

# 9. And now your randomness objection becomes extremely important

You said:

> Why can't the universe just be randomly selected?

This is exactly where the debate becomes interesting.

Suppose the necessary structure \(N\) generates a random outcome:

$$
N \rightarrow \{W_1,W_2,W_3,\ldots\}
$$

with probabilities:

$$
P(W_1),P(W_2),\ldots
$$

Then the actual universe is contingent because the stochastic process doesn't determine which outcome occurs.

Could that work?

**Yes, as far as logical consistency goes.**

O'Connor therefore cannot simply say:

> “There is contingency, therefore there is agency.”

He needs a further argument:

> **Why is agent causation a better explanation of contingent actualization than indeterministic natural causation?**

And this is precisely one of the places where the naturalist can resist.

Oppy's critique is very explicit: he argues that naturalistic alternatives including brute contingency, infinite regress, and immanent necessity can be theoretically preferable to O'Connor's transcendent necessary being. ([OUP Academic][5])

So **O'Connor doesn't prove agency merely from contingency**.

His case is more like:

> Once we require an ultimate explanation *and* require the ultimate ground to preserve genuine contingency, purposive agency provides a particularly elegant model.

That's an abductive argument.

---

# 10. And this is where I think O'Connor is philosophically stronger than the simplistic Leibniz argument

Leibniz says:

> There must be a sufficient reason.

O'Connor asks:

> **What metaphysical architecture allows a necessary reality to be the ground of a contingent reality without making the contingent reality necessary?**

And his answer is:

> **personal causation / free agency.**

That's a genuinely interesting problem.

It is not simply:

> “We don't know why the universe exists, therefore God.”

---

# 11. But what about necessary mathematical structure?

This is your other major question.

Suppose the naturalist says:

> Reality is fundamentally mathematical.

Why isn't that enough?

There are several possible theistic responses, but they aren't equally strong.

## Response 1 — Abstract objects don't cause concrete objects

If mathematical structure is genuinely abstract, then:

$$
2+2=4
$$

doesn't cause four physical objects.

So the theist says:

> **A mathematical structure can describe or characterize a possible reality without explaining why that structure is concretely instantiated.**

That's an important distinction:

$$
\text{truth of structure}
\neq
\text{instantiation of structure}.
$$

But the naturalist can reject the distinction by saying:

> “There isn't a further instantiation relation. Physical reality *is* the structure.”

Then the objection fails.

---

# 12. Response 2 — mathematical structure doesn't explain its own modal space

Suppose reality is structure \(S\).

Why \(S\)?

The structural naturalist might answer:

> \(S\) is necessary.

Fine.

But now we need to ask:

> Why is \(S\) necessary?

If the answer is:

> “Because it is the fundamental mathematical structure,”

the theist can complain that we've simply renamed the brute fact.

This is where the theist's strongest challenge lies.

But notice something subtle:

**The naturalist doesn't necessarily have to explain why a necessary thing is necessary.**

If:

$$
\Box S
$$

then there isn't necessarily a further “why” behind \(\Box S\).

This is exactly analogous to the theist saying:

$$
\Box G.
$$

So the question becomes:

> **Why is \(S\) a better candidate for necessity than \(G\)?**

And *that* is a fair question.

---

# 13. The naturalist has an excellent parsimony argument here

Suppose:

### Naturalism

$$
\boxed{\text{necessary mathematical/physical structure}}
$$

### Theism

$$
\boxed{\text{necessary mind}}
\rightarrow
\text{intellect}
\rightarrow
\text{possibilities}
\rightarrow
\text{will}
\rightarrow
\text{physical reality}
$$

The naturalist says:

> Why introduce intellect, will, consciousness and agency when mathematical structure already gives you the physical structure?

That's a serious argument.

The theist therefore needs to show that **agency does explanatory work that mathematical necessity cannot do**.

O'Connor's response is essentially:

> mathematical/structural necessity cannot explain genuine contingency in the world, whereas a free agent can.

That is the point where his argument becomes genuinely distinctive.

---

# 14. There is also a deeper problem for mathematical Platonism

If mathematical objects exist necessarily and independently of minds, we get:

> Why do those abstract structures have causal relevance to the physical world?

You now have something like:

$$
\text{abstract necessary structure}
$$

and

$$
\text{concrete physical reality}.
$$

What connects them?

This is the old **Benacerraf/Wigner-type problem**, in a broad sense: mathematics seems extraordinarily applicable to physical reality, but the ontology of mathematical objects and their relationship to physical objects is difficult.

The theist can say:

> Mathematics is fundamentally the structure of divine intellect.

That is a serious historical position—Augustinian/Leibnizian in various forms.

Then:

$$
\text{mathematical necessity}
$$

becomes grounded in:

$$
\text{necessary intellect}.
$$

This does not prove theism, but it gives the theist a way of making the mathematical and metaphysical stories cohere.

---

# 15. Now: the “goodness” problem

You're also right that I treated this too casually.

The classical argument isn't:

> God is good because God does good things.

That's circular.

The classical claim is much more metaphysical:

$$
\text{being}
\leftrightarrow
\text{actuality}
\leftrightarrow
\text{perfection}
\leftrightarrow
\text{goodness}.
$$

Aquinas inherits from Aristotle the idea that goodness is related to **desirability/perfection**, and that evil is not a competing substance but a **privation of a due good**.

So the argument isn't:

> “Good exists, therefore God.”

It's:

> **If God is the ultimate source of actuality, then God is the ultimate source of the perfections possessed by things.**

But then we immediately need a theory of value.

And that's where your objection becomes powerful:

> **Why should actuality be identical with goodness?**

That's not self-evident to a modern naturalist.

And:

> Why is suffering a “privation” rather than a positive bad?

Again, classical metaphysics has answers, but they depend upon a substantive theory of flourishing, form, function and perfection.

So **divine goodness is not a free deduction from necessary existence**.

There is a whole additional metaphysical theory underneath it.

---

# 16. What are the serious epistemological sources of theism?

This is perhaps the most important question you've asked.

I would divide them into **five categories**, and distinguish their evidential status.

## A. Metaphysical arguments

These try to infer God from features of reality itself:

* contingency,
* causation,
* grounding,
* necessity,
* existence,
* intelligibility,
* modality.

This is where we've spent most of our time.

The strongest contemporary versions are not “science has a gap.”

They're:

> **Reality appears to have a contingent/derivative layer. What ultimately grounds it?**

---

## B. Modal and mathematical arguments

These concern:

* necessary truths,
* abstract objects,
* mathematical existence,
* modal structure,
* the applicability of mathematics.

They ask:

> Why is there necessary abstract structure?

and:

> Why does concrete reality instantiate mathematical structure so deeply?

These can motivate Platonism, structuralism, conceptualism, or theism.

They don't uniquely point to God.

---

## C. Arguments from mind/consciousness

This is a completely different evidential source.

The argument is roughly:

> We have subjective consciousness, intentionality, rationality and first-person experience.

Can those be completely explained in terms of fundamentally non-conscious matter?

Some philosophers think yes.

Others think there is an explanatory gap.

And some theists argue that consciousness is more naturally understood if mind is fundamental rather than derivative.

This is **not a scientific gap argument**.

It's a metaphysical argument about the ontology of consciousness.

---

## D. Moral realism

Suppose objective moral facts exist:

> torturing a child for amusement is objectively wrong.

Then ask:

> What makes that fact objectively binding?

Naturalists have several answers:

* moral realism without God,
* naturalistic moral facts,
* constructivism,
* contractualism,
* evolutionary accounts plus realism, etc.

So morality does **not** simply prove God.

But if you believe in robust objective normativity, theism offers an attractive grounding story:

$$
\text{objective value}
\rightarrow
\text{ultimate Good}.
$$

Again: abductive, not deductive.

---

## E. Religious experience and testimony

This is the one I think you were particularly leaving open when you said:

> faith doesn't mean blind belief.

Yes.

There can be epistemically serious arguments from:

* personal religious experience,
* mystical experience,
* testimony of others,
* apparent answered prayer,
* transformative experiences,
* perceived encounters with God.

These are not scientific evidence in the narrow sense.

But neither are memories, testimony, perception, or introspection scientific evidence in the narrow sense.

The philosophical question is:

> **Under what conditions is testimony or experience rationally trustworthy?**

William Alston's work on religious experience, for example, tries to defend the rationality of religious belief in a way analogous to our general reliance on perceptual practices.

That doesn't establish Christianity or even theism.

But it undermines:

> “If science can't verify it, believing it is irrational.”

That's much too strong.

---

# 17. And there's a sixth category that I think deserves special attention: rationality itself

This is potentially very relevant to your mathematical point.

We reason.

We believe that:

> valid reasoning tends to lead us toward truth.

But why should a physical organism, produced by evolutionary processes, have cognitive faculties that track:

* mathematics,
* logic,
* metaphysical necessity,
* abstract truths?

Evolution primarily selects for adaptive behavior.

That doesn't automatically mean it produces unreliable reasoning. But it raises the famous **evolutionary argument against naturalism** associated particularly with Plantinga.

The rough structure is:

$$
\text{naturalism + evolution}
$$

may give us reason to doubt the reliability of our cognitive faculties.

If our cognitive faculties are unreliable, then our reasoning toward naturalism itself becomes suspect.

The argument is controversial and has substantial naturalist responses.

But it is exactly the sort of **epistemological argument** you were asking for: it isn't “God because science doesn't know X.” It's about whether the naturalist ontology can adequately account for the reliability of the rational faculties by which we know anything.

---

# 18. Now let's return to multiple universes

You asked something subtle:

> Maybe asking “why are we in this universe?” isn't equally fundamental.

I agree.

Suppose there are 10 universes:

$$
U_1,\ldots,U_{10}.
$$

And our universe is \(U_7\).

Asking:

> “Why am I in \(U_7\)?”

could be merely an **indexical** question.

There doesn't necessarily need to be an explanation.

If all ten exist and observers exist in \(U_7\), then:

> “I observe \(U_7\)”

may simply be a consequence of being an observer located in \(U_7\).

This is where anthropic reasoning enters.

So we should distinguish:

### Fundamental question

> Why does the multiverse exist?

from:

### Selection/indexical question

> Why do I observe this particular member?

The second may be answered by anthropic selection without solving the first.

That's a crucial distinction.

---

# 19. And therefore the multiverse doesn't automatically defeat the cosmological argument

It may answer:

> Why do we see apparently special constants?

without answering:

> Why does the total reality containing all these universes exist?

So:

$$
\text{fine-tuning problem}
$$

and

$$
\text{ultimate existence problem}
$$

are different problems.

A multiverse could be excellent for the first while doing little for the second.

And conversely, the theist could accept a multiverse and still ask for its ultimate ground.

---

# 20. Now let's answer the seven questions we set ourselves

### **1. What does “necessary” mean?**

It means:

> Could not fail to exist.

But we must specify the modality: logical, metaphysical, nomological, etc.

O'Connor's “absolute necessity” is a metaphysical notion, not simply logical necessity. ([Plato][1])

### **2. Can physical reality be necessary?**

Yes, conceptually.

But someone defending that view needs an argument for its necessity, rather than merely saying it's fundamental.

### **3. Can mathematical structure cause anything?**

If mathematics is abstract, this is difficult.

If mathematical structure simply **is** physical reality, then the causal question disappears—but at the cost of a very strong ontological thesis.

### **4. Does a necessary ground need to explain why this world exists?**

If the world is contingent, the theist says yes.

O'Connor tries to preserve contingency through free agency rather than deterministic entailment. ([Tim O'Connor][3])

### **5. Does that require mind?**

Not obviously.

This is one of the weakest links.

O'Connor argues that purposive agency is the best candidate, but Oppy argues that naturalistic alternatives remain preferable. ([OUP Academic][5])

### **6. Is randomness an alternative to intention?**

Yes.

But randomness can explain an outcome **conditional on a probability structure**. Whether it explains why that probability structure exists is another question.

### **7. Does simplicity solve “who explains God?”**

It can solve a particular version:

> God isn't a composite contingent thing requiring an external explanation.

But it doesn't magically prove:

> God exists.

It changes the proposed endpoint from:

> unexplained contingent thing

to:

> necessary, non-composite reality.

The entire argument therefore depends on whether that kind of necessity is independently warranted.

---

# 21. And I think we've reached the deepest version of your original question

The real competition isn't:

> **Science vs God.**

Nor even:

> **Naturalism vs religion.**

It's something closer to:

### Model N

$$
\boxed{
\text{Fundamental reality is impersonal}
}
$$

It might be:

* brute physical reality,
* necessary physical reality,
* necessary mathematical structure,
* or some other impersonal metaphysical structure.

### Model T

$$
\boxed{
\text{Fundamental reality is necessary mind}
}
$$

which then accounts for:

* mathematical truths,
* possibilities,
* contingent actuality,
* causal powers,
* consciousness,
* perhaps normativity.

And now we can compare them according to genuinely philosophical criteria:

1. **Explanatory power**
2. **Internal coherence**
3. **Ontological economy**
4. **Modal plausibility**
5. **Ability to account for contingency**
6. **Ability to account for consciousness**
7. **Ability to account for rationality**
8. **Ability to account for normativity**
9. **Compatibility with empirical reality**

That is a much more interesting contest than asking whether science has “found God.”

---

## And one conclusion I would draw already

I think your intuition that **“the universe is just mathematical”** deserves to be taken much more seriously than I was giving it credit for.

It is not simply a lazy naturalist escape.

It could be a genuine metaphysical competitor to theism.

But the theist has a particularly sharp response:

> **If mathematical structure is necessary and abstract, why should it be concretely instantiated? And if the answer is that mathematical structure simply is concrete reality, what explains the fact that this structure is the actual structure?**

The naturalist can answer:

> “Nothing further. It is necessary.”

And then the theist can answer:

> “Why is *that* necessity more credible than necessary mind?”

At that point, we've reached the real philosophical core.

And **O'Connor's contribution is particularly interesting because he tries to answer exactly that question by arguing that a necessary reality capable of grounding genuinely contingent effects is better understood as a purposive agent than as an impersonal necessary structure.** ([Tim O'Connor][3])

But—and this is important for keeping the discussion intellectually honest—**that argument is contested even by philosophers who accept much of the contingency framework.** Oppy's critique is not “science disproves God”; it is that brute contingency, infinite regress, or immanent necessity may be theoretically preferable to O'Connor's transcendent necessary being. ([OUP Academic][5])

So I think **the next thing we should do is actually reconstruct O'Connor's identification argument premise by premise**, including his argument for *why the necessary being must have a nature involving agency, desires, intentions and beliefs*, and then put Oppy's naturalistic alternative beside each premise.

That will let us test the exact point at which:

$$
\text{necessary ground}
$$

becomes:

$$
\text{necessary mind}
$$

rather than merely asserting that it does.

[3]: https://toconnor.org/2018/07/14/and-this-all-men-call-god/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "‘And This All Men Call God’ – Tim O'Connor"
[4]: https://philpapers.org/rec/OCOTAU-6?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Timothy O’Connor, Theism and Ultimate Explanation - PhilPapers"
[5]: https://academic.oup.com/book/8889/chapter-abstract/155148843?utm_source=chatgpt.com "9 O'Connor's Cosmological Argument | Oxford Studies in Philosophy of Religion Volume 3 | Oxford Academic"

Expand on the metaphysical theory of goodnes.
