Yes. I think we can now make the discussion considerably sharper. There are really **four separate issues** that have been getting intertwined:

1. **What establishes metaphysical necessity for O'Connor?**
2. **What exactly is metaphysical modality?**
3. **Why does Oppy think the naturalist alternatives are preferable?**
4. **Once we compare the two pictures, what actually gets us from “necessary ground” to “God”?**

And I agree that the right way to proceed is not to caricature either side. O'Connor's position is much more sophisticated than “we don't like brute facts,” while Oppy's response is much stronger than “science doesn't need God.”

---

# 1. First: what does “SEP” mean?

**SEP = Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy.**

It is a major academic reference work in philosophy, with articles written and peer-reviewed/edited by specialists. It isn't an argument in itself; it's a useful way of establishing what the contemporary philosophical landscape looks like.

So when I cite SEP, think of it roughly as an authoritative philosophical encyclopedia, not as “the position of Stanford.”

---

# 2. What establishes necessity for O'Connor?

This is the crucial point.

**O'Connor does not simply discover that some particular thing is necessary and then infer God.**

His argument has a prior commitment:

> **Reality has genuine modal structure.**

That means that statements such as

> “This could not have been otherwise”

and

> “This could have been otherwise”

are not merely ways of speaking, or descriptions of our ignorance. They correspond to something real about the world.

O'Connor calls his position a form of **modal realism**—not David Lewis's famous “there are infinitely many concrete possible worlds” theory, but a realist view that modality is an objective feature of reality. He argues against various attempts to reduce or deflate modality. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

His thought is roughly:

### Step 1

Our reasoning already presupposes genuine modality.

For example:

> Copper is disposed to conduct electricity.

This isn't merely:

> “Whenever we've observed copper, it conducted.”

It says something about **what copper can and cannot do given its nature**.

Likewise:

> A particular fundamental kind of thing necessarily has certain properties.

O'Connor therefore adopts a broadly **neo-Aristotelian dispositional metaphysics**: genuine properties have causal powers/dispositions, and these powers have modal consequences. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

### Step 2

If modality were merely a linguistic convention or shorthand for actual facts, O'Connor thinks we couldn't adequately account for the modal reasoning that science and ordinary explanation already employ.

So he argues that modality is **ontologically serious**.

### Step 3

Once genuine modality is admitted, we can distinguish:

$$
\text{contingent}
$$

from

$$
\text{necessary}.
$$

And then the question becomes:

> What ultimately explains the existence of contingent reality?

O'Connor's answer is:

> Contingent reality cannot ultimately explain itself; its ultimate ground must therefore be necessary.

This is why the modal argument comes **before** the God argument.

---

# 3. But this means O'Connor has a controversial first premise

And this is important.

He isn't deriving metaphysical necessity from physics.

He is saying:

> **A realist metaphysics of modality is already required to make sense of our best reasoning about properties, causation, possibility and necessity.**

That's a philosophical argument.

And it is contestable.

SEP's discussion of the epistemology of modality makes precisely this point: logical and physical modality have relatively straightforward definitions, whereas metaphysical modality is much more controversial. One standard approach defines it in terms of possible worlds, but there are competing accounts based on metaphysical laws or other structures. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

So we should **not** say:

> “O'Connor proves necessity.”

Better:

> **O'Connor argues that a realist conception of metaphysical modality is independently warranted, and given that framework, he argues that contingent reality requires a necessary ground.**

That is much more accurate.

---

# 4. What exactly is metaphysical modality?

Let's make this very precise.

Take a proposition:

$$
P = \text{“Water is H₂O.”}
$$

We can ask several different modal questions.

### Logical necessity

Is \(P\) required by logic alone?

No.

There is no contradiction in:

> “Water is not H₂O.”

if we're using “water” merely as an arbitrary label.

---

### Physical necessity

Given the actual laws of physics, is \(P\) necessary?

This is a different question.

---

### Metaphysical necessity

Could there genuinely have been a world in which **water itself** wasn't H₂O?

After Kripke, many philosophers would say:

> No.

If something is water, its chemical identity is H₂O.

So:

$$
\Box(\text{water}=\mathrm{H_2O})
$$

even though this isn't a theorem of logic.

---

### Metaphysical possibility

Conversely:

$$
\Diamond P
$$

means roughly:

> There is some genuinely possible way reality could have been in which \(P\) is true.

So:

$$
\Box P \equiv \neg\Diamond\neg P
$$

in standard modal logic.

The difficult question isn't the formal logic.

The difficult question is:

> **What determines which worlds are metaphysically possible?**

That's the deep metaphysical problem.

Are possible worlds:

* abstract mathematical structures?
* maximally consistent descriptions?
* recombinations of actual properties?
* structures governed by metaphysical essences?
* something primitive?
* God's ideas?
* something else?

O'Connor thinks **modality is a real feature of reality**, not something reducible to language or our conceptual scheme. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

---

# 5. And this actually connects beautifully with your mathematical point

You were saying earlier:

> We can generate enormous mathematical structures from relatively simple starting principles.

Exactly.

But notice what you're doing.

You are exploring a **space of possibility**.

From:

$$
\mathbb N
$$

you can construct:

$$
\mathbb Z,\mathbb Q,\mathbb R,
$$

groups, rings, fields, manifolds, topological spaces, etc.

Some structures are:

* possible,
* impossible,
* internally consistent,
* inconsistent,
* derivable,
* independent.

So mathematics itself seems saturated with modality.

The question then becomes:

> **What makes modal facts true?**

For example:

$$
\Box(\text{a group inverse is unique})
$$

isn't an empirical generalization.

It's necessary *given the relevant mathematical structure*.

But then we can ask:

> What makes mathematical necessity itself necessary?

That is exactly where your earlier intuition about God enters.

---

# 6. But there's an important correction

We should **not** say:

> “Mathematical necessity therefore requires God.”

That's much too quick.

There are at least four possibilities:

### Platonism

Mathematical objects/structures exist necessarily and independently of minds.

### Structuralism

Mathematics concerns structures rather than independently existing objects.

### Formalism/fictionalism

Mathematical necessity is ultimately grounded in rules, formal systems, or useful representational practices.

### Theistic conceptualism

Mathematical truths ultimately reside in the necessary intellect of God.

The last is historically important—Augustine, Leibniz and others develop versions of it—but it is one metaphysical option among several.

So mathematics doesn't straightforwardly *prove* God.

What it does is make the **metaphysics of necessity** impossible to ignore.

---

# 7. Now we can understand Oppy's objection much better

Oppy isn't saying:

> “Brute facts are fine because science doesn't care.”

His position is considerably more sophisticated.

He says:

> **Even granting much of O'Connor's framework, naturalism has several possible ultimate structures, and O'Connor hasn't demonstrated that the theistic one is theoretically superior.**

His three main naturalistic alternatives are:

### 1. Infinite regress

There is no ultimate terminus.

$$
A_1 \leftarrow A_2 \leftarrow A_3 \leftarrow \cdots
$$

No final ground.

### 2. Brute contingency

Reality ultimately bottoms out in something contingent that simply exists.

$$
\boxed{B}
$$

No further explanation.

### 3. Necessary non-agential reality

The ultimate physical/natural structure simply exists necessarily.

$$
\boxed{\Box N}
$$

Oppy says naturalists don't even necessarily have to choose among these. He argues that each may have greater theoretical virtue than introducing a transcendent necessary agent. ([OUP Academic][3])

---

# 8. Why does Oppy prefer these?

This is where the **theoretical virtue** criterion becomes important.

Oppy compares theories using things like:

* simplicity,
* explanatory scope,
* explanatory power,
* fit with evidence,
* fit with established science,
* ontological economy,
* avoidance of ad hoc assumptions.

He explicitly argues that if there is an infinite regress, naturalism doesn't gain anything by adding God; and if there is brute contingency or necessary non-agential reality, those are, in his view, simpler than theism. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

His basic thought is:

> Suppose both theories terminate somewhere.

Naturalism:

$$
\boxed{\text{necessary impersonal reality}}
$$

Theism:

$$
\boxed{\text{necessary personal reality}}
$$

Why pay for:

* consciousness,
* intentionality,
* agency,
* beliefs,
* desires,
* intentions,
* divine powers,

if none of those adds explanatory power?

That's a **very serious Occam's Razor argument**.

---

# 9. O'Connor's answer is: because contingency needs agency

This is where his argument becomes much more interesting.

Suppose:

$$
N = \text{necessary being}.
$$

And:

$$
W = \text{our contingent universe}.
$$

If:

$$
N \Rightarrow W
$$

then \(W\) becomes necessary.

But:

$$
\neg\Box W.
$$

So we cannot have:

$$
\Box N \land (N \Rightarrow W)
$$

if \(W\) is genuinely contingent.

O'Connor's solution is:

$$
\boxed{N \text{ freely produces } W}
$$

rather than:

$$
\boxed{N \text{ necessarily entails } W}.
$$

This is why **agency** enters.

A necessary agent can have:

$$
\Box N
$$

while freely producing:

$$
\Diamond W.
$$

This is the key insight behind his “Logos” model.

His preferred scheme involves a necessary being whose intention to create is not mechanically necessitated by its nature; the intention is “irreducibly a product of the agent qua agent.” ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

---

# 10. And here Oppy has a devastatingly precise objection

You raised randomness earlier.

Oppy says:

> Fine. But why can't the necessary reality simply produce outcomes probabilistically?

Suppose:

$$
N \rightarrow
\begin{cases}
W_1 & 20\%\\
W_2 & 30\%\\
W_3 & 50\%
\end{cases}
$$

Then \(N\) doesn't necessitate which universe occurs.

You've preserved contingency.

But now O'Connor needs to explain:

> What establishes this probability distribution?

And more importantly:

> What makes this probabilistic process preferable to brute contingency?

Oppy argues that if O'Connor wants agency to genuinely *produce* the outcome, there needs to be some meaningful counterfactual or probabilistic dependence. Otherwise words like “control” and “production” don't do much explanatory work. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

This is a very good objection.

---

# 11. The free-will issue you mentioned

This deserves a separate treatment because people often mix three completely different things.

### Quantum indeterminacy

$$
\text{not deterministic}
$$

does **not** imply:

$$
\text{free}.
$$

A radioactive atom doesn't appear to “choose” anything.

So:

$$
\text{indeterminism} \neq \text{agency}.
$$

---

### Determinism

Likewise:

$$
\text{determinism}
$$

doesn't automatically imply:

$$
\text{no free will}.
$$

That's the compatibilist position.

---

### Agent causation

The O'Connor-relevant position is stronger:

> An agent can be the originator of an action in a way that isn't reducible to prior event-to-event causation.

This is called **agent-causal libertarianism**.

The agent isn't simply another event in a causal chain.

Rather:

$$
\boxed{\text{agent} \rightarrow \text{decision}}
$$

where the agent is itself the source of the causal process.

The contemporary philosophical literature explicitly distinguishes this from ordinary event causation. Agent-causal theories hold that the substance/agent itself originates the decision rather than a prior event determining it. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][4])

And **this is the relevant philosophical argument for O'Connor**, not whether individual particles possess “free will.”

Quantum mechanics might establish indeterminism.

It does not establish agent causation.

That distinction is absolutely essential.

---

# 12. Now let's expand the theory of goodness

This is another place where classical theism is considerably more sophisticated than:

> “God is good because God says so.”

The classical tradition begins with a metaphysics of **being and actuality**.

Very roughly:

$$
\text{good} \sim \text{that which is desirable/perfective}
$$

and:

$$
\text{evil} \sim \text{privation of an appropriate good}.
$$

A thing is good insofar as it possesses the actuality/perfection appropriate to its nature.

For example:

A good eye is one that possesses the capacities appropriate to an eye.

A good tree is one that successfully realizes the characteristic capacities of its kind.

A good human being realizes the characteristic excellences of human nature.

This is an **Aristotelian theory of teleology**.

Modern Aristotelian philosophers still develop versions of this idea, although contemporary moral naturalism has many alternatives. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][5])

---

# 13. Why does classical theism identify God with Goodness?

The argument becomes:

1. Things have degrees of actuality/perfection.
2. Perfection is a form of goodness.
3. Finite things possess goodness derivatively.
4. If all finite actuality is ultimately grounded in God,
5. then God is the ultimate source of actuality and therefore goodness.

So God isn't merely:

> “A very morally good person.”

Classical theism means something much stronger:

$$
\boxed{\text{God is the ultimate fullness/source of being and goodness.}}
$$

This is why the classical God is called **ipsum esse subsistens** in Aquinas:

> subsistent Being itself.

The modern theist who simply says:

> “God is a maximally good person”

is actually simplifying a much deeper metaphysics.

---

# 14. But your objection remains

You can ask:

> Why should actuality be good?

That's a legitimate objection.

The classical answer is essentially:

> To exist is to possess actuality; actuality is perfection relative to potentiality; perfection is good.

But that depends on accepting Aristotelian metaphysics.

A naturalist can instead say:

> Existence isn't intrinsically valuable.

And now we have a genuine philosophical disagreement.

---

# 15. And what about evil?

The classical answer is **privation theory**.

Evil isn't a positive substance competing with God.

Instead:

$$
\text{evil} = \text{privation of a due good}.
$$

Blindness is evil for an organism whose nature includes sight.

Disease is a privation of proper bodily functioning.

Injustice is a privation/disorder in rational/social relations.

The classical view therefore doesn't say:

> “Badness is another kind of stuff.”

It says:

> **Badness is a deficiency in being/actuality relative to what a thing ought to be.**

Augustine and Aquinas develop this idea, and it remains a central classical theodicy strategy. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][6])

But this doesn't solve the problem of evil.

It answers:

> **What is evil?**

It does not yet answer:

> **Why would a perfectly good God permit so much privation?**

That's a separate problem.

And here O'Connor's cosmological argument doesn't get you very far.

---

# 16. This gives us a useful distinction

There are actually **three different “goodness” arguments** that often get conflated:

### A. Ontological goodness

$$
\text{being} \rightarrow \text{perfection} \rightarrow \text{goodness}
$$

Classical metaphysics.

### B. Moral goodness

$$
\text{right/wrong}
$$

Metaethics.

### C. Divine goodness

$$
\text{God is perfectly good}.
$$

These require additional arguments.

You can't derive all three from “necessary being.”

---

# 17. Now let's put the epistemological arguments into one structure

Here's where I think your project gets particularly interesting.

You don't have to say:

> “Science proves God.”

Instead, you can say:

> **Theism is a hypothesis intended to unify several kinds of evidence that aren't exhausted by empirical science.**

Consider:

| Phenomenon                 | Naturalist explanation                               | Theistic explanation                               |
| -------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Contingency                | brute / necessary nature / regress                   | necessary God                                      |
| Mathematical necessity     | Platonism / structuralism / brute modal facts        | divine intellect                                   |
| Mathematical applicability | structural identity / selection / brute fact         | intelligibility grounded in intellect              |
| Consciousness              | physicalism / emergence / panpsychism etc.           | mind fundamental                                   |
| Rationality                | evolution + reliable cognition                       | rational mind ultimately grounded in rational Mind |
| Objective morality         | naturalistic realism / constructivism / error theory | objective value grounded in Good                   |
| Modal structure            | primitive / structural / dispositional               | grounded in necessary being/intellect              |
| Religious experience       | psychological/social/neural explanations             | possible perception of God                         |

Notice what happens.

**None of these individually entails God.**

The theistic case is cumulative.

$$
E_1+E_2+E_3+\cdots
$$

may make:

$$
P(\text{Theism}\mid E)
$$

greater than:

$$
P(\text{Naturalism}\mid E).
$$

That is an **inference to the best explanation**, not a deductive proof.

---

# 18. And naturalism can do the same thing

Naturalism says:

> Why multiply entities?

It can construct a unified picture:

$$
\boxed{\text{one natural reality}}
$$

from which:

* mathematics,
* consciousness,
* life,
* morality,
* rationality

emerge or are instantiated.

It doesn't necessarily have to explain each of these by one fundamental entity.

So the real competition is:

### Theism

$$
\boxed{
\text{Necessary Mind}
\rightarrow
\begin{cases}
\text{modality}\\
\text{mathematics}\\
\text{physical reality}\\
\text{mind}\\
\text{value}
\end{cases}}
$$

versus:

### Naturalism

$$
\boxed{
\text{Necessary/Brute Natural Reality}
\rightarrow
\begin{cases}
\text{physical structures}\\
\text{mathematics}\\
\text{mind}\\
\text{value}
\end{cases}}
$$

The question becomes:

> **Which architecture has fewer unexplained primitives relative to what it explains?**

That's the right comparison.

---

# 19. Let's score them philosophically

Not numerically—because that would create false precision—but conceptually.

### Explanatory scope

**Theism:** Very strong.

A single fundamental entity potentially accounts for reality, modality, mind and value.

**Naturalism:** Also potentially strong, but often requires separate explanatory stories for consciousness, normativity, mathematical necessity, etc.

**Advantage: theism, if its grounding relations work.**

---

### Ontological simplicity

**Naturalism:** Strong.

No transcendent person required.

**Theism:** Weak at first sight because God is a very rich entity.

But the theist can respond:

> A necessary, simple mind isn't necessarily ontologically “more complicated” than a gigantic brute physical structure.

So simplicity becomes complicated.

**Advantage: naturalism prima facie.**

---

### Explanation of contingency

**Theism:** Very strong if necessary agency is coherent.

$$
\Box G + \text{free act} \rightarrow \Diamond W
$$

**Naturalism:** weaker if it terminates in brute contingency; stronger if it has necessary natural structure.

**Advantage: theism against brute naturalism; much less clear against necessary naturalism.**

---

### Explanation of consciousness

Potential advantage for theism.

If consciousness is fundamental, the theistic picture makes it unsurprising.

But idealist, panpsychist and other non-theistic views exist.

So:

**modest advantage to theism, not decisive.**

---

### Explanation of mathematics

This is genuinely interesting.

Theism:

> necessary truths reside in necessary intellect.

Naturalism:

> mathematical structures are necessary abstract structures / structural facts.

Neither is obviously victorious.

---

### Objective morality

Theism gives a powerful grounding story.

But contemporary naturalistic moral realism is a serious position. The SEP notes that naturalistic moral realism can treat objective moral properties as natural, including complex causally embedded properties; moral realism therefore does not automatically imply theism. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][7])

So again:

**interesting advantage, not knockout.**

---

### Parsimony

Naturalism wins initially.

Theism needs to show that the extra ontology is **explanatorily productive** rather than ornamental.

This is perhaps the most important criterion.

---

# 20. And this gives us the strongest version of your desired dichotomy

Your original:

> **“Either God exists or we can't explain reality.”**

is rhetorically excellent but philosophically too strong.

Because a naturalist can say:

> “Reality simply has a necessary natural structure.”

So I'd formulate the **sharp version** as:

> ### **Either ultimate reality is grounded in a necessary personal source, or the ultimate natural order is itself necessary, brute, or regressively unexplained.**

That's technically much safer.

But it doesn't have quite the polemical punch you wanted.

So I'd use:

> ## **Either reality is grounded in a necessary mind, or its ultimate order is an unexplained fact.**

And then the technically precise expansion:

> **Every account of reality must ultimately terminate either in something whose existence is necessary and whose explanatory power derives from its own nature, or in contingent facts, an infinite regress, or an impersonal necessary structure whose existence and modal character are not further explained. The central dispute between theism and naturalism is therefore not whether science explains the universe, but what—if anything—can constitute a non-arbitrary ultimate ground of contingent reality.**

I think **that** captures almost everything we've been circling around.

And there is an even sharper O'Connor-specific formulation:

> ## **If contingent reality has an ultimate explanation, its ultimate ground must either necessitate what exists or freely actualize what could have been otherwise.**
>
> **If it necessitates it, the apparent contingency of reality becomes mysterious; if it freely actualizes it, we have something much closer to a necessary personal agent.**

That is, in my view, the most interesting form of O'Connor's argument.

---

## One final qualification that I think is important

I would **not yet say that O'Connor has established God**.

He has constructed a fascinating chain:

$$
\text{real modality}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\text{contingent reality}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\text{need for ultimate ground}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\text{necessary ground}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\text{necessary ground cannot simply entail contingent reality}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\text{agency provides a model of contingent actualization}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\text{necessary personal creator}
$$

The **weakest transitions are the last two**.

That's exactly where Oppy attacks.

And I think this is actually good news for your investigation, because we've now located the real battlefield rather than vaguely debating “science versus God.”

The next step I'd suggest is to take **those two transitions apart with formal precision**:

1. **Why can't an impersonal necessary reality produce contingent reality?**
2. **Why does free agency succeed where randomness, laws, dispositions, or probabilistic processes don't?**
3. **Does agent causation actually solve the problem, or merely rename the unexplained choice?**
4. **Then, if agency succeeds, what properties follow—intellect, intentionality, omnipotence, goodness, unity, transcendence—and which ones require additional arguments?**

That is where O'Connor either becomes a genuinely powerful argument for God—or where the naturalist can finally say, “No, you've only moved the brute fact one level up.”

[1]: https://ndpr.nd.edu/reviews/theism-and-ultimate-explanation-the-necessary-shape-of-contingency/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Theism and Ultimate Explanation: The Necessary Shape of Contingency | Reviews | Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews | University of Notre Dame"
[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/fall2021/entries/modality-epistemology/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "The Epistemology of Modality (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy/Fall 2021 Edition)"
[3]: https://academic.oup.com/book/8889/chapter-abstract/155148843?utm_source=chatgpt.com "9 O'Connor's Cosmological Argument | Oxford Studies in Philosophy of Religion Volume 3 | Oxford Academic"
[4]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/incompatibilism-theories/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Incompatibilist (Nondeterministic) Theories of Free Will (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[5]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/naturalism-moral/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Moral Naturalism (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[6]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/theodicies/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Theodicies (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[7]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/moral-realism/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Moral Realism (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"




 But the reply to 10 is what you already replied to me that then that probability distribution is not fundamental.

Is agent caused libertarianism related to more broad libertarianism in philosophy and economics etc?

What are the more modern conceptions of goodness? Sounds as if what it is actual is good, since how do you otherwise identify what is the supposedly good characteristic? And therefore evil would not exist?

So which architecture has fewer primitives? Sounds to me like naturalist unifications is similar to theist but impersonal rather than personal, so the difference is not massive.


Go ahead answering the suggested questions



I think we're finally at the point where the disagreement becomes genuinely interesting rather than rhetorical.

The key thing I want to do in this answer is stop treating “theism” and “naturalism” as giant black boxes. Instead, we'll isolate exactly where the philosophical work is being done. And I think you'll see that your objections have already eliminated several weak versions of the theistic argument.

There are five threads here:

1. Your objection about the probability distribution.

2. Agent-causal libertarianism and its relation to libertarianism generally.

3. Modern theories of goodness.

4. Whether the naturalist and theist architectures really differ much in number of primitives.

5. The two hardest questions we left open:

   * Why can't an impersonal necessary reality produce contingency?

   * Why would agency do better?

# 1. You're right: the probability distribution objection just moves the problem upward

You caught an important point.

Previously I said to your randomness objection:

> Why not randomly select one universe?

and answered:

> Then the probability distribution itself needs explanation.

You correctly replied:

> But that just means the distribution isn't fundamental.

Exactly.

So let's distinguish two cases.

## Case A: The distribution is contingent

Suppose there is a necessary generator NNN, but it has contingent probabilities:

P(W1)=0.2,P(W2)=0.8P(W_1)=0.2,\quad P(W_2)=0.8P(W1)=0.2,P(W2)=0.8

Then the obvious question is:

> Why those probabilities?

The distribution becomes another contingent feature needing grounding.

So this doesn't terminate explanation.

## Case B: The distribution is necessary

Now suppose instead:

> It is metaphysically necessary that the generator has exactly this probability law.

Then we've reached an ultimate structure:

![](data\:image/svg+xml;charset=utf-8,%3Csvg%20font-family%3D%22-apple-system-body%2C%20ui-sans-serif%2C%20-apple-system%2C%20system-ui%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%26quot%3B%2C%20Helvetica%2C%20%26quot%3BApple%20Color%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20Arial%2C%20sans-serif%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Symbol%26quot%3B%22%20font-weight%3D%22400%22%20data-d-component%3D%22svg%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20style%3D%22color%3Argb\(255%2C%20255%2C%20255\)%22%20viewBox%3D%220%200%20320%20220%22%20width%3D%22100%25%22%20xmlns%3D%22http%3A%2F%2Fwww.w3.org%2F2000%2Fsvg%22%3E%3Crect%20width%3D%22320%22%20height%3D%22220%22%20rx%3D%2218%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20opacity%3D%220.35%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2278%22%20y%3D%2218%22%20width%3D%22164%22%20height%3D%2236%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%2240%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2213%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3ENecessary%20structure%20N%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M160%2054%20V72%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2248%22%20y%3D%2272%22%20width%3D%22224%22%20height%3D%2242%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%2289%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2212%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3ENecessary%20probability%20law%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%22103%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EP\(W%E2%82%81\)%2C%20P\(W%E2%82%82\)%2C%20...%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M160%20114%20V132%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2218%22%20y%3D%22132%22%20width%3D%2288%22%20height%3D%2270%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22116%22%20y%3D%22132%22%20width%3D%2288%22%20height%3D%2270%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22214%22%20y%3D%22132%22%20width%3D%2288%22%20height%3D%2270%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2262%22%20y%3D%22150%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2212%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EW%E2%82%81%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2262%22%20y%3D%22166%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Econtingent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2262%22%20y%3D%22179%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eoutcome%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%22150%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2212%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EW%E2%82%82%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%22166%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Econtingent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%22179%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eoutcome%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22258%22%20y%3D%22150%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2212%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EW%E2%82%83%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22258%22%20y%3D%22166%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Econtingent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22258%22%20y%3D%22179%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eoutcome%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

Now O'Connor's argument has to confront this much stronger naturalist proposal.

The naturalist says:

> I don't have a contingent universe produced by an unexplained mechanism. I have a necessary impersonal structure whose laws include irreducible stochasticity.

That is a perfectly coherent metaphysical position.

And this is important:

O'Connor cannot refute it merely by saying that contingency exists.

He needs to argue that necessary impersonal probabilistic structure is somehow inferior to necessary agency.

That is exactly where Graham Oppy's criticism bites. Oppy explicitly argues that naturalists may reasonably prefer one of three ultimate pictures—infinite regress, brute contingency, or immanent necessity—over a transcendent necessary being. His criticism is not that O'Connor's reasoning is invalid at every step, but that the naturalistic stopping points are theoretically competitive.

![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://academic.oup.com\&sz=32)

Oxford Academic+1

So your correction is absolutely right:

> The probability objection only works if the probability law is contingent. If it is necessary, the argument has not yet reached God.

That leaves O'Connor with a much harder task.

# 2. Is agent-causal libertarianism related to broader libertarianism?

Yes—but the terminology is unfortunately overloaded.

There are three different libertarianisms that people constantly confuse.

## Political libertarianism

This is the political philosophy associated with:

* limited government,

* strong property rights,

* individual liberty.

Completely unrelated.

## Economic libertarianism

Usually just a branch or practical expression of political libertarianism emphasizing free markets.

Also unrelated.

## Libertarian free will

This is the philosophical position that:

1. genuine free will exists;

2. determinism is incompatible with that kind of freedom;

3. therefore at least some human actions are not determined by prior events.

This is the family to which O'Connor belongs.

Within philosophical libertarianism there are multiple theories.

![](data\:image/svg+xml;charset=utf-8,%3Csvg%20font-family%3D%22-apple-system-body%2C%20ui-sans-serif%2C%20-apple-system%2C%20system-ui%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%26quot%3B%2C%20Helvetica%2C%20%26quot%3BApple%20Color%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20Arial%2C%20sans-serif%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Symbol%26quot%3B%22%20font-weight%3D%22400%22%20data-d-component%3D%22svg%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20style%3D%22color%3Argb\(255%2C%20255%2C%20255\)%22%20viewBox%3D%220%200%20340%20260%22%20width%3D%22100%25%22%20xmlns%3D%22http%3A%2F%2Fwww.w3.org%2F2000%2Fsvg%22%3E%3Crect%20width%3D%22340%22%20height%3D%22260%22%20rx%3D%2218%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20opacity%3D%220.35%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2270%22%20y%3D%2218%22%20width%3D%22200%22%20height%3D%2234%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22170%22%20y%3D%2239%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2213%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3ELibertarian%20free%20will%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M170%2052%20V72%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M170%2072%20L58%2096%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M170%2072%20V96%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M170%2072%20L282%2096%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2210%22%20y%3D%2296%22%20width%3D%2296%22%20height%3D%2262%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2258%22%20y%3D%22114%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3ENon-causal%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2258%22%20y%3D%22128%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%229%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eactions%20not%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2258%22%20y%3D%22139%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%229%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Efully%20caused%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22122%22%20y%3D%2296%22%20width%3D%2296%22%20height%3D%2262%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22170%22%20y%3D%22114%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EEvent-causal%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22170%22%20y%3D%22128%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%229%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Ereasons%20%26amp%3B%20beliefs%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22170%22%20y%3D%22139%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%229%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eindeterministically%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22234%22%20y%3D%2296%22%20width%3D%2296%22%20height%3D%2262%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22282%22%20y%3D%22114%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EAgent-causal%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22282%22%20y%3D%22128%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%229%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Ethe%20agent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22282%22%20y%3D%22139%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%229%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eoriginates%20action%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2254%22%20y%3D%22188%22%20width%3D%22232%22%20height%3D%2248%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22170%22%20y%3D%22207%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EO'Connor%20defends%20this%20third%20family%3A%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22170%22%20y%3D%22221%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3Esubstance%2Fagent%20causation%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

The distinction is important.

### Event-causal libertarian

Imagine I choose tea rather than coffee.

My:

* beliefs,

* desires,

* preferences,

* deliberation

cause my decision.

But the causal process is indeterministic rather than deterministic.

So the events do the causing.

### Agent-causal libertarian

O'Connor says that's still insufficient.

Why?

Because if all that exists are prior events producing later events—even probabilistically—then one can still ask:

> Did I originate the action, or did events merely happen inside me?

His answer is:

> The agent herself is a genuine cause.

Not merely her mental events.

That is called substance causation or agent causation.

The contemporary literature still treats agent-causal libertarianism as a serious position within incompatibilist theories of free will, with O'Connor among its principal defenders. Recent work continues debating whether it gives a better account of sourcehood and reasons-responsiveness than event-causal libertarianism.

![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://academic.oup.com\&sz=32)

Oxford Academic+3

Notice how this connects to cosmology.

O'Connor is effectively saying:

> A necessary being can produce contingent reality in the same metaphysical way that an agent can freely perform one action among several possible actions.

The structure is analogous.

Not identical—but analogous.

# 3. The quantum free-will issue: where particles enter the discussion

I think I know the debate you're referring to.

There has been renewed discussion around quantum indeterminacy, Bell-type results, Conway and Kochen's Free Will Theorem, superdeterminism, causal independence, and whether “free choice” assumptions in physics have philosophical implications.

But there is an essential distinction.

![](data\:image/svg+xml;charset=utf-8,%3Csvg%20font-family%3D%22-apple-system-body%2C%20ui-sans-serif%2C%20-apple-system%2C%20system-ui%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%26quot%3B%2C%20Helvetica%2C%20%26quot%3BApple%20Color%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20Arial%2C%20sans-serif%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Symbol%26quot%3B%22%20font-weight%3D%22400%22%20data-d-component%3D%22svg%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20style%3D%22color%3Argb\(255%2C%20255%2C%20255\)%22%20viewBox%3D%220%200%20320%20280%22%20width%3D%22100%25%22%20xmlns%3D%22http%3A%2F%2Fwww.w3.org%2F2000%2Fsvg%22%3E%3Crect%20width%3D%22320%22%20height%3D%22280%22%20rx%3D%2218%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20opacity%3D%220.35%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%2224%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2213%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EThree%20different%20claims%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2218%22%20y%3D%2242%22%20width%3D%22284%22%20height%3D%2256%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%2260%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3E%3Clist%20marker%3D%22number%22%3E%3Clist-item%3E%3Ctext%3EQuantum%20indeterminism%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Flist-item%3E%3C%2Flist%3E%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%2276%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%3EFuture%20events%20are%20not%20fully%20determined.%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2218%22%20y%3D%22112%22%20width%3D%22284%22%20height%3D%2256%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%22130%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3E%3Clist%20marker%3D%22number%22%20start%3D%222%22%3E%3Clist-item%3E%3Ctext%3ELibertarian%20freedom%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Flist-item%3E%3C%2Flist%3E%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%22146%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%3ESome%20agents%20genuinely%20could%20do%20otherwise.%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2218%22%20y%3D%22182%22%20width%3D%22284%22%20height%3D%2278%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%22200%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3E%3Clist%20marker%3D%22number%22%20start%3D%223%22%3E%3Clist-item%3E%3Ctext%3EAgent%20causation%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Flist-item%3E%3C%2Flist%3E%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%22216%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%3EThe%20agent%20is%20an%20irreducible%20originator%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%22229%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%3Erather%20than%20merely%20a%20locus%20of%20events.%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%2230%22%20y%3D%22242%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%3EThis%20is%20O'Connor's%20view.%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

These do not imply one another.

Quantum mechanics may undermine strict determinism.

That does not establish libertarian freedom.

Likewise libertarian freedom does not automatically establish agent causation; one might instead defend event-causal libertarianism.

The Conway–Kochen theorem, despite its provocative name, is not a proof that particles literally possess human-style free will. Its “free will” terminology concerns assumptions about the independence of experimental choices and corresponding indeterminacy properties of particle responses. It belongs primarily to the foundations of quantum mechanics, not directly to moral agency.

For our discussion, therefore, the important argument is philosophical rather than physical:

> Can irreducibly personal causation exist?

O'Connor says yes.

A reductionist naturalist often says no.

That is the real dispute.

# 4. Modern conceptions of goodness

Your criticism of the Aristotelian account is exactly the standard modern criticism.

You noticed something important:

> If goodness is simply actuality, then whatever actually exists seems good.

And clearly:

* cancer exists,

* cruelty exists,

* torture exists,

* earthquakes exist.

So how can actuality equal goodness?

The answer is that Aquinas does not identify “existing” with “morally good.”

He identifies being with goodness in a much more technical sense.

Let's reconstruct it.

## The classical picture

Every natural thing has a nature.

A nature includes characteristic capacities.

For example:

|
Thing

|

Characteristic capacities

|
| --- | --- |
|

Eye

|

Seeing

|
|

Heart

|

Pumping blood

|
|

Oak tree

|

Growing and flourishing as an oak

|
|

Human

|

Rationality, sociality, practical reason

|

Goodness is then defined relative to the fulfillment of those capacities.

So blindness is not evil because blindness exists as some mysterious substance.

Blindness is evil because it is the absence of a perfection proper to an eye.

Likewise disease.

Likewise ignorance in a rational being.

This is called the privation theory.

The important point is:

> Evil is not “non-being” absolutely.

It is the lack of some due perfection.

A blind eye still exists.

It simply lacks sight.

An unjust society exists.

It lacks justice.

That is much subtler than saying “everything actual is good.”

Classical natural-law ethics still develops morality from this teleological picture of human flourishing, though modern philosophers debate whether natural functions can genuinely ground normative facts.

![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://plato.stanford.edu\&sz=32)

stanford.edu+1

## Modern theories depart dramatically

Today there is no consensus theory of goodness.

Here are four major families.

Aristotelian / Neo-Aristotelian

Goodness consists in flourishing according to one's nature or life-form.

This is the closest descendant of Aquinas.

Consequentialism

Goodness is fundamentally about outcomes—well-being, happiness, preference satisfaction, etc.

There is no need for Aristotelian essences.

Deontology

Goodness is connected to rational duties and respect for persons.

Kantian ethics grounds morality differently from flourishing.

Moral realism without theism

Objective moral truths exist as fundamental features of reality.

Contemporary philosophers defend this without invoking God.

Notice what happened.

The ancient question:

> What is good?

has split into many independent philosophical research programs.

Theism is therefore not competing with one account of goodness.

It can be combined with Aristotelian ethics, divine command theory, natural law, virtue ethics, and others.

# 5. Does evil exist on the privation theory?

Yes—but not as a positive metaphysical substance.

Think of a hole.

A hole is real.

You can fall into it.

It has causal consequences.

Yet it isn't an additional piece of matter.

It is an absence relative to surrounding matter.

Privation theory treats evil somewhat analogously.

Evil is real as a defect, not as an independent ontological ingredient competing with goodness.

That solves one problem:

> God does not create an evil substance.

But it creates another:

> Why does God create a world containing so many defects?

That's the genuine problem of evil, and privation theory alone absolutely does not solve it.

It merely clarifies the ontology of evil.

The evidential problem of suffering remains one of the strongest challenges to classical theism.

# 6. Which architecture has fewer primitives?

I think your intuition is basically correct.

The difference is smaller than popular debates make it appear.

Let's write them in their strongest forms.

%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3ENecessary%20simple%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22510%22%20y%3D%2288%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3Epersonal%20reality%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M510%2098%20V118%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22395%22%20y%3D%22118%22%20width%3D%22230%22%20height%3D%2246%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22510%22%20y%3D%22136%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EIntellect%2C%20modality%2C%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22510%22%20y%3D%22150%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3Efree%20agency%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M510%20164%20V184%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22395%22%20y%3D%22184%22%20width%3D%22230%22%20height%3D%2264%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22510%22%20y%3D%22202%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EContingent%20universes%2C%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22510%22%20y%3D%22216%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eminds%2C%20life%2C%20morality%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22510%22%20y%3D%22230%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eintentionally%20actualized%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

Both are trying to do essentially the same thing:

> Explain everything from one ultimate ontological principle.

So the primitive count is indeed similar.

The disagreement isn't primarily:

> one primitive versus fifty primitives.

It's about the nature of the primitive.

|
Naturalism

|

Theism

|
| --- | --- |
|

Necessary impersonal structure

|

Necessary personal reality

|
|

Modal facts primitive or structural

|

Modal facts grounded in intellect/nature

|
|

Contingency via stochasticity or laws

|

Contingency via free agency

|
|

Mathematics fundamental

|

Mathematics grounded in intellect or divine nature

|
|

Mind emerges or is derivative

|

Mind is fundamental

|

That is a much narrower and more honest contrast.

In other words:

> Theism is not mainly adding another layer; it is proposing a different interpretation of the same ultimate layer.

That's much closer to how sophisticated contemporary philosophers frame the issue.

# 7. Now let's attack the first hard question properly

## Why can't an impersonal necessary reality produce contingent reality?

This is O'Connor's central challenge.

Let's formulate it carefully.

Suppose there is a necessary structure:

□N\Box N□N

and our universe is contingent:

◊W  ∧  ¬□W\Diamond W\;\land\;\neg\Box W◊W∧¬□W

The obvious natural model is:

![](data\:image/svg+xml;charset=utf-8,%3Csvg%20font-family%3D%22-apple-system-body%2C%20ui-sans-serif%2C%20-apple-system%2C%20system-ui%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%26quot%3B%2C%20Helvetica%2C%20%26quot%3BApple%20Color%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20Arial%2C%20sans-serif%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Symbol%26quot%3B%22%20font-weight%3D%22400%22%20data-d-component%3D%22svg%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20style%3D%22color%3Argb\(255%2C%20255%2C%20255\)%22%20viewBox%3D%220%200%20320%20180%22%20width%3D%22100%25%22%20xmlns%3D%22http%3A%2F%2Fwww.w3.org%2F2000%2Fsvg%22%3E%3Crect%20width%3D%22320%22%20height%3D%22180%22%20rx%3D%2218%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20opacity%3D%220.35%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2278%22%20y%3D%2218%22%20width%3D%22164%22%20height%3D%2236%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%2240%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2213%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3ENecessary%20N%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M160%2054%20V78%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2248%22%20y%3D%2278%22%20width%3D%22224%22%20height%3D%2236%22%20rx%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%2293%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3ENecessary%20laws%20including%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%22105%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eirreducible%20stochasticity%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M160%20114%20V138%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2278%22%20y%3D%22138%22%20width%3D%22164%22%20height%3D%2224%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22160%22%20y%3D%22154%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EContingent%20W%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

Nothing appears contradictory.

So what exactly is O'Connor's complaint?

It is not:

> This is logically impossible.

His complaint is subtler.

He argues that if the ultimate reality is entirely impersonal and its complete nature is fixed necessarily, then its causal behavior should be exhausted by that nature.

If everything about NNN is necessary, why is its effect not necessary too?

This is essentially a principle of causal completeness of nature.

An impersonal thing behaves according to what it is.

A magnet attracts iron because of its nature.

A particle decays according to its physical dispositions.

A field evolves according to its laws.

So O'Connor asks:

> Where does the extra contingency come from?

The naturalist says:

> From irreducible probabilistic dispositions.

O'Connor says:

> Then contingency is built into the fundamental nature itself—but now we need an account of what it means for a necessary nature to contain genuinely open alternatives.

This is where the argument lives.

Not in a contradiction.

In a theory of how necessity and contingency coexist.

# 8. Why does agency supposedly do better?

Here is the strongest reconstruction of O'Connor.

Imagine two different explanatory relations.

### Impersonal causation

A complete state plus laws yields an outcome.

S+L→ES+L\rightarrow ES+L→E

Even if stochastic:

S+L→{E1,E2,E3}S+L\rightarrow\{E_1,E_2,E_3\}S+L→{E1,E2,E3}

the causal powers belong to the state and laws.

### Personal causation

An agent possesses reasons but is not mechanically determined by them.

Reasons⇝Deliberation⇝Choice\text{Reasons}\leadsto\text{Deliberation}\leadsto\text{Choice}Reasons⇝Deliberation⇝Choice

The reasons explain the action without necessitating it.

This is exactly what agent-causal libertarians claim about ourselves.

The same reasons could have existed while another action remained genuinely possible.

O'Connor generalizes that metaphysical architecture to God.

%2F%20dispositions%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M140%20126%20V142%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2240%22%20y%3D%22142%22%20width%3D%22200%22%20height%3D%2240%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22140%22%20y%3D%22158%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EOne%20outcome%20or%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22140%22%20y%3D%22171%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Estochastic%20outcome%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22320%22%20y%3D%2242%22%20width%3D%22200%22%20height%3D%2234%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22420%22%20y%3D%2263%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3ENecessary%20agent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M420%2076%20V92%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22320%22%20y%3D%2292%22%20width%3D%22200%22%20height%3D%2234%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22420%22%20y%3D%22113%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EReasons%20%26amp%3B%20purposes%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M420%20126%20V142%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22320%22%20y%3D%22142%22%20width%3D%22200%22%20height%3D%2240%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22420%22%20y%3D%22158%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EFree%20intentional%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22420%22%20y%3D%22171%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eactualization%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

The philosophical advantage he claims is this:

> Agency allows reasons without necessitation.

A reason can genuinely explain an action while leaving alternatives open.

For example:

> I went hiking because I enjoy mountains.

That explains my action.

But it doesn't entail that hiking was unavoidable.

The explanation is teleological, not merely mechanical.

O'Connor thinks creation is better understood in that category.

The world exists for reasons, not because an impersonal mechanism mechanically emitted it.

The contemporary reconstruction of his argument explicitly characterizes the necessary being as a purposive agent whose activity is guided but not determined by desires, intentions and beliefs.

![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://plato.stanford.edu\&sz=32)

Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford+1

# 9. But here's the strongest objection: doesn't that merely relocate arbitrariness?

Yes.

This is probably the deepest criticism.

Suppose God had equally good reasons to create either:

* universe A,

* universe B.

Then why A?

If the answer is:

> God freely chose A.

The naturalist can ask:

> What explains that choice?

Now we're dangerously close to brute fact again.

O'Connor's response is not that every divine choice is determined by prior reasons.

That would destroy freedom.

Instead he appeals to agent causation:

> The agent herself is the ultimate source.

But now Oppy's criticism becomes:

> You've introduced an unexplained personal act instead of an unexplained natural event.

What has improved?

This is not a trivial objection.

It is the central issue.

I would formulate the dispute like this:

Naturalist challenge

An irreducibly stochastic law is a perfectly acceptable ultimate primitive. Why is an unexplained free act any less primitive?

O'Connor's reply

A free act is not arbitrary randomness because it is intelligible through reasons, purposes and rational agency even though those reasons do not necessitate one unique outcome.

So the debate becomes:

> Can reasons explain without determining?

If yes, agency offers a distinctive explanatory category.

If no, O'Connor's bridge from necessary ground to God weakens considerably.

This is exactly why his free-will metaphysics and his cosmological argument are deeply connected. His work on agent causation is not an unrelated hobby; it supplies the metaphysical model used in the creation argument.

![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://academic.oup.com\&sz=32)

Oxford Academic+1

# 10. Now let's compare the two architectures more carefully

I think your intuition is right:

> They're much closer than people often admit.

The real difference is not the number of entities.

It's the explanatory primitives.

Let's compare them using exactly the criteria philosophers normally use.

|
Criterion

|

Structural naturalism

|

Classical theism

|
| --- | --- | --- |
|

Ultimate primitive

|

Necessary or brute impersonal reality

|

Necessary simple personal reality

|
|

Mathematics

|

Fundamental structure

|

Grounded in necessary intellect/nature

|
|

Modality

|

Primitive or structural feature

|

Grounded in divine nature/intellect

|
|

Contingency

|

Stochastic laws or contingent brute facts

|

Free actualization by an agent

|
|

Consciousness

|

Emergent, reductive or fundamental depending version

|

Derivative finite minds from fundamental Mind

|
|

Normativity

|

Separate realist/constructivist theory often needed

|

Potentially unified with divine goodness/rationality

|
|

Parsimony

|

Strong prima facie

|

Depends whether divine simplicity genuinely reduces complexity

|
|

Explanatory unity

|

High

|

High

|

Notice something surprising.

Both are monistic at the ultimate level.

They each want one fundamental explanatory principle.

So you're right that the primitive count is not massively different.

The deepest disagreement is qualitative:

> Is the ultimate primitive better understood as structure or as intellect?

Not:

> One thing versus many things.

That is much more precise.

# 11. Which actually has fewer primitives?

This is subtle because “primitive” can mean different things.

Suppose the naturalist says:

> One primitive: necessary mathematical structure.

Suppose the theist says:

> One primitive: God.

At first glance they're tied.

But Oppy would say:

> God's one primitive is internally richer because it includes consciousness, intentionality, rationality, freedom, knowledge and power.

The theist responds:

> Classical divine simplicity denies that these are separate components. They are different conceptual descriptions of one absolutely simple reality.

This is one of the strangest doctrines in classical theism.

It says, roughly:

> God's intellect isn't one part, goodness another, will another, power another.

Rather these distinctions exist primarily in our finite understanding.

Metaphysically there is one simple actuality.

Whether that genuinely reduces ontological complexity is heavily disputed.

But that's the actual argument—not merely counting nouns.

# 12. Let's now continue the epistemological arguments

Earlier we listed several sources of evidence for theism.

I want to distinguish them according to how they enter the argument.

## Metaphysical evidence

This includes:

* contingency,

* modality,

* grounding,

* necessary existence,

* causal dependence.

These attempt to infer God from the structure of reality itself.

This is where O'Connor belongs.

## Mathematical evidence

Not:

> Mathematics proves God.

Rather:

> Mathematical necessity and applicability require some ontology.

Competing explanations include:

* Platonism,

* structuralism,

* nominalism,

* theistic conceptualism.

Theism becomes attractive only if one independently thinks intellect is a better ground of necessary abstract truths than impersonal abstracta.

That's a genuine philosophical comparison rather than a scientific result.

## Consciousness

Here the argument becomes:

> We know consciousness exists directly.

Indeed, first-person experience is arguably one of the most certain things we know.

The question is:

> Can fundamentally non-conscious reality generate consciousness?

Physicalists say yes.

Property dualists say perhaps not.

Idealists say consciousness is fundamental.

Theists say finite consciousness is unsurprising if ultimate reality is conscious.

Again, cumulative evidence—not proof.

## Rationality

This is particularly close to your interest.

Why do our cognitive faculties successfully grasp:

* logic,

* higher mathematics,

* modal truths,

* abstract structures?

A naturalist can invoke evolutionary selection plus cultural refinement.

A theist can argue that rational minds are more expected in a reality grounded in Logos—a rational principle.

Plantinga's evolutionary argument pushes this line much further, though it's controversial.

The important thing is that this is an epistemological argument:

> What worldview better accounts for our confidence in reason itself?

Not a gap in physics.

## Moral realism

Suppose objective normativity exists.

Then:

> Why are there truths about what one ought to do?

Naturalism has sophisticated answers.

Theism has another:

> Objective value is grounded in the nature of ultimate Good.

Again, this becomes evidence only if objective morality is independently plausible.

## Religious experience

This is the one people often dismiss too quickly.

A person has ordinary perceptual experience.

Another has mystical experience.

Philosophers ask:

> Under what conditions is experience prima facie trustworthy?

William Alston famously argued that religious experience may function analogously to other socially established doxastic practices, though naturally it is much more controversial.

The point isn't:

> Therefore every religious claim is true.

The point is:

> Testimony and experience are legitimate epistemic categories, not automatically irrational merely because they aren't laboratory measurements.

So the cumulative theistic case looks more like this:

3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22561%22%20y%3D%22160%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2212%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EReligious%20experience%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22561%22%20y%3D%22176%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Etestimony%20%26amp%3B%20perception%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M119%20110%20L290%20250%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M340%20110%20V250%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M561%20110%20L390%20250%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M119%20200%20L290%20250%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M340%20200%20V250%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M561%20200%20L390%20250%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22210%22%20y%3D%22250%22%20width%3D%22260%22%20height%3D%2278%22%20rx%3D%2212%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22340%22%20y%3D%22272%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2213%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3ENecessary%20personal%20reality%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22340%22%20y%3D%22290%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eas%20inference%20to%20the%20best%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22340%22%20y%3D%22304%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eexplanation%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

The naturalist attacks each arrow individually.

The theist argues the combined explanatory convergence matters.

That is a much stronger version than pretending one cosmological argument settles everything.

# 13. Let's return to goodness one level deeper

You asked a very perceptive question:

> How do you identify the supposedly good characteristic?

Exactly.

This is the Achilles heel of Aristotelian ethics if presented too quickly.

There are two very different senses of “good.”

### Functional good

A knife is good if it cuts well.

A heart is good if it pumps blood.

This is relatively uncontroversial.

### Moral good

A person is good if they are just, courageous, compassionate, honest, etc.

Now the question is:

> Why should functional flourishing generate moral normativity?

The Aristotelian answer is that humans possess a distinctive rational and social nature, so human flourishing includes virtues rather than merely biological success.

Modern critics reply:

> That doesn't obviously yield obligation.

This is one reason Kantian and contractualist theories became so influential.

So even before God enters the picture, the theory of goodness is already deeply contested.

The theist therefore cannot simply say:

> Being = goodness = morality.

That compresses several difficult arguments into one slogan.

A much more careful formulation is:

> Classical theism identifies the fullness of being with ontological goodness, and then develops moral goodness through an Aristotelian account of rational flourishing and natural law. Those are connected doctrines, but not identical propositions.

That's much more defensible.

# 14. Now the deepest comparison: can reasons explain without determining?

This is, I think, the heart of O'Connor.

Imagine I ask:

> Why did Marie become a mathematician?

You answer:

> Because she loved abstract reasoning and found mathematics beautiful.

That's an explanation.

But it doesn't function like Newton's law.

It doesn't imply inevitability.

Another person with identical reasons might choose physics.

So there is a familiar explanatory category:

> Reason explanation.

O'Connor thinks teleological explanation is irreducible.

Not everything is explained by efficient event causation.

Aristotle already distinguished:

* efficient causes,

* formal causes,

* final causes.

Modern science retained mainly efficient causation.

O'Connor wants to restore a genuine metaphysical role for purposive explanation in the ontology of agency.

So creation becomes:

> God creates because of reasons.

Those reasons explain the world.

Yet they do not entail it.

That avoids both deterministic necessity and arbitrary randomness—at least according to the theory.

The objection remains obvious:

> Why did those reasons lead to this particular choice rather than another?

And the agent-causal answer is deliberately radical:

> Because agents themselves are ultimate originators; explanation terminates in the agent, not in a prior determining event.

That is not a scientific claim.

It is a metaphysical thesis about persons.

Whether it's coherent is precisely the debate within contemporary free-will philosophy. O'Connor, Clarke and others defend versions of it, while critics argue that the theory either becomes mysterious or fails to improve upon sophisticated event-causal accounts.

![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://academic.oup.com\&sz=32)
