# 15. Does this actually improve upon randomness?

Here's my own neutral reconstruction.

There are two explanatory ideals.

![](data\:image/svg+xml;charset=utf-8,%3Csvg%20font-family%3D%22-apple-system-body%2C%20ui-sans-serif%2C%20-apple-system%2C%20system-ui%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%26quot%3B%2C%20Helvetica%2C%20%26quot%3BApple%20Color%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20Arial%2C%20sans-serif%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Emoji%26quot%3B%2C%20%26quot%3BSegoe%20UI%20Symbol%26quot%3B%22%20font-weight%3D%22400%22%20data-d-component%3D%22svg%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20style%3D%22color%3Argb\(255%2C%20255%2C%20255\)%22%20viewBox%3D%220%200%20640%20250%22%20width%3D%22100%25%22%20xmlns%3D%22http%3A%2F%2Fwww.w3.org%2F2000%2Fsvg%22%3E%3Crect%20width%3D%22640%22%20height%3D%22250%22%20rx%3D%2218%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20opacity%3D%220.35%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2220%22%20y%3D%2218%22%20width%3D%22290%22%20height%3D%22214%22%20rx%3D%2214%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22165%22%20y%3D%2240%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2215%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3ERandomness%20model%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2245%22%20y%3D%2258%22%20width%3D%22240%22%20height%3D%2236%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22165%22%20y%3D%2280%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3ENecessary%20probabilistic%20law%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M165%2094%20V112%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%2245%22%20y%3D%22112%22%20width%3D%22240%22%20height%3D%2242%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22165%22%20y%3D%22130%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EOne%20contingent%20outcome%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22165%22%20y%3D%22143%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Eoccurs%20without%20intention%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22165%22%20y%3D%22190%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EExplanation%20terminates%20in%20law%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22330%22%20y%3D%2218%22%20width%3D%22290%22%20height%3D%22214%22%20rx%3D%2214%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22475%22%20y%3D%2240%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2215%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%20font-weight%3D%22bold%22%3EAgency%20model%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22355%22%20y%3D%2258%22%20width%3D%22240%22%20height%3D%2236%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22475%22%20y%3D%2280%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3ENecessary%20rational%20agent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Cpath%20d%3D%22M475%2094%20V112%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%20stroke-dasharray%3D%224%204%22%20stroke-linecap%3D%22round%22%2F%3E%3Crect%20x%3D%22355%22%20y%3D%22112%22%20width%3D%22240%22%20height%3D%2242%22%20rx%3D%228%22%20fill%3D%22none%22%20stroke%3D%22currentColor%22%20stroke-width%3D%221.5%22%2F%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22475%22%20y%3D%22130%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2211%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EOne%20contingent%20outcome%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22475%22%20y%3D%22143%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3Echosen%20for%20reasons%3C%2Ftext%3E%3Ctext%20x%3D%22475%22%20y%3D%22190%22%20font-family%3D%22Arial%22%20font-size%3D%2210%22%20fill%3D%22currentColor%22%20text-anchor%3D%22middle%22%3EExplanation%20terminates%20in%20agent%3C%2Ftext%3E%3C%2Fsvg%3E)

Neither eliminates an ultimate primitive.

The difference is what kind of primitive explanation you permit.

The random model says:

> At the bottom there are irreducible stochastic laws.

The agency model says:

> At the bottom there is irreducible rational agency.

So the argument is not:

> Agency removes all mystery.

It is:

> Agency provides a richer kind of explanation because reasons and purposes are intelligible explanatory categories unavailable to randomness.

That is much more modest—and much stronger.

# 16. Where does that leave the sharp dichotomy?

You wanted a deliberately polemical sentence that is nevertheless technically defensible.

I think these are the best versions.

### Polemical version

> Either reality is grounded in a necessary mind, or at some point explanation ends in an impersonal fact that simply is.

That is sharp.

But it's not quite exhaustive because naturalists may say the impersonal fact is necessary rather than brute.

So the technically precise companion should be:

### Precise version

> Every ultimate metaphysics must terminate either in a necessary personal ground capable of rationally actualizing contingent reality, or in an impersonal ultimate structure—brute, necessary, or infinitely regressive—whose modal character is accepted as the final explanatory stopping point.

That captures the genuine disagreement without pretending naturalism has no sophisticated endpoint.

And I think there's an even more O'Connor-specific formulation:

> The deepest dispute is not whether the universe has an explanation, but whether genuine contingency is better grounded by necessary structure or by necessary agency.

That, in my view, is the sentence around which the whole contemporary debate can be organized.

It includes:

* Leibniz,

* Aquinas's dependence arguments,

* O'Connor's modalized cosmological argument,

* Oppy's naturalistic critique,

* agent causation,

* stochastic alternatives,

* and even your original concern about mathematical structure.

## Where I think we should go next

There are now two extremely promising directions.

The first is to reconstruct O'Connor almost as a formal proof, premise by premise, and alongside every premise write Oppy's strongest objection and O'Connor's reply. That would let us see exactly whether the argument really establishes a necessary personal being rather than merely making it plausible.

The second is the epistemological route you asked for: taking consciousness, mathematics, rationality, morality and religious experience and asking, almost in Bayesian fashion, which worldview makes each less surprising, without claiming any of them individually proves God.

Those two projects fit together beautifully because one is about metaphysical grounding, while the other is about evidential confirmation. The first asks what must exist if reality has an ultimate explanation? The second asks which hypothesis best accounts for the full range of things we actually know? Classical natural theology has always contained both strands, even though modern discussions often separate them.

Yes. And I think your latest objection actually sharpens the whole discussion considerably. There are three distinctions we should keep separate:

1. **What follows from the metaphysics of agency?**
2. **What follows from quantum indeterminacy?**
3. **What, if anything, follows from objective goodness/value?**

They can reinforce a theistic picture, but none should simply be smuggled into another.

---

# 1. O'Connor: why prefer the theistic explanation?

This is the right place to return to O'Connor, because I think his argument is more interesting than the simplified "everything needs an explanation" version.

O'Connor explicitly avoids making the **Principle of Sufficient Reason** the starting point. His question is narrower and more metaphysical:

> Given that contingent reality exists, what could constitute its ultimate ground?

His project has two stages: first, argue for a necessary being; second, argue that the necessary being is better understood as a personal/agentive source rather than an impersonal mechanism. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

The important point is that O'Connor isn't saying:

> "I dislike brute facts, therefore God."

His argument is closer to:

> **If contingent reality has an ultimate ground, then the ground cannot itself be contingent; and if that necessary ground produces contingent reality in a way that isn't necessitated by its nature, agency provides a distinctive model of how this could happen.**

That last step is where O'Connor becomes particularly interesting.

## The crucial problem

Suppose we have a necessary entity **N**.

There are two broad possibilities:

### A. Mechanistic necessity

N necessarily exists and necessarily produces universe U.

Then:

**N → necessarily U**

But then U starts looking necessary too.

If the complete nature of N entails U, why is U contingent?

### B. Agentive production

N necessarily exists, but N **chooses** among possible outcomes.

Then:

**N → [U₁, U₂, U₃, ...]**

without:

**N → necessarily U₁**

That gives you a way of getting **contingency from necessity**.

And this is where the agent becomes important.

An agent can have a necessary nature without having every particular choice necessitated by that nature.

That is very close to O'Connor's basic insight.

His proposed necessary being therefore isn't merely "a thing that cannot fail to exist." It is supposed to be an entity capable of willing its acts in accordance with reasons. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

---

# 2. But why is agency preferable to randomness?

This connects directly to your earlier objection about Leibniz.

You said:

> Why couldn't randomness explain the selection?

Exactly. **It could, in principle.**

And this is an important limitation on the cosmological argument.

Imagine:

> Necessary structure N exists → probability distribution P over possible universes → one universe happens.

Nothing is logically incoherent about that.

So the theist cannot simply say:

> "The universe isn't necessary, therefore God."

That's too quick.

The real comparison is:

### Naturalistic model

**Necessary structure + stochastic selection**

versus

### Theistic model

**Necessary agent + rational selection**

And now we can ask which gives us a better theory of the whole phenomenon.

O'Connor's move is essentially that **agency provides an intelligible source of contingent selection that randomness does not**. His model explicitly contrasts a personal "Logos" with an impersonal "Chaos" model. O'Connor then uses fine-tuning as support for preferring the former. ([Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews][1])

But notice the qualification:

**This does not demonstrate that randomness is impossible.**

It argues that agency may be a better *explanatory category*.

And this is exactly where your earlier concern about "adding entities unnecessarily" becomes important.

---

# 3. So where does your "fundamental probability distribution" objection land?

I think your objection is very strong:

> If the probability distribution itself is fundamental, then the probability distribution simply becomes part of the brute fundamental structure.

Correct.

And this exposes an important point:

**Theism doesn't win merely by replacing one unexplained primitive with another.**

If the naturalist says:

> "The fundamental laws and probability structure simply exist."

and the theist says:

> "God simply exists."

then neither has automatically won.

The theist needs an additional argument for why:

**necessary personal agency**

is a better ultimate category than:

**necessary impersonal structure**.

That is why O'Connor's argument is not really finished at "necessary being."

The interesting question is **what sort of necessary being could explain contingent reality?**

---

# 4. This gives us a much better formulation of the dispute

Instead of:

> **Naturalism:** brute fact
> **Theism:** explanation

we should say:

> **Naturalism:** ultimate reality is exhausted by impersonal necessary structure, contingent brute fact, or some combination thereof.

versus:

> **Theism:** ultimate reality includes a necessary agent whose rational agency grounds the contingent order.

Now the disagreement is much more precise.

And neither side gets to declare victory merely by saying "my primitive is simpler."

---

# 5. Your point about ignorance and goodness is excellent

I agree that the previous formulation of the "goodness" argument was too crude.

You said:

> Ignorance is the natural state, and knowledge has to be acquired. So perhaps the good is not knowledge itself but the capacity to acquire it.

Yes. That is exactly the sort of correction we need.

There is a deeper issue here:

**What does "good" mean metaphysically?**

Contemporary philosophy does not have one accepted answer. There are several major families of theories. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

For example:

### 1. Naturalist realism

Goodness is ultimately some natural property.

Perhaps:

> flourishing
> well-being
> preference satisfaction
> functioning
> cooperation
> etc.

This keeps everything within naturalism.

But then comes a familiar question:

> Why is that natural property *good* rather than merely a property that organisms happen to value?

A sophisticated naturalist has answers, but the issue isn't trivial.

---

### 2. Non-naturalist realism

Goodness is real but cannot be reduced to physical/natural properties.

Something like:

> There really are objective normative facts.

But then we have the famous problem:

> What kind of things are these facts?

And how do we know them?

Non-naturalist realism therefore gives you objective value without God—but at the price of accepting a realm of irreducibly normative truths. Contemporary non-naturalism is explicitly understood in roughly this way. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][3])

This is important for our theism/naturalism comparison because it means:

**"Naturalism vs God" isn't the whole debate.**

There is also:

**naturalism vs non-naturalist moral realism vs constructivism vs other positions.**

---

### 3. Constructivism

Your moral truths aren't discovered as independently existing objects.

They are generated/constituted through the requirements of rational agency under suitable conditions.

For example:

> Given what it is to be a rational agent among other rational agents, certain principles follow.

Modern constructivism explicitly tries to retain objective normativity without positing an independent Platonic realm of values. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][4])

This is potentially a very powerful naturalist-friendly response.

---

### 4. Fitting-attitude theories

"Good" can be analyzed in terms of what is **fitting to value, admire, love, desire**, etc.

That gives you an interesting inversion:

> Something isn't good because God happens to approve of it.

Rather:

> God's approval is fitting because the thing is good.

But then you haven't explained the ultimate source of goodness yet.

This is closely related to the old Euthyphro problem, and contemporary value theory contains sophisticated versions of precisely these debates. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

---

# 6. The classical theist move is actually subtler

Classical theism does **not** ideally say:

> "God looks at things and decides that some things are good."

That would make goodness external to God.

The stronger claim is:

> **Goodness is grounded in being itself, and God is the paradigmatic/necessary fullness of being.**

This is the classical metaphysical route.

Very schematically:

**being → actuality → perfection → goodness**

Something is good insofar as it possesses a form of perfection appropriate to its nature.

So:

* a good knife functions well as a knife;
* a good organism flourishes according to its nature;
* a good rational agent exercises rational capacities excellently;
* etc.

Then "good" isn't primarily "whatever God likes."

Rather:

> **God's nature is itself the ultimate actuality/perfection against which derivative goods participate.**

This is very different from divine-command theory.

And it gives the theist a response to your question:

> "How do you identify the good characteristic?"

You identify it through **the nature and actuality of things**.

The question then becomes:

> Why is actuality/perfection intrinsically good?

And that takes us into Aristotle/Aquinas-style metaphysics.

---

# 7. Your objection about evil is also important

You asked previously whether, if actuality is good, evil disappears.

Classical theism has a very specific answer:

**Evil isn't a positive substance competing with goodness.**

It is a **privation**—a lack of some perfection that ought to be present given the nature of the thing.

Blindness is not a second substance opposing sight.

It is the absence of a capacity that an organism of that kind ought to possess.

Likewise, injustice is not a "negative substance."

It is a disorder/lack in the proper relation between rational agents.

This is the classical **privation theory of evil**.

But your earlier objection remains extremely important:

> What tells us that a particular apparent evil is actually an evil in the total order?

Exactly.

Classical theism can distinguish:

**local evil**

from

**ultimate evil**.

A painful event might be bad *qua pain* while contributing to some greater good in the complete order.

That doesn't automatically solve the problem of evil, because one still has to explain why an omnipotent God permits horrendous suffering. But it prevents the simplistic argument:

> "Humans perceive X as bad → therefore God must perceive X as bad in exactly the same respect."

That's not a sophisticated classical-theist position.

---

# 8. And your "ignorance is natural" point actually leads somewhere interesting

Suppose we say:

> Knowledge is good because it is the realization of a rational capacity.

Then ignorance isn't necessarily evil.

An infant's ignorance is perfectly natural.

But **culpable ignorance**, perhaps, is different.

This gives us something like:

> goodness = appropriate realization of a being's capacities

rather than:

> goodness = possessing maximal amounts of some particular property.

That's much closer to Aristotelian-Thomistic metaphysics.

And importantly, it doesn't imply:

> more knowledge = always more good.

Sometimes ignorance can be appropriate; sometimes knowledge can be harmful; sometimes knowledge of something is good but possessing it at a particular moment isn't.

So the theory is fundamentally **teleological and relational to a nature**, rather than a simplistic scalar ranking.

---

# 9. Now let's return to free will — because this is where your intuition about O'Connor gets interesting

You mentioned the possible connection between libertarian political philosophy and metaphysical libertarianism.

They are related **historically/conceptually in some contexts, but they are not the same doctrine**.

In metaphysics:

> **libertarian free will** = genuine freedom is incompatible with determinism.

In political philosophy:

> **libertarianism** = a political doctrine emphasizing individual liberty, property, limited coercion, etc.

They can be psychologically connected through a common conception of the person as an autonomous agent, but neither logically entails the other.

---

# 10. O'Connor's agent-causal theory

This is much more interesting than "quantum particles are random."

The basic distinction is:

### Event causation

> My desire caused my decision.

or more fully:

> neural/psychological event E caused decision D.

### Agent causation

> **I caused D.**

And this is not reducible to:

> some event inside me caused D.

That is a genuinely different causal ontology.

The contemporary literature explicitly distinguishes these two approaches. O'Connor is one of the major modern defenders of agent causation. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][5])

The intuition is:

Suppose you are choosing between A and B.

Your reasons make both intelligible.

Your psychological state does not necessitate either one.

Then:

**A is possible**
**B is possible**

and you, the agent, settle which occurs.

That's not:

> random event → A

nor:

> deterministic psychological state → A.

It is:

> **agent → A**

with reasons influencing but not necessitating the action.

---

# 11. Why quantum mechanics doesn't itself establish this

This is where we should be extremely careful.

Quantum mechanics potentially gives us:

> **not-deterministic physical events.**

But that does not give us:

> **agent causation.**

A radioactive atom randomly decaying isn't exercising free will.

So:

**determinism → no libertarian freedom**

might be a plausible argument.

But:

**indeterminism → libertarian freedom**

is definitely not valid.

The classic problem is:

> If my decision is determined, I don't control it.

But if it is merely random:

> I don't control it either.

That's the **luck problem** for libertarianism. Contemporary work continues to treat this as one of the central problems. ([Springer][6])

The agent-causal theory is precisely an attempt to insert a third category:

> **neither determined nor random: agent-originated.**

That's the important philosophical move.

---

# 12. And there really is current work trying to connect this with physics

Your memory wasn't completely off.

There is contemporary work exploring how agency, indeterminism and quantum mechanics might interact. For example, recent work explicitly explores quantum indeterminism and self-causation, while other work develops emergent versions of agent causation that attempt to make substance causation compatible with contemporary physicalism/emergentism. ([ResearchGate][7])

There is also recent work on free will under particular interpretations of quantum mechanics, including many-worlds. ([Springer][8])

But the important conclusion is:

> **Physics does not currently give us an experimental demonstration of agent causation.**

The philosophical argument is instead:

1. Libertarian freedom requires indeterminacy.
2. Quantum physics gives us serious reasons not to assume classical determinism.
3. But quantum randomness isn't agency.
4. Therefore a libertarian theory still requires an additional metaphysics of agency.
5. Agent causation is one candidate for that additional metaphysics.

That's much more defensible.

---

# 13. And this gives us an interesting bridge to theism

Now notice something.

The cosmological argument and the free-will argument can independently point toward a similar metaphysical category:

> **irreducible agency**

Cosmological argument:

> How can a necessary reality produce a contingent world?

Possible answer:

> **through agency.**

Free-will argument:

> How can an agent make a genuinely undetermined choice?

Possible answer:

> **through agent causation.**

So the same metaphysical concept appears in two places:

**agent causation as a fundamental kind of causation.**

That doesn't prove God.

But it makes the theistic architecture more internally coherent.

---

# 14. And now we can compare the two worldviews more seriously

I would formulate them like this.

### Naturalist architecture

Ultimate reality:

> impersonal physical/mathematical structure

Then we need to explain:

* why that structure exists;
* why it has those laws;
* why there is something rather than nothing;
* why there is normativity, if objective normativity exists;
* why consciousness exists;
* whether genuine agency exists;
* whether rationality is truth-tracking;
* whether moral facts exist.

A naturalist can answer these questions in different ways, including:

> brute fact
> emergence
> reduction
> structural necessity
> naturalistic realism
> constructivism
> epistemic deflationism
> etc.

There is no single "naturalist answer."

---

### Theistic architecture

Ultimate reality:

> necessary personal reality.

Then:

* contingent reality ← grounded by necessary reality;
* rational order ← grounded in divine rationality;
* objective goodness ← grounded in divine nature/being;
* agency ← fundamental rather than reducible;
* contingent creation ← explained through divine agency;
* moral normativity ← connected to objective goodness;
* rational minds ← creatures participating in/reflecting rational order.

But the theist now inherits difficult questions:

* Why does the necessary being have that nature?
* Why does necessary existence belong to God?
* How can a necessary being make contingent choices?
* How does divine knowledge relate to contingent/free events?
* Why this world?
* Why evil?
* How can finite minds know God?
* Why isn't necessary mathematical structure enough?

So theism isn't "problem solved."

It is a **different architecture of primitives**.

---

# 15. Which architecture has fewer primitives?

This is where I think your earlier intuition was right:

**It isn't obvious.**

The naive comparison is:

> Naturalism = universe
> Theism = universe + God

Therefore naturalism is simpler.

But that's not how serious metaphysical simplicity works.

Suppose naturalism requires:

> physical structure + mathematical structure + laws + consciousness + normativity + rationality + agency...

while theism says:

> one necessary rational agent from which these derive.

Then perhaps theism has **fewer fundamental explanatory categories**, despite having an additional entity.

This is exactly the sort of response contemporary defenders of classical theism make to Oppy's simplicity argument: the dispute is not merely about counting entities but about which worldview has the smaller overall metaphysical architecture. ([Cambridge][9])

Oppy's response, however, is serious: he argues that naturalists can reasonably accept brute contingency, infinite regress, or immanent necessity rather than introduce a transcendent necessary being, and he argues that these options can be theoretically preferable. ([OUP Academic][10])

So the real comparison isn't:

> **one thing vs two things**

but:

> **Which set of primitive categories gives the most unified account of the data?**

That's a much harder question.

---

# 16. And this brings us back to your original "faith" point

I think your use of **faith** is defensible if we define it carefully.

There is an enormous difference between:

> "I believe this without evidence."

and:

> "I assent to a proposition whose justification is inferential, experiential, metaphysical, or otherwise not reducible to empirical measurement."

The second is a perfectly respectable epistemic category.

The important question isn't:

> "Is theism faith?"

Of course it can be.

The interesting question is:

> **What is the evidence and what inferential structure connects it to theism?**

And now we can begin to make that completely non-circular.

---

# 17. The epistemological case can be constructed independently

We can divide the possible evidence into several categories:

### A. Metaphysical evidence

Examples:

* contingency;
* necessity;
* causal structure;
* existence itself;
* agency.

### B. Epistemological evidence

Why is the universe intelligible to rational beings?

Why should our cognitive faculties be expected to produce truth?

Why should abstract reasoning connect to external reality?

### C. Normative evidence

If objective moral/value facts exist:

> What kind of ontology best accommodates them?

### D. Consciousness

Why does subjective experience exist at all?

Why does first-person awareness exist rather than merely physical information processing?

### E. Rational agency/free will

If genuine rational agency exists:

> Is it reducible to impersonal physical processes?

### F. Religious/experiential evidence

Personal religious experience, testimony, mystical experience, etc.

This is not "science," but it can still be evidence.

### G. Historical evidence

Specific religious claims can then be evaluated separately.

And importantly, **we don't need G in order to establish that theism is philosophically reasonable.**

---

# 18. But here's the important epistemological symmetry

The naturalist can say:

> "I don't see sufficient evidence for God."

That's perfectly reasonable.

But the theist can respond:

> "I don't see sufficient reason to believe that physical reality is ontologically exhaustive."

That's also a legitimate philosophical position.

And now the dispute becomes:

**Which worldview has the better explanatory and epistemic balance?**

Not:

> science vs religion.

That's a much more sophisticated debate.

---

# 19. I think we're now in a position to formulate your central question much better

Your original sentence:

> **"Either God exists or we can't explain reality."**

is too strong.

Because the naturalist can perfectly legitimately say:

> "Reality ultimately has no further explanation."

And that isn't logically contradictory.

The sharper version would be:

> **"Either reality ultimately rests on an impersonal brute/necessary structure, or its ultimate ground is something beyond impersonal structure—potentially a necessary rational agent."**

And the polemical version could be:

> **"At the bottom of reality, you eventually choose between brute structure and a reason for the structure."**

But even that hides something important: **a necessary structure could itself be the reason.**

So I'd actually propose this as the strongest short formulation:

> ### **"The ultimate question is not whether reality has an explanation, but whether its final ground is impersonal necessity—or necessary rational agency."**

That, I think, captures the real debate we've arrived at.

And it also makes clear why your attraction to O'Connor is philosophically interesting: **his strongest contribution isn't "everything needs an explanation"; it is the attempt to show that once we ask what could ground contingent reality, agency may solve a problem that impersonal necessity and randomness struggle to solve.** That is a much more substantive claim—and one that naturalists can attack without merely saying "I don't like explanations." ([philpapers.org][11])

The next step I'd suggest is to take that exact claim and do the **hardest possible naturalist attack on O'Connor**: assume that brute facts are allowed, allow necessary mathematical structure, allow fundamental probability, allow emergent agency, and see whether the theist still has an argument left. That would tell us much more than simply surveying the two camps.
