Okay, there's another thing I've been thinking of. Maybe now it's moving on a different direction, but regarding this agent who chooses the universe. First of all, there are two things. One is: are you choosing among existing universes only? Or does this agent have the ability to create those universes? And what does it mean to choose or create? Is it just creating the initial conditions from which everything else arises? Or do you have any further control over this universe chosen or created? Because basically that's the jump from agent to God, right? Because you could have been the one choosing the world, but that doesn't mean that you can control what is happening. You can maybe observe, because maybe you cannot even observe. But there are different things that you could potentially do, right? So, from being able to choose or create, to being able to observe, to being able to understand and interpret, because you could be just simply a being that's above it, just like we could see atoms, or we could see other things that we don't necessarily understand or have an influence on. Or even, you know, having any knowledge about what happens in those places. So, I don't know. It's another question, right? So, how do you go from the basic theism of just, okay, there is an agent, someone who has to pick up this universe, even though we don't know if it's just choosing an existing one or creating one, to actually becoming something that could be considered a God and not just a simple, let's say, instrumentalist, like a worker in a factory, let's say, and someone playing games.



Yes. This is exactly the right objection, and it exposes a step that is often compressed too quickly in presentations of the cosmological argument:

> **Necessary agent → creator → God**

Those are **three different claims**, and the first does not automatically entail the second, nor the second the third.

In fact, I would separate at least **seven properties**:

1. **Necessarily existent** — cannot fail to exist.
2. **Agentive** — capable of originating an event/action rather than merely being a prior event.
3. **Creative** — can bring contingent reality into existence rather than merely select from already-existing alternatives.
4. **Cosmically sufficient** — the whole contingent physical order depends on it.
5. **Omnipotent** — can bring about anything metaphysically possible.
6. **Omniscient** — knows everything that can be known.
7. **Perfectly good** — has maximal/complete goodness.

The interesting philosophical work is showing which properties actually follow from which.

---

# 1. First: "choosing a universe" is ambiguous

You're absolutely right.

Suppose there are possible universes:

> U₁, U₂, U₃, ...

and some agent chooses U₂.

There are at least three radically different interpretations.

### Model A — Selector

The universes already exist somehow.

The agent merely selects one:

> {U₁, U₂, U₃} → Agent → U₂

This is **not creation**.

And in fact it creates an immediate problem for classical theism:

> What explains the existence of the space of possible universes?

If the universes already exist independently, the agent isn't ultimate.

---

### Model B — Generator

The agent doesn't find pre-existing universes.

Instead, it has the power to instantiate one:

> Agent → U₂

Here "creation" means something like:

> **bringing a contingent reality into existence without requiring a pre-existing physical substrate.**

That's much closer to classical theism.

But even here, we need to be careful about what "create" means.

---

### Model C — Designer/constructor

The agent constructs a universe out of something already existing:

> Agent + pre-existing substrate → U₂

This is more like a cosmic engineer.

That would be a very different metaphysics.

It might be a **demiurge**, for example, rather than the classical God.

And this is exactly why classical theism makes a much stronger claim than "there is a cosmic engineer."

---

# 2. Classical creation is stronger than setting initial conditions

This is a really important distinction.

Suppose I program a cellular automaton:

> initial state → deterministic evolution → entire history

I haven't created the mathematical rules.

I've specified an initial condition within an already-existing computational structure.

Likewise, suppose some cosmic engineer establishes:

> particles at t=0 + physical laws

and everything follows.

That being is responsible for the **physical history**, but it isn't necessarily responsible for:

* the existence of the laws;
* the mathematical structure;
* spacetime itself;
* the possibility of physical existence;
* the existence of the causal order.

Classical **creatio ex nihilo** is much stronger.

It means, roughly:

> **the contingent order depends for its existence on God, rather than God merely rearranging something that independently exists.**

Aquinas is especially careful about this. Creation isn't understood as a change from one physical state into another. It is the dependence of the entire created order on its ultimate cause.

So:

**Big Bang ≠ creation in the metaphysical sense.**

Even if God caused the Big Bang, that would only be one physical event.

Creation concerns:

> **Why does there exist any physical reality at all?**

---

# 3. And this gives us a very important distinction: cause of existence vs cause of change

Ordinary scientific causation usually looks like:

> A → B

where A and B are events/states inside the universe.

But the cosmological argument is eventually asking about:

> **Why does the entire causal system exist?**

That's a different kind of explanatory relation.

For example:

> domino 1 → domino 2 → domino 3 → ...

is one kind of causal explanation.

But asking:

> Why is there a domino system at all?

is a different question.

And asking:

> Why does anything capable of having dominoes exist?

is another level again.

Classical theists call the latter **causation of being** rather than merely causation of change.

That distinction is absolutely essential to understanding Aquinas and later classical theism.

---

# 4. Now your second point is even more important: control

Suppose we grant:

> Agent A creates Universe U.

What follows?

Almost nothing about A's subsequent relationship to U.

A could:

### 1. Create and disappear

> A → U → A does nothing further.

### 2. Create and observe

> A → U
> A → observes U

### 3. Create and intervene

> A → U
> A → occasionally modifies U

### 4. Create and continuously sustain

> A → U
> A → continually causes U to exist

### 5. Create, sustain and know everything

> A → U
> A sustains U
> A knows every fact about U

### 6. Create, sustain, know and govern

> A → U
> A knows U
> A acts within U according to purposes.

Those are radically different beings.

So you're completely right:

> **"Creator" does not automatically mean "God" in the full classical sense.**

---

# 5. But classical theism has a clever argument here

The classical theist doesn't normally say:

> "We discovered a creator, therefore it must be omniscient."

Instead, they try to derive divine attributes from the **kind of causation involved**.

The reasoning becomes something like:

> If God is the ultimate cause of all contingent being, then God must have certain properties because otherwise God couldn't perform the relevant causal role.

Let's examine them one by one.

---

# 6. Why would the ultimate creator need to be outside the universe?

If the universe is the totality of contingent physical reality, then the ultimate ground cannot simply be another physical object inside it.

Otherwise:

> physical reality → physical object A → ...

and A itself is part of the thing we're trying to explain.

So the proposed ground has to be **ontologically prior to** the physical universe.

Not necessarily temporally prior.

That's another crucial distinction.

Classical theism does **not** require:

> God existed for 10 billion years and then created the universe.

It can instead say:

> God is the reason the universe exists at all, even if the universe has no first temporal moment.

Aquinas actually thought philosophical reason could not establish that the universe had a temporal beginning; creation and temporal beginning are different questions.

That is a surprisingly modern-looking point.

---

# 7. Why omnipotence?

Here the argument is more delicate.

Suppose A creates this universe.

That doesn't mean A can create every possible universe.

Maybe A has limited capabilities.

So:

> **Creator ≠ omnipotent**

in the logical sense.

To get omnipotence, you need another argument.

The classical route is approximately:

If A is the **ultimate ground of all contingent being**, then there cannot be some independent contingent power that exceeds A, because then A wouldn't be the ultimate ground of that power.

But that still doesn't immediately establish:

> "A can do absolutely anything."

Rather, it establishes something more like:

> **There is no independently existing power that limits God's causal power.**

Then one still has to define "anything."

Classical theism therefore qualifies omnipotence:

> God can do everything that is **metaphysically possible**.

God cannot make:

> a square circle

because that's not a possible state of affairs.

That's not a limitation on power; there's simply no coherent thing there to produce.

---

# 8. What about omniscience?

This is even less automatic.

You're absolutely right.

A creator could conceivably be ignorant of some consequences of its creation.

Imagine an extremely sophisticated programmer who creates an artificial universe but doesn't know what every agent inside it will eventually do.

So:

> Creator → omniscient

is not logically immediate.

The classical argument goes through the concept of **perfect being**.

If the ultimate being is absolutely actual/perfect, then lacking knowledge of some knowable truth would constitute an imperfection.

So:

> necessary being
>
> * maximal actuality/perfection
>   → no cognitive deficiency
>   → omniscience.

But notice that we've now introduced an additional metaphysical premise:

> **perfection is relevant to the nature of the ultimate being.**

That's why the route from O'Connor to full classical theism isn't one argument. It's a **chain of arguments**.

---

# 9. And here your earlier question about goodness returns

The same thing happens with goodness.

Suppose we establish:

> necessary creator.

We haven't established:

> morally perfect creator.

We need an argument that:

> the ultimate reality is not merely powerful, but **perfectly good**.

Classical theism gets there through its theory of being and goodness:

> Being → actuality → perfection → goodness.

The ultimate source of all being therefore cannot lack the perfection appropriate to ultimate actuality.

But again, this is not a trivial inference.

It depends on the Aristotelian-Thomistic metaphysics we discussed earlier.

And this is precisely why your instinct to slow down the argument is correct.

---

# 10. There is another fascinating possibility: the cosmic "worker"

Your factory analogy is excellent.

Imagine:

> Ultimate reality = God
> God delegates creation to subordinate being A
> A creates universe.

Then A is a creator but not God.

This is essentially the problem of **intermediary creators**.

And it demonstrates why "creator" isn't enough.

The classical theist needs something like:

> **the being that causes the existence of the entire contingent order cannot itself depend on another contingent creator.**

Otherwise A isn't ultimate.

So you get a hierarchy:

> God
> ↓
> cosmic engineer
> ↓
> universe

The question is:

> Why stop at God?

And the classical answer is:

> Because God isn't merely the first member of the causal hierarchy; God is the **non-contingent ground of the hierarchy itself**.

That is the crucial move.

---

# 11. This is where "necessary" does real work

Earlier you asked why necessity matters.

Here's the payoff.

Suppose:

> A creates U.

But A could fail to exist.

Then:

> Why does A exist?

Perhaps B created A.

Then:

> Why B?

If B is contingent, continue.

Eventually you either get:

### Option 1

An infinite regress of contingent explainers.

### Option 2

A brute contingent fact.

### Option 3

Something necessary.

The classical theist chooses option 3.

And then says:

> **The ultimate creator isn't merely the first creator; it is the necessary ground upon which the existence of every contingent creator and created thing depends.**

Now the "cosmic worker" possibility disappears if the argument succeeds.

Because a worker is contingent.

---

# 12. But here's a subtle point: necessary doesn't automatically mean personal

And we're back to your original concern.

You could say:

> Fine. There is a necessary mathematical structure.

or:

> Fine. There is a necessary physical structure.

or:

> Fine. There is a necessary impersonal metaphysical structure.

So why:

> **necessary agent?**

That's where O'Connor's contribution matters.

The argument isn't simply:

> necessary → God.

It is closer to:

> contingent reality requires an ultimate ground
> → ultimate ground must be necessary
> → impersonal necessary structures seem to make the contingent order necessary if they entail it
> → stochastic structure doesn't explain why this actualization rather than another
> → agent causation provides a way for a necessary ground to produce a contingent outcome without necessitating that outcome.

That's the architecture.

But notice something beautiful:

**This still gets us only to something like a necessary creative agent.**

It does not yet get us all the way to:

> omniscient, omnipotent, perfectly good, providential God.

That is a separate project.

---

# 13. And this gives us a hierarchy of theistic conclusions

I think this is a much better way to think about "God."

### Level 0 — Ultimate reality

Something fundamental exists.

### Level 1 — Necessary reality

Something exists necessarily.

### Level 2 — Necessary ground

That necessary reality grounds contingent reality.

### Level 3 — Necessary agent

The grounding relation involves genuine agency.

### Level 4 — Creator

The agent doesn't merely rearrange pre-existing reality but grounds the existence of contingent reality.

### Level 5 — Omnipotent creator

Its causal power isn't bounded by another independent contingent power.

### Level 6 — Omniscient creator

Its perfection entails complete knowledge.

### Level 7 — Perfectly good creator

Its nature is the ultimate ground of goodness.

### Level 8 — Personal God

The ultimate reality possesses intellect and will and relates to creatures as an agent.

### Level 9 — Providential God

God sustains and governs creation.

### Level 10 — Particular religion

God has revealed specific propositions through history, scripture, prophets, incarnation, etc.

And **every arrow needs an argument.**

That's the discipline I think was missing when we were using "God" too early.

---

# 14. There's an especially interesting question about observation

You asked whether the creator could simply not know what happens.

There's a classical argument against that if we accept the Thomistic framework.

If God is the cause of the being of everything at every moment, then God isn't like an engineer who builds a machine and walks away.

Rather:

> **the existence of every event depends continuously upon the sustaining cause.**

So God's relation to the universe isn't:

> create at t=0 → leave it alone.

It's more like:

> God → existence of U at t₁
> God → existence of U at t₂
> God → existence of U at t₃
> ...

This is called **conservation/sustenance of being**.

That gives classical theism a very different picture from a deistic "cosmic engineer."

---

# 15. And that produces a very strong distinction between deism and classical theism

### Deistic creator

> God starts the universe and lets it run.

### Classical theism

> God is the continuously sufficient ground of the universe's existence.

So classical theism doesn't actually require God to intervene in the laws of physics.

God's relation to the universe is more fundamental than intervention.

This is important because otherwise we would have:

> ordinary physics + occasional supernatural violations.

That's not really the classical conception.

The classical conception is:

> **physics itself describes patterns within a reality whose existence is continuously grounded in God.**

---

# 16. This also answers your "control" question in an unexpected way

There are two meanings of control.

### Interventionist control

> "I can change what happens."

### Ontological dependence

> "The existence of what happens depends on me."

Classical theism is primarily claiming the second.

God doesn't need to constantly reach into the universe and rearrange particles.

Rather:

> **every particle, law, event, field, person and moment exists contingently and therefore depends upon the ultimate cause.**

This is a much stronger claim than "God can control the universe."

And it also explains why classical theism can coexist perfectly well with highly regular physical laws.

---

# 17. But there's still a huge unresolved problem: divine action

And here we reach one of the genuinely difficult questions.

If God continuously grounds everything, what does it mean for God to **act**?

Because if:

> God grounds every event,

then how is God's action different from the ordinary causal relations among physical events?

This is one reason contemporary philosophy of religion has spent so much effort on **divine action**, causation, providence, and God's relation to natural laws.

The sophisticated theist cannot simply say:

> "God is another cause."

God is supposed to be a fundamentally different kind of cause.

That's why classical metaphysics talks about:

> **primary causation**

versus

> **secondary causation**.

Physical causes are secondary causes.

God is the primary cause of their existence and causal efficacy.

So:

> fire heats wood

and

> God causes the existence of fire, wood, their causal powers, and the entire physical order

are not competing explanations at the same causal level.

---

# 18. And this loops beautifully back to your original "science describes but doesn't explain" intuition

Now I think we can state your original insight much more carefully.

Science might tell us:

> "Given these laws and these initial/boundary conditions, this system evolves according to F."

That's an extraordinarily powerful **description**, and because F generates consequences, we call it an **explanation** in ordinary scientific language.

But metaphysics can ask:

> Why is there this law-governed system at all?

And then even deeper:

> Why does anything instantiate a mathematical/causal structure?

And deeper still:

> Is the ultimate structure itself brute, necessary, or grounded in something else?

Science can potentially continue finding deeper physical structures indefinitely.

But **finding a deeper physical structure doesn't logically settle the metaphysical question of why there is any structure at all.**

That's the precise version of your original intuition.

---

# 19. So where are we now?

I think the strongest O'Connor-style argument we've been reconstructing is actually quite modest but interesting:

> **Contingent reality exists.**
>
> If contingent reality has an ultimate ground, that ground cannot itself be contingent.
>
> A necessary impersonal structure has difficulty explaining why a particular contingent reality is actual rather than another, unless the actual reality follows necessarily—which threatens its contingency.
>
> Pure randomness doesn't obviously solve this, because a probability distribution can itself be treated as a brute fundamental fact.
>
> Agent causation provides a third possibility: a necessary agent can possess a necessary nature without being necessitated to produce one particular contingent outcome.
>
> Therefore, **necessary agency is a serious candidate for the ultimate ground of contingent reality.**

That is already philosophically substantial.

But:

> **necessary agency ≠ omniscient God.**

To get there we need additional arguments concerning:

**intellect → knowledge → perfect knowledge**

**will → agency → freedom**

**being → actuality → perfection → goodness**

**ultimate causal power → omnipotence**

**sustaining causation → providence**

And *then* we can ask whether this being corresponds to anything recognizably called "God."

---

## The key insight

I think the most productive way for us to continue is **not** to jump directly from O'Connor to "God."

Instead, we should build a rigorous **attribute ladder**:

> **necessary → ultimate ground → agent → creator → intellect → omniscience → will → omnipotence → goodness → personhood → providence**

and, at every arrow, ask:

**Does this actually follow? What is the argument? What is the strongest naturalist objection? Is the inference deductive, abductive, or merely suggestive?**

That would let us determine exactly **how far the philosophical case gets before we have to introduce specifically theological premises**—which is, I think, precisely the question you've been trying to isolate throughout this conversation.



Until 8 it sounded self contained, but adding perfection in there seemed unjustified. Goodness can also be expanded in relation with what we already mentioned before. O sea yo acepto bastante lo de necessary y me convence lo de agent, y como digo tiene sentido hasta omniscencia sin incluirla todavia (in other words level 6 sounds reasonable to me) but from there I feel it needs more explanation. I agree that the continuous action is an open problem to explore, it can be actually discrete (few actions) or properly continuous, so here it is affect vs sustain



Yes — I think you've identified a genuine weak point in the ladder I gave you. I smuggled in **perfection** as though it were a consequence of being the necessary ground, when it is actually an additional metaphysical thesis. We should not do that.

And I think your distinction between **affecting** and **sustaining** is exactly the right next distinction.

## 1. The ladder should stop at omniscience — or perhaps even before it

I'd revise the previous ladder substantially.

From the O'Connor-style route, we can plausibly get something like:

> **contingent reality → necessary ground → agent → creator → intellect**

and potentially:

> **intellect → knowledge → complete knowledge**

But even the last step needs an argument. We shouldn't say:

> necessary agent therefore omniscient.

Rather, we need to ask whether the particular conception of a necessary agent that grounds contingent reality has implications for what it knows.

For example, suppose:

> A is necessary, agentive, and creates U.

There is nothing contradictory about imagining:

> A doesn't know that a particular event E will happen in U.

So **omniscience is not contained in "agent + necessary + creator" by logic alone.**

There must be an additional argument.

And then, importantly:

> **Perfection should not be the bridge.**

We can investigate perfection later as one possible route, but it cannot simply be inserted as a premise without justification.

---

# 2. I would actually separate three questions

This gives us a much cleaner architecture:

### A. What is required to be the ultimate ground?

This is the O'Connor/cosmological question.

> Why necessary rather than contingent?

> Why agent rather than impersonal structure?

### B. What properties does such an agent necessarily have?

This is where we investigate:

> intellect
> knowledge
> power
> freedom
> goodness

without assuming they all follow.

### C. What additional properties characterize "God"?

Only after doing A and B should we ask:

> At what point does "necessary agent" become sufficiently God-like that calling it God is justified?

That's much more rigorous.

---

# 3. Your distinction between affecting and sustaining is extremely important

Yes: **creation, intervention, and sustenance are different relations.**

Imagine an agent A and universe U.

There are at least four possibilities.

### Creation

> A → U exists

A brings U into existence.

### Initial determination

> A → initial state S₀ → physical evolution

A determines the initial state, after which the universe evolves autonomously.

### Intervention

> A → U
> A → changes event E at t₁
> A → perhaps changes another event at t₂

A occasionally affects events within U.

### Sustenance

> A → U exists at t₁
> A → U exists at t₂
> A → U exists at t₃
> ...

A continuously grounds the existence of U.

And these can be combined in different ways.

---

# 4. The really interesting case is: creation without intervention

Suppose God creates the universe:

> God → U

and then never interferes with anything.

If the laws and initial conditions completely determine everything afterwards, then God doesn't need to intervene.

That's basically a **deistic** picture.

But if God also continuously sustains U:

> God → existence of U at every moment,

God can still refrain completely from **intervening in events**.

This is an important distinction:

> **Sustaining something in existence isn't the same as determining every event that happens within it.**

That's one of the central ideas in classical theism.

---

# 5. And that produces a very interesting causal diagram

Suppose:

> God → existence of universe

while:

> physical cause A → physical event B.

The classical theist says both can be true:

**God → [A exists and has causal power] → B**

and

**A → B**

God's causal relation isn't competing with A's causal relation.

God is not necessarily saying:

> "At 14:03:17, I personally pushed particle X."

Rather:

> "Particle X exists, has its causal powers, and those powers are operative because the entire physical order depends upon me."

This is the **primary/secondary causation** distinction.

And it becomes particularly useful for your original science question.

Physics studies:

> **secondary causal regularities.**

Metaphysics asks whether those regularities themselves depend on something more fundamental.

---

# 6. But your question exposes an important problem

You said:

> it can actually be discrete (few actions) or properly continuous.

Absolutely.

And there are actually three possibilities:

### Discrete divine intervention

God creates U and occasionally modifies it:

> t₀: create
> t₁: intervene
> t₂: intervene
> t₃: nothing

This is the intuitive "God intervenes" model.

### Continuous causal activity

God's causal activity is continuous:

> God sustains U at every moment.

But this doesn't imply continuous *intervention*.

### Timeless dependence

This is the more radical classical-theist position.

God doesn't perform a sequence of actions:

> action₁ → action₂ → action₃.

Instead, God's causal act is somehow **outside temporal succession**, while the created universe exists temporally.

Then "God sustains the universe at every moment" is a way of describing the relationship from the universe's temporal perspective, rather than saying God repeatedly performs little acts.

This is one reason classical theism's conception of God is so different from a cosmic engineer.

---

# 7. And now we hit a genuinely difficult question

Suppose God creates the universe and sets its laws.

Does God then **control** what happens?

Not necessarily.

Imagine:

> God → laws + initial conditions → entire deterministic history.

Then God determines the complete history **counterfactually**, in the sense that choosing different initial conditions would have generated a different history.

But God doesn't need to intervene later.

Compare:

> God chooses U₁.

versus:

> God chooses U₁ and then changes event E.

The second is much stronger.

And:

> God knows E before it happens

is stronger still.

And:

> God knows E and could have prevented it

is stronger again.

And:

> God knows E, could prevent it, but deliberately permits it for some reason

gets us into **providence and the problem of evil**.

So these are genuinely separate theological claims.

---

# 8. There's also a fascinating issue concerning free will

This matters because we've been discussing agent causation.

Suppose humans are genuine agent-causes.

Then perhaps God creates:

> a universe containing autonomous agents.

God might sustain their existence without determining their decisions.

So:

> **God sustains the agent**

doesn't entail:

> **God causes the agent's decision.**

This gives the classical-theist picture a possible distinction between:

**ontological dependence**

and

**determination of action.**

That's potentially very important for reconciling divine creation with libertarian free will.

Of course, it immediately creates the problem:

> If God knows exactly what a free agent will do, how can the action remain genuinely open?

And that takes us into the enormous literature on divine foreknowledge and free will.

---

# 9. I think this also changes how we should think about "God controls the universe"

"Control" is actually a terrible word here because it conflates several things.

We should replace it with at least:

| Relation      | Meaning                                      |
| ------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| **Create**    | Bring contingent reality into existence      |
| **Sustain**   | Ground its continued existence               |
| **Determine** | Make an outcome necessary                    |
| **Intervene** | Alter an otherwise expected course of events |
| **Know**      | Have epistemic access to what occurs         |
| **Govern**    | Intentionally order events toward ends       |
| **Permit**    | Allow an event without preventing it         |

Then we can ask each question separately.

That is much cleaner than saying "God controls everything."

---

# 10. And there's a particularly important consequence for our earlier argument

The O'Connor argument, if successful, does **not** seem to need:

> God intervenes in the universe.

It needs something closer to:

> **The contingent universe has an ultimate agentive ground.**

Whether that ground subsequently:

* intervenes,
* sustains,
* observes,
* knows,
* governs,
* or does nothing further

is a **separate question**.

This is actually philosophically good news for the argument, because it prevents us from loading too many theological conclusions into the initial metaphysical argument.

---

# 11. Where I'd take this next

I think there are now **two particularly deep questions**.

### Question A — From necessary agent to intellect/omniscience

You said level 6 feels reasonable to you. Let's therefore test it properly:

> **Does a necessary agent who is the ultimate ground of contingent reality actually have to possess intellect, and if so, does that intellect have to be omniscient?**

There are some surprisingly strong arguments here that don't require "perfection" as a premise.

In particular, we can investigate whether **knowing the possibilities it can actualize** and **knowing the consequences of its creative act** follows from the kind of agency we're attributing to it.

### Question B — From creator/sustainer to providential God

Separately:

> **What follows from creation and sustenance about God's relationship with the universe?**

Here we can distinguish:

**creation → sustenance → intervention → providence → foreknowledge**

and see exactly where additional premises enter.

And I think we should then tackle the hardest issue:

> **If the necessary agent is genuinely free, why does it choose this particular universe rather than another, and what constrains that choice?**

Because that question connects **O'Connor's agent causation, divine freedom, goodness, mathematical structure, contingency, and the problem of evil** in one place.

That is probably the point where the "necessary agent" hypothesis either starts looking remarkably like classical theism—or we discover that there is a substantial gap between the two.



Justo estaba pensando que se supone que si elige un universo, sea lo que sea esa elección, si lo hace de manera agentica realmente y no por pura probabilidad, que es lo que hemos descartado ya, tendría que tener algún motivo para elegirlo. Es decir, que sería sensato pensar que lo ha elegido porque sabe algo sobre la consecuencia de esa elección. No quiere decir que lo tenga que saber todo, quizá, pero por ejemplo imagínate que como es un agente, pues quiere que ese universo tenga agentes autónomos y ahí entra también todo el tema de free will and so on. So, that case it needs to know that this universe is going to contain autonomous agents. Or let's say that it wants other things to happen. I guess we don't really know what the minimal requirements for God to choose that one, but the thing is that because, or well, we could imagine rather that any possible variation is possible, is contingent, right? So if God, or let's call it this agent, didn't know something or didn't choose something in advance, that would mean some of it would have been random and therefore even though it wouldn't be a full random selection, it would still be random among a specific subset of possible universes. So that means that everything that could have been determined or known in advance would have had to be known for the agent to choose entirely and not randomly. Assuming that any randomized aspect would imply some of this non-determinacy that we have avoided as unnecessary. I mean, the agent itself could create the probability distribution. I don't know if that solves anything in here, but I guess that's one way to go. And I would be interested in explaining or exploring further all those different possibilities of intervention that you mentioned, the ways in which the agent could interact with the universe, basically. Because we also separated, distinguished two kinds: the ones that are atemporal, let's say, it's just ontologically prior. The universe, it just sets the existence of the universe, versus those that are temporal in the sense that are within the universe. If they are within the universe, they need to be temporal also. They need to be located, even if it's multiple locations or multiple times. But there needs to be a notion of time and space because they are in the universe. There could be other notions that we don't know because there are dimensions that we don't know or things like that. But at least they have some component in what the universe contains, right? So it would be interesting to develop those further, all those different kinds of interventions that you describe in order to split the word control into the actual components.



Sí. Y creo que aquí estás encontrando una consecuencia bastante interesante de la idea de **causación agéntica**: si queremos que la elección del agente sea realmente una elección y no simplemente una etiqueta para una selección probabilística, entonces parece natural introducir **razones, fines y conocimiento de posibilidades/consecuencias**.

Pero hay que tener cuidado con un salto: **actuar por una razón no implica conocer exhaustivamente todas las consecuencias**. Podemos hacer una elección genuinamente agéntica sabiendo algunas consecuencias, ignorando otras y dejando otras indeterminadas. La cuestión es qué cantidad de conocimiento necesita el agente para que *la elección del universo* sea completamente suya.

Y esto nos lleva a una distinción que creo que nos va a ordenar muchísimo la discusión.

---

# 1. Elección agéntica no significa determinación completa

Imagina que el agente tiene ante sí:

> U₁, U₂, U₃, ...

y quiere que el universo contenga agentes autónomos.

Podría saber:

> U₁ → contiene agentes autónomos
> U₂ → no contiene agentes autónomos
> U₃ → no sé si los contiene

Entonces puede elegir U₁ por una razón:

> **"Quiero un universo con agentes autónomos."**

Eso es claramente una acción agéntica.

Pero no necesita conocer absolutamente todo sobre U₁.

Por ejemplo, podría no saber:

> qué harán esos agentes dentro de 10¹⁰ años.

Así que:

**elección racional ≠ omnisciencia.**

Eso es importante porque permite que tu intuición avance hacia omnisciencia sin hacerla circular.

---

# 2. Pero tu argumento sobre el azar es más interesante

Estás diciendo algo aproximadamente así:

> Si el agente quiere seleccionar U por razones, pero no determina qué características tendrá U, entonces esas características quedan abiertas. Si no están determinadas por su decisión ni por las leyes necesarias, ¿qué las determina?

Y si la respuesta es:

> "simplemente ocurre probabilísticamente",

entonces volvemos a introducir el elemento que precisamente queríamos evitar.

Por ejemplo:

> Agente quiere un universo con agentes autónomos.

Pero después:

> P(agentes autónomos | elección del agente) = 0.4

y sale que sí.

Entonces tenemos:

> **agencia + azar**

y no:

> **agencia como explicación completa de la contingencia.**

Eso no es necesariamente incoherente. Pero sí significa que **la agencia ya no explica toda la selección**.

---

# 3. Y aquí aparece una distinción fundamental

Hay tres posibilidades:

### Determinación

> El agente elige U → necesariamente ocurre C.

### Agencia con conocimiento

> El agente elige U porque sabe que U → C.

### Agencia + azar

> El agente elige U → C tiene probabilidad p.

La segunda es probablemente la más interesante para nuestro argumento.

Porque permite:

> **contingencia sin azar fundamental.**

El agente puede tener una razón suficiente para elegir U sin que la elección sea físicamente necesaria.

Esto es precisamente lo que hace atractiva la causación agéntica para O'Connor.

---

# 4. Pero no hace falta que el agente determine todos los detalles

Aquí corregiría ligeramente tu intuición.

Supongamos que el agente quiere:

> "un universo que contenga seres racionales libres."

No necesita decidir:

> cada estrella
> cada planeta
> cada átomo
> cada decisión humana
> cada fotón

Podría seleccionar unas condiciones suficientemente generales para conseguir ese objetivo.

Por ejemplo:

> **A → leyes L + condiciones iniciales I**

y de ahí emerge:

> galaxias → estrellas → química → vida → agentes.

Entonces la agencia está en:

> **seleccionar la estructura que realiza un determinado objetivo**

y no necesariamente en:

> **determinar cada acontecimiento de la historia.**

Esto es exactamente análogo a algo que hacemos nosotros.

---

# 5. Y entonces podemos separar cuatro niveles de "control"

Creo que aquí está la taxonomía que estabas buscando.

## I. Control ontológico

El agente hace que U **exista**.

> A → U

No implica que determine ningún acontecimiento particular dentro de U.

---

## II. Control estructural

El agente determina:

> leyes
> constantes
> condiciones iniciales
> estructura matemática
> dimensiones, etc.

Entonces:

> A → estructura de U → evolución de U

Esto permite una enorme cantidad de predicción sin intervención posterior.

Es probablemente lo más parecido al "diseñador" de un universo.

---

## III. Control causal local

El agente interviene en acontecimientos particulares:

> A → E₁
> A → E₂
> A → E₃

Por ejemplo:

> alterar una trayectoria
> producir un evento físico
> impedir otro.

Esto ya es lo que normalmente imaginamos como **milagro/intervención**.

---

## IV. Control teleológico

El agente no solamente produce acontecimientos, sino que los organiza **hacia fines**.

Por ejemplo:

> A quiere que exista vida racional
> A crea/sostiene condiciones que permiten vida racional
> A actúa posteriormente de manera orientada a ese fin.

Esto es ya mucho más cercano a **providencia**.

---

# 6. Y podemos cruzar esto con temporalidad

Aquí tu distinción anterior es excelente.

Hay dos relaciones fundamentalmente diferentes:

### Relación ontológica

> A → U existe.

No es necesariamente un acontecimiento *dentro* del tiempo de U.

Si el tiempo comienza con U, entonces no tiene sentido decir:

> "A ocurrió 5 segundos antes."

Es simplemente:

> **A es ontológicamente anterior a U.**

---

### Intervención temporal

> A → E en t₁.

Ahora sí tenemos:

> universo
> tiempo
> acontecimiento
> localización.

Si el agente actúa *dentro* del universo, su acción debe estar relacionada de alguna manera con la estructura espacio-temporal del universo.

Y aquí aparecen problemas filosóficos muy interesantes.

---

# 7. Podemos construir una matriz bastante bonita

| Tipo de relación             |                                     ¿Temporal? |               ¿Produce existencia? |     ¿Determina eventos? |
| ---------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------: | ---------------------------------: | ----------------------: |
| **Creación**                 |                              No necesariamente |                                 Sí |       No necesariamente |
| **Sustentación**             |                              No necesariamente | Sí, continuamente/dependientemente |       No necesariamente |
| **Diseño estructural**       |                              No necesariamente |                  Sí/indirectamente |            Parcialmente |
| **Intervención**             |                                             Sí |                  No necesariamente |          Sí, localmente |
| **Providencia**              |                        Puede involucrar tiempo |                  No necesariamente | Orientación hacia fines |
| **Observación/conocimiento** | Puede ser atemporal o temporal según el modelo |                                 No |                      No |
| **Determinación total**      |                            Puede ser indirecta |                  No necesariamente |                      Sí |

Y esto nos permite dejar de hablar de "control" como una sola propiedad.

---

# 8. Sustentar no es intervenir

Esto es probablemente la distinción más importante.

Supongamos:

> Dios crea U.

y después:

> U evoluciona perfectamente según sus leyes.

El teísta clásico puede decir:

> Dios está causando que U exista en cada momento.

Pero eso no significa:

> Dios está interviniendo en cada momento.

La diferencia es:

**sustentar:**

> "sin mí, este sistema no existiría."

**intervenir:**

> "sin mi acción adicional, este acontecimiento concreto habría sido diferente."

Son cosas completamente distintas.

---

# 9. Y esto permite incluso un universo completamente determinista

Imagina:

> Dios → leyes + condiciones iniciales → historia completa.

Supongamos que las leyes son deterministas.

Entonces, una vez seleccionados L e I:

> todo el futuro está fijado.

Dios no necesita volver a intervenir.

Esto sería compatible con:

* creación;
* sustentación;
* conocimiento de la historia;
* diseño;
* providencia, dependiendo de cómo definamos esta última.

Pero sería incompatible con ciertas versiones de **libre albedrío libertario**.

Así que aquí se empiezan a cruzar nuestras dos investigaciones:

> **metafísica de la creación**

y

> **metafísica de la libertad.**

---

# 10. Ahora cambia el panorama si existen agentes libres dentro del universo

Supongamos que Dios quiere:

> agentes genuinamente autónomos.

Entonces podría querer crear un universo donde:

> las leyes físicas → permiten agentes
> pero no → determinan completamente sus decisiones.

Podríamos tener:

> Dios → universo → agente humano → decisión.

Y la decisión podría ser:

> **causada por el agente**, no simplemente por el estado físico anterior.

Esto permitiría algo bastante interesante:

> Dios determina el espacio de posibilidades sin determinar necesariamente qué posibilidad actualiza cada agente.

Eso sería una forma de **control estructural sin control determinista local**.

Y creo que esto encaja muy bien con la intuición que estás desarrollando.

---

# 11. Pero entonces aparece la cuestión del conocimiento divino

Supongamos que Dios quiere crear un universo con agentes libres.

Tiene que saber al menos:

> qué tipo de universo permite agentes libres.

Pero quizá no necesita saber:

> qué va a elegir cada agente.

Eso abre varias concepciones:

### Open theism

Dios conoce perfectamente todo lo que puede conocerse, pero las decisiones libertarias futuras todavía no constituyen hechos determinados que puedan conocerse como hechos.

### Molinismo

Dios conoce, además de las posibilidades, qué haría libremente cada agente en determinadas circunstancias.

Entonces:

> Dios conoce contrafácticos de libertad.

### Teísmo clásico

Dios conoce los acontecimientos futuros de una manera atemporal, sin que su conocimiento los determine causalmente.

Son tres soluciones muy diferentes al mismo problema.

Y aquí sí podemos volver a nuestra pregunta anterior:

> **¿La omnisciencia realmente sigue de la necesidad + agencia?**

Probablemente no de manera deductiva.

Pero podemos ahora formular una versión mucho más interesante:

> **Si un agente racional selecciona un universo por razones y con fines, ¿qué grado de conocimiento de las posibilidades y consecuencias requiere la racionalidad de esa elección?**

Eso sí merece un argumento propio.

---

# 12. Y tu idea del "agente que crea la distribución de probabilidad" es muy útil

Supongamos que Dios no determina exactamente qué sucede, sino que crea:

> **P(U | L)**

una distribución de probabilidad.

Entonces Dios controla:

> el espacio de posibilidades + sus probabilidades.

Pero deja:

> la realización concreta

al azar.

Esto produce un modelo híbrido:

> **agencia → estructura probabilística → azar → universo concreto.**

Es perfectamente coherente.

Pero entonces tenemos que preguntar:

> ¿Por qué elegir precisamente esa distribución?

Si la respuesta es:

> "porque el agente quiso esa distribución",

bien.

Pero entonces volvemos a preguntar:

> ¿por qué quiso esa distribución?

Y si finalmente llegamos a:

> "porque sí",

tenemos un hecho bruto **dentro de la propia agencia divina**.

Eso no necesariamente destruye el teísmo, pero muestra que **la agencia no elimina automáticamente toda arbitrariedad**.

---

# 13. Y aquí hay una distinción que creo que será crucial para nuestro siguiente paso

Hay dos sentidos de "explicación".

### Explicación causal

> A hizo que B ocurriera.

### Explicación racional

> A hizo B **por la razón R**.

Cuando O'Connor introduce agencia, está intentando conseguir algo parecido a la segunda.

Por ejemplo:

> ¿Por qué U y no U'?

Respuesta:

> Porque el agente tenía razones para preferir U.

Eso es muy diferente de:

> P(U) = 0.01 y simplemente salió U.

Pero entonces aparece inmediatamente:

> **¿Por qué R?**

Y ahí entramos directamente en **la teoría del bien, los fines y las razones para la acción**.

Eso conecta perfectamente con nuestra conversación anterior sobre goodness.

---

# 14. Y ahora veo una posible estructura mucho más profunda de todo nuestro proyecto

Hasta ahora estábamos preguntando:

> **¿Por qué existe este universo?**

Pero si aceptamos provisionalmente un agente necesario, la pregunta se transforma:

> **¿Por qué este agente elige/actualiza esta realidad?**

Y entonces aparecen cuatro niveles:

### 1. Ontología

> ¿Por qué existe algo?

### 2. Modalidad

> ¿Qué podría haber existido?

### 3. Agencia

> ¿Quién/qué actualiza una posibilidad?

### 4. Razones

> ¿Por qué se actualiza precisamente esa posibilidad?

Y aquí es donde **goodness** puede entrar de una manera mucho más natural que antes.

No necesitamos decir:

> "Dios es perfecto, por tanto es bueno."

Podemos preguntar:

> **Si la realidad última es un agente racional, ¿qué hace que una elección de ese agente sea racional?**

Eso lleva directamente a:

> razones → fines → bienes → valores → racionalidad de la elección.

Y solamente después podemos preguntar si esos bienes están objetivamente fundamentados en la naturaleza del agente.

---

# 15. Esto también cambia nuestra pregunta sobre "Dios"

Quizá no deberíamos intentar demostrar:

> **necessary agent = God**

de una sola vez.

Podemos construir algo como:

> **Necessary reality**
> ↓
> **Ultimate ground**
> ↓
> **Agent**
> ↓
> **Rational agent**
> ↓
> **Agent capable of selecting among contingent possibilities**
> ↓
> **Agent with reasons for selection**
> ↓
> **Knowledge of relevant possibilities/consequences**
> ↓
> **Intellect**
> ↓
> eventualmente **omniscience**
> ↓
> y por otro camino **goodness**
> ↓
> **omnipotence / providence / etc.**

Y cada flecha tiene que ganarse.

Eso es mucho más convincente que introducir "perfección" arbitrariamente en el medio.

---

## Y hay una consecuencia bastante interesante de tu intuición

Si aceptamos provisionalmente:

> **la contingencia no debe terminar simplemente en azar cuando tenemos una alternativa agéntica disponible,**

entonces la pregunta ya no es solamente:

> "¿Por qué existe el universo?"

sino:

> **"¿Qué razones podrían tener las que hacen que una posibilidad contingente sea actualizada?"**

Y ahí, por primera vez, la teoría del **bien** deja de parecer un añadido arbitrario para convertirse en una pieza potencialmente necesaria de la explicación: **si la realidad última es racional y agéntica, quizá necesitemos una teoría de qué constituye una buena razón para que un agente elija una posibilidad en lugar de otra.**

Pero eso también abre la objeción naturalista más fuerte:

> **¿Por qué necesitamos que las razones sean objetivas? ¿No puede un agente simplemente tener preferencias, fines o disposiciones que expliquen su elección?**

Esa es, creo, la siguiente batalla filosófica realmente interesante: **razones para la acción → valores objetivos → goodness**, sin introducir "perfección" por decreto.

Again, you are still right saying that that doesn't mean that I need to know every possible consequence, because if the minimal set is precisely the laws of physics, you don't need to know everything else. Yes — now I understand the probability point, and it is substantially stronger than the way I reconstructed it.

You are **not** saying that the agent randomly decides whether a universe has property \(P\). You're asking:

> **If the agent chooses a universe for a reason, what makes the chosen universe *this particular universe* rather than another universe that satisfies the same reason?**

And that produces a very interesting problem about **individuation of possible worlds, sufficient reasons, and the granularity of divine choice**.

Let's rebuild the argument from there, and then continue the ladder beyond omniscience.

---

# 1. Your argument about uniqueness

Suppose the agent wants:

> "a universe containing autonomous rational agents."

That doesn't identify our universe.

There could be:

$$
U_1,U_2,U_3,\ldots
$$

all containing autonomous rational agents.

So the reason:

> \(R =\) "contains autonomous rational agents"

doesn't explain why **this** universe was actualized.

We could add another property:

> \(R_2 =\) "contains autonomous rational agents + electron charge = X."

Still, probably many possible universes satisfy that.

Add another:

> \(R_3 = R_2 + \text{gravitational constant}=G.\)

Still potentially many.

Continue until we get a set of properties \(S\) such that:

$$
S \Rightarrow U_{\text{actual}}
$$

and no other possible universe satisfies \(S\).

That's essentially your **generating set** idea.

And if the fundamental laws/constants determine the rest of the universe, then perhaps:

$$
\{\text{fundamental laws + constants + boundary conditions}\}
$$

is enough to identify the universe.

That's a much sharper formulation.

---

# 2. But there's an even deeper point

You said:

> any property of the universe can conceivably be altered uniquely.

Yes.

Suppose we alter some apparently tiny fact:

> electron charge \(q\rightarrow q'\).

Then the universe changes.

And because physical systems are interconnected, that potentially propagates into:

> atoms → chemistry → stars → biology → minds → history.

So a universe isn't necessarily something like a finite list of independent Lego blocks.

It can instead be generated from a relatively small **basis**.

That's why your idea of a generating set is so interesting.

Mathematically, we'd want something like:

> **a minimal specification from which the complete physical structure follows.**

Call it \(G\).

Then:

$$
G \longrightarrow U.
$$

The agent doesn't need to select every fact individually.

It can select \(G\), and the rest follows.

---

# 3. And this gives us three different meanings of "choosing a universe"

This distinction is essential.

### Choice of complete history

The agent selects:

$$
U = \{\text{every fact throughout spacetime}\}.
$$

This requires enormous specification.

### Choice of generating structure

The agent selects:

$$
G=\{\text{laws, constants, boundary conditions, etc.}\}
$$

and the rest follows.

### Choice of principle

The agent chooses something even more abstract:

> "I want a universe satisfying principle P."

Then perhaps \(P\) determines \(G\), or perhaps several \(G\)'s satisfy \(P\).

Those are very different notions of divine choice.

---

# 4. And now your probability objection becomes precise

Suppose:

$$
P \rightarrow G_1,G_2,G_3,\ldots
$$

and the agent says:

> "I want a universe satisfying P."

But there are several possible \(G\)'s.

Then **P does not uniquely determine the universe**.

If the agent selects one of them without a further reason, there is an unexplained element of selection.

We could represent it as:

$$
P \rightarrow \{G_1,G_2,G_3\}
$$

followed by:

$$
? \rightarrow G_2.
$$

The question is:

> **What explains the arrow from the set to G₂?**

It could be:

1. another reason;
2. a necessary consequence;
3. an arbitrary act of will;
4. objective probability;
5. brute fact.

And this is where your original point about probability comes back.

If we say:

> "The agent chooses \(G_2\) with probability 1/3."

we haven't eliminated the unexplained selection. We've merely put a probability distribution over it.

And then:

> **Why this distribution?**

So yes: **the agent can generate the probability distribution**, but then the distribution itself becomes something whose selection needs explanation if we are pursuing complete explanation.

That doesn't make probability incoherent. It means probability doesn't automatically solve the *ultimate selection* problem.

---

# 5. But here's where I would challenge one part of your argument

You said:

> "For the choice to be entirely non-random, everything that could have been determined or known in advance would have to be known."

I would modify that.

The agent doesn't necessarily have to **know every consequence**.

It needs to have a **sufficient reason for choosing the generating structure**.

For example:

> "I choose laws \(G\) because \(G\) realizes objective end E."

The agent might not know that:

> \(G\) also produces some extremely complicated consequence C.

That doesn't make the choice random.

So the key issue isn't:

> **Does the agent know every consequence?**

It's:

> **Does the agent have a reason sufficient to distinguish \(G\) from the alternatives it could have chosen?**

That's a much stronger and cleaner formulation.

---

# 6. This gives us the real question

We now have:

$$
\text{possible }G_1,G_2,G_3,\ldots
$$

The agent chooses \(G_2\).

Why?

There are only a few possibilities.

### A. Necessity

$$
G_2
$$

is the only possible one.

Then there isn't genuine contingency.

### B. External constraint

Something else forces \(G_2\).

Then the agent isn't ultimate.

### C. Probability

$$
P(G_2)=p.
$$

Then the realization isn't completely explained by agency.

### D. Brute preference

> "I simply chose \(G_2\)."

This is agential, but the reason is opaque.

### E. Reason

> "I chose \(G_2\) because \(G_2\) realizes E."

This is the most philosophically interesting option.

And now we have arrived at **reasons, values and goodness** without having smuggled "perfection" into the argument.

---

# 7. And this connects directly to your "image of God" intuition

Suppose the necessary agent is itself:

> rational + autonomous + agentive.

Then perhaps it values:

> rationality
> agency
> autonomy
> consciousness.

A universe containing beings possessing these characteristics could therefore be intrinsically attractive to such an agent.

That gives us something stronger than:

> "God likes humans because God is good."

It gives:

> **The agent creates conditions under which characteristics analogous to those constitutive of agency itself can emerge.**

And then "created in God's image" becomes philosophically interesting.

Not necessarily:

> humans look like God.

But:

> **rational agency is a participation, analogy or reflection of the structure of the ultimate agent.**

That's a much deeper idea.

And importantly, it doesn't yet require us to accept Christianity or even classical theism.

---

# 8. Now let's return to our ladder

We had reached roughly:

> **Necessary → ultimate ground → agent → creator → intellect → knowledge → potentially omniscience.**

Let's now ask how to move further.

---

# 9. From intellect to omniscience

The weakest claim is:

> The agent knows something.

That follows almost trivially from rational agency.

Then:

> The agent knows the relevant alternatives.

Because otherwise its choice is not fully informed.

Then:

> The agent knows the consequences relevant to its reasons.

Because otherwise it cannot intentionally select \(G\) *for the sake of* E.

But does that yield omniscience?

Not yet.

We need an additional argument.

A promising route is this:

If the agent is the **ultimate ground of every contingent reality**, then every contingent possibility ultimately lies within the scope of its creative power.

If the agent is rational enough to choose among those possibilities, perhaps its intellect must represent the relevant modal structure:

> what could exist
> what cannot exist
> what would follow from what
> what alternatives are compatible with which principles.

That gives something approaching:

> **complete modal knowledge.**

But that's still not necessarily knowledge of every contingent fact.

So we should distinguish:

### Modal omniscience

Knowing every possible state of affairs and their relations.

### Factual omniscience

Knowing every actual fact.

### Counterfactual omniscience

Knowing what would happen under every relevant hypothetical condition.

Those are different.

A sophisticated theory of divine knowledge has to deal with all three.

---

# 10. And here the agent's own creative act becomes relevant

Suppose God chooses:

$$
G
$$

and knows:

$$
G\rightarrow U.
$$

If the relationship is deterministic, then knowing \(G\) and the laws may allow knowledge of the entire resulting universe.

But if there are libertarian agents:

$$
G\rightarrow \text{conditions for agent A}
$$

and:

$$
A\rightsquigarrow E
$$

where the decision is genuinely free, then the future may not be derivable from G alone.

This is where the classical problem of divine foreknowledge becomes genuinely difficult.

---

# 11. Three possibilities emerge

### Classical omniscience

God knows the entire history because God's knowledge isn't temporally predictive.

God doesn't "look into the future."

Rather, all temporal events are immediately present to an atemporal intellect.

### Molinism

God knows:

> "If A were placed in circumstances C, A would freely choose E."

These are called **counterfactuals of creaturely freedom**.

This gives God extraordinarily detailed knowledge of possible histories.

### Open theism

Some future libertarian choices aren't yet determinate facts.

Therefore:

> even omniscience doesn't require knowing an undetermined future as though it were already fixed.

This is actually a very elegant way of preserving libertarian freedom, though it comes at a cost for traditional conceptions of omniscience.

---

# 12. Now omnipotence

Again, we shouldn't simply say:

> "God is necessary, therefore omnipotent."

The better route is:

If the agent is the **ultimate source of contingent reality**, then its causal power cannot depend on another contingent causal power.

But that's still weaker than omnipotence.

To get omnipotence, we need something like:

> **For every metaphysically possible state of affairs P, there is no independent ontological limitation preventing the ultimate ground from actualizing P.**

Then:

$$
\text{ultimate causal ground}
\rightarrow
\text{unbounded causal power}
\rightarrow
\text{omnipotence}.
$$

But again, this requires an argument that there cannot be an independently existing necessary limitation on its power.

And that gets us into the metaphysics of necessity.

---

# 13. And notice something fascinating

Our two attributes are beginning to arise through **different routes**.

### Knowledge

comes from:

> rational agency + reasons + representation of possibilities/consequences.

### Power

comes from:

> ultimate causal dependence of contingent reality.

### Goodness

might come from:

> reasons for action + objective ends + value.

So we don't need:

> "God is perfect, therefore God has everything."

Instead, we can derive each attribute independently.

That's much stronger philosophically.

---

# 14. And this is where goodness becomes particularly interesting

Suppose the agent chooses \(G\).

We ask:

> Why \(G\)?

If the answer is:

> "Because G produces E, which the agent values."

then we need to know:

> Why does the agent value E?

There are two possibilities.

### Subjectivist answer

> The agent simply has preference E.

Then:

> "God chose this universe because God likes it."

That's coherent.

But it gives us no objective morality.

### Objectivist answer

> E is genuinely good.

Now we have objective value.

And then we can ask:

> What makes E good?

This is where we can reconstruct the classical theory of goodness without simply assuming "perfection."

---

# 15. And there's an intriguing possibility here

Perhaps **goodness isn't something added to being from outside.**

Instead:

> A thing is good insofar as it realizes the kinds of actuality appropriate to its nature.

This is the Aristotelian/Thomistic idea of goodness as related to **actualization**.

For example:

> an acorn is good insofar as it realizes the capacities characteristic of an acorn.

For rational beings:

> knowledge, rational activity, agency, friendship, etc.

might constitute genuine goods because they are forms of flourishing appropriate to rational nature.

Then a universe containing rational autonomous beings could be valuable **not merely because God happens to like them**, but because rational agency is objectively a good.

That is a much more serious theory.

But we haven't yet established that this theory is true.

That's exactly the argument we should investigate next.

---

# 16. And this takes us surprisingly close to the God of classical theism

Notice the progression:

> **Necessary ground**
>
> → must be capable of grounding contingent reality
>
> → **agent**
>
> → acts for reasons
>
> → reasons concern possible states of affairs
>
> → requires knowledge of relevant possibilities
>
> → **intellect**
>
> → if knowledge is unrestricted
>
> → **omniscience**
>
> → ultimate causal ground has no independent contingent limitation
>
> → potentially **omnipotence**
>
> → reasons for action raise the question of objective value
>
> → objective value raises the question of **goodness**
>
> → if goodness is grounded in the nature of the ultimate actuality
>
> → **perfect goodness** becomes a possible conclusion rather than an assumption.

That's a much better ladder than my previous one.

But each arrow is now a separate philosophical problem.

---

# 17. And your "affect vs sustain" distinction fits into this perfectly

We can now make the entire divine relationship with reality into separate axes:

### Ontological axis

**Create**

> Why does U exist?

**Sustain**

> Why does U continue to exist?

### Epistemic axis

**Know**

> Does the agent know U?

**Know possibilities**

> Does it know alternative universes?

**Know consequences**

> Does it know what follows from G?

**Know counterfactuals**

> Does it know what free agents would do?

### Causal axis

**Determine**

> Does it fix what happens?

**Intervene**

> Does it alter events inside U?

**Permit**

> Does it allow events it could prevent?

### Teleological axis

**Choose**

> Why G rather than G'?

**Order**

> Does it organize reality toward ends?

**Providence**

> Does it govern the history of U toward those ends?

Those are **independent properties**.

And that means "God controls the universe" is almost useless philosophically unless we specify which of these we mean.

---

# 18. I think we've now uncovered the real philosophical architecture

It isn't:

> **Universe → God.**

It's more like:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Contingent reality}
\rightarrow
\text{necessary ground}
\rightarrow
\text{agent}
\rightarrow
\text{reason}
\rightarrow
\text{intellect}
\rightarrow
\text{knowledge}
}
$$

and independently:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Ultimate causal ground}
\rightarrow
\text{causal sufficiency}
\rightarrow
\text{power}
}
$$

and:

$$
\boxed{
\text{rational choice}
\rightarrow
\text{reasons}
\rightarrow
\text{ends}
\rightarrow
\text{value}
\rightarrow
\text{goodness}
}
$$

Then these three strands potentially converge on something recognizably like **God**.

And crucially, **we haven't yet established that convergence**. We've identified what would have to be shown.

---

## The question I think we should tackle next

There is now one particularly hard problem sitting exactly at the intersection of everything we've discussed:

> **If the necessary agent chooses among genuinely contingent generating structures, what can possibly constitute a sufficient reason for choosing one rather than another?**

There are only a few candidates:

**necessity**, **external constraint**, **arbitrary preference**, **objective value**, or **some deeper principle**.

And this is where I think your intuition about "created in God's image" can be developed into something philosophically serious: **if agency, rationality, consciousness, knowledge, or freedom are themselves goods, perhaps they provide objective reasons for creation.**

But then we have to answer the much harder question:

> **Why are those things good?**

That takes us directly into the modern versions of the Aristotelian/Thomistic theory of goodness, contemporary axiological realism, the grounding of normativity, and the problem of evil — and *there* we can finally test whether "God is good" is an independent theological assertion or whether goodness can actually be derived from the same metaphysical architecture we've been constructing.



Okay, I feel like we're getting into something here now, because precisely we are getting to a good point of how to define omniscience properly. Since you mentioned a good point: if free will exists, and precisely this agent wanted autonomous agents to exist, without free will, they wouldn't truly be autonomous agents. Although here again we are assuming a lot, because we are assuming that the agent wanted that. But we already argued that that's a very reasonable choice. Indeed, like, yeah, why not? We could explore other possibilities, but this seems to be more interesting, probably. Therefore, even if we go to traditional religions and other movements, they believe that humans have free to choose, because we can choose God or we can not choose God, right? And that's precisely the conception of hell. Hell is when you don't choose God. So, rather than a specific place, that's a representation, right? But that's this conception. So it wouldn't make sense to give us choice if we don't have it, right? It would be far less interesting, even if we could argue about it and imagine it, okay? So I would rather explore omniscience on that way, because it also kind of solves this issue of having to know all the possibilities and future events. I mean, even if he knows all the possibilities, you don't need to know which one is going to be realized. And also about goodness, because I think we are going in the right direction of how if that purpose exists, then goodness is what fulfills that purpose, and the reason why evil exists is because in order for those things to exist, there are other consequences in the world, particularly those that we call evil. Although that's another extra layer, more theological maybe, and more grounded to people. So I don't want this to be mixed with the argument that it's something that can inform, after all, the philosophy, which is we sometimes, as humans, need specific ways of getting things, and they are not always the ones that are nicer. For instance, spoiling children doesn't make them better people when they grow up, right? You don't want them to have everything easy. You also want them to struggle. Another argument that Christianity uses sometimes is that in order for people to actually follow God, they had to kill and torture and kill the only innocent human because it was God in human form, right? So if that crime wouldn't have been committed, if that most evil thing wouldn't have happened, then people would not follow God, just because we are humans and we have our incentives. That's just a bit of grounding in human psychology and behavior, because after all, that's a bit of a consequence of being autonomous agents that are rational and explore the world and so on. That's just a bit of human context to make sense of all of this. So it's not the metaphysical point of view. And on top of that, I think the idea of having things that are temporal also solves many issues. Because I think most of the paradoxes arises from us humans being confined into this universe. Right, so we experience time and we struggle to separate things from time. But if a greater agent can observe eternally, observe all the reality at once, that brings another layer of omniscience. It's not knowledge about the future. It's knowledge about every moment, which makes intervention a bit more interesting because, yeah, again, intervention, if it takes place inside, it needs to manifest somehow temporally, and then it modifies the entire universe with all temporal moments. But I think that kind of makes more sense of everything somehow. I can't really put into words right now, but it seems to make sense. One thing that I want to add, by the way, is if at any point we are touching something that is, let's say, unexplored territory, although that's very optimistic, let's say something that hasn't been put together all the time, because for the first time I'm starting to give you my own thoughts and arguments rather than just trying to find explanations of what you tell me, or trying to replicate what others have done, or just review other philosophers. So if at any point any of these arguments seem to be, even if not completely new, just things that haven't been put together before, or that add some layer that is original, let me know, because I would like to extract it and then write it properly and formally in some sort of book or essay or something. With that in mind, we can continue exploring those questions and how we can go further in the line. Like, we don't necessarily need to reach the classical God. Maybe we reach another set of God. But I think at this point it's mostly about exploring, seeing possibilities, and among those possibilities sometimes there will be some that are more interesting. Like I said, the agent that created the universe may have done it for other reasons, okay? But we can consider one of them the interesting one, and then explore it. And it's always something that is possible, and maybe we have good reasons. So obviously choosing good reasons of why this over other reasons, other possibilities, is a plus, and it's something that helps us a lot, and the more we do, the better. But we have to acknowledge that doesn't determine anything. You can always imagine an alternative.




Yes. I think your correction produces a much better version of the argument, and it also reveals that we were conflating **three different questions**:

1. **What does it mean for the agent to choose a universe?**
2. **What must the agent know in order for that choice to be rational/agential?**
3. **What does omniscience mean if some of the universe contains genuinely free agents?**

Those should be separated.

And I agree that the third question may actually give us a more interesting conception of omniscience than simply "knows every future event."

One caveat on originality: some of the pieces we're reaching are well-developed in the literature—especially Boethian atemporality, Molinism, open theism, and agent-causation—but **your particular way of combining the generating-set problem for universe-selection with agentive choice, libertarian agency, and a layered conception of divine knowledge is not something I would label "novel" without doing a much more exhaustive literature search**. I can, however, flag places where your formulation seems especially distinctive or where it appears to be a useful synthesis rather than merely reproducing an established argument.

---

# 1. First, let's formalize your "which universe?" problem

Suppose there is a set of possible universes:

$$
\mathcal U=\{U_1,U_2,U_3,\ldots\}.
$$

The agent chooses \(U^*\).

But the agent doesn't necessarily choose every fact of \(U^*\) individually. There may be some generating specification:

$$
G(U^*)=\{L_1,L_2,\ldots,L_n\}
$$

such that the rest of the physical structure follows from \(G\).

For example, \(G\) might contain:

* fundamental dynamical laws;
* constants;
* symmetry structure;
* dimensionality;
* boundary conditions;
* perhaps some quantum state;
* whatever else is genuinely fundamental.

Then:

$$
G \Rightarrow U^*.
$$

So the agent can select \(G\), rather than selecting the whole gigantic history of the universe.

That is a very important clarification.

---

# 2. Your question is then: what makes \(G\) *the* choice?

Suppose the agent has a reason:

> "I want a universe containing autonomous rational agents."

Call this:

$$
R_1.
$$

But suppose:

$$
G_1,G_2,G_3,\ldots
$$

all satisfy \(R_1\).

Then \(R_1\) does not determine which \(G\) is selected.

So we ask:

> Why \(G_2\) rather than \(G_1\)?

Perhaps there is another reason:

$$
R_2.
$$

But perhaps \(R_2\) is also satisfied by several structures.

Then:

$$
R_1+R_2+R_3+\ldots
$$

until eventually we get a specification that uniquely identifies \(G_2\).

Or perhaps we never do.

That is the interesting problem.

---

# 3. And this gives us a much better definition of an agentive choice

An **agentive choice** need not determine every fact of the resulting world.

Rather, it could mean:

> **The agent intentionally selects one possibility from a set of genuinely available alternatives for reasons that favor that possibility.**

That definition has three components:

### Alternatives

There really are multiple possibilities.

### Intention

The agent selects one rather than merely being randomly assigned one.

### Reason

The selection isn't arbitrary in the sense of being completely unmotivated.

This is stronger than probability but weaker than deterministic specification.

And I think this is exactly the conceptual space you've been looking for.

---

# 4. This also means that "not random" doesn't require omniscience

Suppose I choose a house because:

> "I want a house near the sea."

There may be 10,000 houses satisfying that condition.

I don't have to know every consequence of buying each house.

I just need some reason that makes one option preferable to the others.

So for the cosmic agent:

> "I want a universe containing rational autonomous beings"

doesn't require knowing every event that will ever occur.

But it raises a deeper question:

> **What distinguishes the selected generating structure from all the other structures satisfying that goal?**

And *that* is where knowledge becomes interesting.

---

# 5. There are two ways the agent could distinguish them

### Consequential selection

> "I choose \(G\) because I know \(G\) will produce E."

Then the agent needs knowledge of the relevant consequences.

For example:

> "This set of laws will produce rational embodied agents."

It doesn't need to know everything; it needs to know the consequences relevant to its reason.

### Principle-based selection

The agent could instead say:

> "I choose these laws because these laws themselves instantiate a principle I value."

For example:

> simplicity
> symmetry
> mathematical elegance
> maximal autonomy
> maximal diversity
> rational intelligibility.

Then the agent doesn't need to choose the universe because of what it *eventually produces*.

It can choose the fundamental structure because of an intrinsic feature of that structure.

This is an important alternative, and I think you're right not to assume consequentialism too early.

---

# 6. This gives us an interesting taxonomy of divine reasons

The agent might choose a universe because of:

### 1. Structural reasons

> "These laws have property P."

### 2. Consequential reasons

> "These laws produce E."

### 3. Teleological reasons

> "These laws enable beings to realize F."

### 4. Relational reasons

> "These laws permit relationships between agents."

### 5. Axiological reasons

> "This universe realizes more value."

These aren't equivalent.

And we shouldn't assume the agent uses human-style instrumental reasoning.

---

# 7. Now your autonomous-agent hypothesis becomes particularly interesting

Suppose we tentatively propose:

> **The agent values the existence of other autonomous rational agents.**

That's not yet a theological conclusion.

It's a hypothesis about the agent's reason for creation.

And it's not obviously irrational.

Indeed, there is an interesting structural analogy:

> agent → values agency → creates conditions for agency.

You can formulate a stronger version:

> **A rational agent has reason to value the existence of other centers of rational agency because agency is itself a mode of value-bearing existence.**

That would be a serious philosophical thesis.

But we mustn't quietly assume it.

There are alternatives:

> The agent values consciousness.

> The agent values knowledge.

> The agent values beauty.

> The agent values complexity.

> The agent values freedom.

> The agent values moral development.

> The agent values something utterly unlike anything humans recognize.

So your point is exactly right:

> **The fact that one reason is attractive does not establish that it is the agent's actual reason.**

It merely makes that hypothesis more interesting.

---

# 8. Now we can revisit omniscience in a much better way

I think "omniscience = knowing every future event" is actually too crude for what we're doing.

We should distinguish at least four kinds of divine knowledge.

## I. Necessary knowledge

Knowledge of truths that cannot be false.

For example:

$$
2+2=4.
$$

And perhaps knowledge of all metaphysically necessary truths.

---

## II. Modal knowledge

Knowledge of what is possible and impossible.

For example:

> Could a universe with laws \(G\) exist?

> Could a universe contain libertarian agents?

> Is \(G_1\) compatible with \(G_2\)?

This becomes extremely important for universe-selection.

---

## III. Actual knowledge

Knowledge of what actually occurs:

> U exists.

> Event E occurs at \(t\).

> Agent A freely chooses X.

---

## IV. Counterfactual knowledge

Knowledge of:

> What would happen if...

For example:

> If agent A were placed in circumstances C, A would freely choose X.
