This is the controversial territory of **Molinism / middle knowledge**. ([OUP Academic][1])

And there is a fifth category that may be particularly useful for our project:

## V. Teleological knowledge

Knowledge of:

> what ends are realized by which structures,

and perhaps:

> which structures realize which forms of value.

This isn't normally listed as a separate "type of divine knowledge," but conceptually it is extremely relevant to the argument we're constructing.

---

# 9. And now free will creates an important distinction

Suppose humans genuinely have libertarian freedom.

Then there are facts like:

> "At \(t\), Alice freely chooses X."

If Alice really could choose Y instead, then the fact that she chooses X isn't simply deducible from the prior physical state.

So:

$$
\text{Past physical state} \not\Rightarrow \text{future choice}.
$$

That's precisely what libertarianism means in the relevant sense.

Now ask:

> Does God nevertheless know X in advance?

There are several answers.

---

# 10. The classical/Boethian answer

God isn't predicting the future.

God is not located at:

$$
t_0
$$

looking toward:

$$
t_1,t_2,t_3.
$$

Instead, God's knowledge is **atemporal**.

From God's perspective:

> \(t_0,t_1,t_2,\ldots\)

are all present to the divine intellect.

So God doesn't know:

> "Alice will choose X."

in the sense of making a prediction from an earlier moment.

God knows:

> **Alice's choosing X as an eternal object of knowledge.**

This is close to the Boethian idea.

And I think your intuition here is important:

> **knowledge of every moment is conceptually different from prediction of every future moment.**

That distinction dissolves some apparent paradoxes.

But it doesn't dissolve everything.

---

# 11. Because the hard objection remains

Someone can say:

> If God infallibly knows that Alice chooses X, then Alice cannot choose Y.

The classical response is:

> God's knowledge doesn't cause Alice's choice.

But the critic can reply:

> Fine, but the infallibility of the knowledge still seems to make Y impossible.

This is the famous foreknowledge/freedom problem.

The Boethian response is basically:

> **The necessity belongs to the truth of the proposition, not to the causal determination of the action.**

In other words:

> It is necessarily true that *if God knows X, then X occurs.*

But:

> It doesn't follow that *God causes X.*

Whether that is enough to preserve libertarian freedom is heavily disputed.

---

# 12. And here Molinism becomes fascinating for our project

Molinism introduces:

> **counterfactuals of creaturely freedom.**

God knows:

> "If Alice were in circumstances C, she would freely choose X."

before deciding which world to create.

Then God can select a world based on what free creatures would do in it. ([OUP Academic][1])

This is extraordinarily close to the problem you independently formulated:

> **How can the agent choose a universe without determining all the choices inside it?**

Molinism says:

> By knowing the relevant counterfactual structure of the free agents.

Then the agent chooses the generating structure.

So:

$$
G
\rightarrow
\text{circumstances}
\rightarrow
\text{free actions}
$$

but the free actions aren't causally determined by G.

That is almost tailor-made for the architecture we've been building.

---

# 13. But there is a major problem — and it's exactly the one you would expect

Where do the counterfactuals come from?

Suppose:

> "If Alice were in C, she would freely choose X."

What makes that statement true?

If God makes it true, then it seems that God's will determines Alice's choice.

If Alice makes it true, then something about Alice exists logically prior to her creation.

If nothing makes it true, then we have **brute modal facts**.

This is known as the **grounding problem for counterfactuals of creaturely freedom**. It's one of the central objections to Molinism. ([Enciclopedia Internet de Filosofía][2])

And contemporary work continues to push on exactly this problem; recent criticism has argued, for example, that Molinism may introduce explanatory or luck-related difficulties rather than completely solving them. ([OUP Academic][3])

So this is not a solved escape hatch.

---

# 14. And this gives us a very interesting three-way comparison

If libertarian free will exists:

### Classical atemporalism

God knows all actual events timelessly.

### Molinism

God knows:

> possibilities + counterfactual free choices + actual history.

### Open theism

Some future free choices aren't yet settled facts, so God knows all that can be known but not a determinate future that doesn't yet exist. ([OUP Academic][1])

This means that **omniscience itself needs to be defined before we can ask whether God possesses it.**

That's a very important methodological point.

---

# 15. And I think your "temporal intervention" insight can now be made much sharper

Suppose God is atemporal.

Then:

> **creation**

can be an atemporal relation between God and the entire spacetime universe.

But an intervention at \(t_1\) cannot simply be "another atemporal creation."

It has to correspond to a change in the history of the universe.

Suppose the unmodified history would be:

$$
H_0.
$$

God intervenes at \(t_1\).

The actual history becomes:

$$
H_1.
$$

But because spacetime is a four-dimensional structure, the intervention isn't merely modifying "the present."

It changes which complete history is actual:

$$
H_0 \rightarrow H_1.
$$

From our perspective:

> something changed at \(t_1\).

From an atemporal perspective:

> the complete spacetime history \(H_1\) is the actual history.

This is very close to the intuition you were trying to articulate.

---

# 16. But this creates another fascinating distinction

An atemporal God could potentially perform an action whose **effect is temporal**.

So:

> **atemporal cause ≠ atemporal effect.**

The causal relation doesn't have to share the temporal structure of its effect.

That's exactly analogous to:

> mathematical structure → temporal physical structure.

The mathematical relation isn't itself an event occurring at a particular time.

Likewise, on classical theism:

> God → spacetime event E

doesn't require:

> God was at t = -5 and then acted at t = 0.

Rather, the entire causal relation can be conceived as outside temporal succession while its effect is located at a particular spacetime coordinate.

That's a genuinely powerful conceptual tool.

---

# 17. Intervention therefore has two meanings

### External/ontological intervention

God determines that the spacetime history contains E.

No "moment" of divine action is required.

### Internal/temporal intervention

From inside the universe:

> E happens at \(t\).

For creatures, this looks like a physical event.

So what appears to us as:

> "God intervened at t"

could metaphysically be:

> "God's timeless creative act includes a universe in which E occurs at t."

That is much closer to classical theism.

---

# 18. And this helps separate miracles from sustenance

A miracle isn't necessarily:

> God temporarily becomes a physical cause.

It could instead be:

> God actualizes a spacetime history that contains an event not generated by the ordinary secondary causal structure.

So:

**ordinary event**

$$
A\rightarrow B
$$

versus:

**miraculous event**

$$
A\quad\text{and}\quad God\rightarrow B.
$$

God's causal relation is not necessarily another physical mechanism competing with \(A\).

Again, that's the primary/secondary causation distinction.

---

# 19. Now let's return to goodness

I agree with your intuition that this can be developed without immediately importing Christian theology.

Suppose:

> The agent has a purpose \(P\).

Then one candidate definition is:

$$
Good(x) \iff x\text{ contributes to the realization of }P.
$$

This is essentially **teleological goodness**.

But we have to be careful.

If God wants something, does that make it good?

If yes, goodness risks becoming:

> "whatever God happens to prefer."

That's a divine-command theory.

The more interesting position is:

> God chooses something **because it is good**, rather than it becoming good merely because God chooses it.

Then goodness is independent of divine preference.

But now we need a theory of objective goodness.

And your earlier thought about autonomous rational agents is potentially important:

> perhaps **rational agency itself is intrinsically valuable**.

Then:

> consciousness → rationality → autonomy → relationships → knowledge → moral development

might constitute a hierarchy of goods.

The universe can contain suffering because realizing those goods requires a world with:

> risk, scarcity, ignorance, difficulty, genuine alternatives, causal independence, etc.

That gives us a possible **naturalistic-looking explanation of evil within a teleological framework**.

But it is still only a candidate.

---

# 20. Your example of parenting is actually philosophically useful

You said:

> spoiling children doesn't make them better people; sometimes struggle is necessary.

Exactly.

The relevant principle would be:

> **A world optimized for immediate pleasant experience isn't necessarily a world optimized for the realization of the deeper goods of rational agency.**

If autonomy is valuable, then a world with:

> no danger
> no failure
> no scarcity
> no consequences
> no uncertainty
> no difficulty

could actually be *worse* for certain forms of value.

This is related to contemporary **soul-making** approaches to the problem of evil, though your formulation is broader than the traditional Christian version.

And importantly, it gives us a possible reason why a good creator wouldn't simply maximize pleasure.

---

# 21. The crucifixion example needs one correction

Your example is interesting, but I'd separate the **theological narrative** from the philosophical principle.

Christian theology doesn't straightforwardly say:

> "God needed people to commit the worst possible crime so that people would believe."

Different Christian traditions understand the crucifixion in very different ways: atonement, victory over death, reconciliation, revelation of divine love, satisfaction, moral transformation, etc.

But philosophically, the structure you're interested in is legitimate:

> **A valuable end may require allowing a world in which agents can produce very serious evil.**

And that's a general metaphysical thesis, not specifically Christian.

It becomes:

> If autonomous agency is a good, then a world containing autonomous agency necessarily carries the possibility of misuse of that agency.

That is much cleaner.

---

# 22. And now I think your argument is reaching something genuinely interesting

Not:

> "God exists because humans are good."

Nor:

> "God created humans because he likes them."

Rather:

> **If the ultimate reality is itself an autonomous rational agent, then the creation of additional autonomous rational agents is a structurally natural candidate for a creative end.**

Why?

Because:

1. agency is characteristic of the ultimate reality;
2. agency allows participation in rational/teleological activity;
3. other agents can instantiate that same form of activity;
4. a universe capable of generating such agents therefore realizes a distinctive kind of value.

That's not yet an argument for God.

But it is a **candidate theory of why a necessary agent would create anything at all.**

And that's exactly the question we were struggling with.

---

# 23. Is this original?

This is where I want to be careful with you.

The individual pieces are definitely **not new**:

* God creating rational beings in God's image is ancient.
* Divine goodness and creation are central to classical theism.
* Molinism explicitly connects divine world-selection with knowledge of free creatures' counterfactual choices. ([OUP Academic][1])
* The idea that freedom introduces the possibility of evil is extremely well established.
* Atemporal divine knowledge is classical.
* Soul-making accounts of evil are established.

But **your particular line of thought is worth isolating**, especially:

> **The ultimate agent's selection of a contingent world requires a specification of what distinguishes the selected world from other worlds satisfying the same higher-order purpose; this pushes us toward a distinction between structural choice, consequential knowledge, and knowledge of free-agent counterfactuals.**

That is a useful formulation.

I would **not yet call it a new argument**. But I would absolutely put it in our "candidate original synthesis" folder and then search specifically for whether someone has already formulated the same structure.

That's actually how I'd approach the book/essay project: **don't try to prove novelty first; formulate the argument as strongly as possible, then deliberately try to find prior formulations that defeat the claim of novelty.**

If it survives that process, *then* we have something.

---

# 24. And I think our next step is now very clear

We have three increasingly strong conceptions of omniscience:

### Minimal

> God knows everything necessary for rationally choosing the universe.

### Strong

> God knows all possibilities and all relevant consequences.

### Maximal

> God knows all actual facts, all possibilities, and all counterfactuals concerning free agents.

The third is essentially the territory of **Molinism/classical omniscience**, and it has serious grounding problems. ([OUP Academic][1])

But there is another possibility that I think fits your line of thought particularly well:

> **God doesn't need exhaustive predictive knowledge if the future contains genuinely free choices. Omniscience could instead mean knowing every truth that exists—including every actual event, every possibility, every necessary relation, and perhaps every presently determinate counterfactual—without requiring nonexistent future contingents to already have determinate truth values.**

That takes us toward **open-future models**, but with an atemporal God rather than simply a temporally limited one.

And then the really interesting question becomes:

> **Can an atemporal being know every actual temporal event while those events remain genuinely contingent, and can it intervene in that history without becoming a temporal entity itself?**

That is where I think your intuition about **"God doesn't know the future; God knows every moment"** deserves a much more rigorous treatment. It may turn out to be essentially a version of an old Boethian idea, or it may contain a useful refinement. We should test it against the strongest objections rather than assume either outcome. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][4])

After that, I'd take the **goodness branch** in parallel: whether "autonomous rational agency is good" can actually be defended independently, and whether that gives us a principled reason for creation rather than merely projecting human preferences onto the hypothetical agent.

[1]: https://academic.oup.com/edited-volume/42642/chapter/358143853?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Molinism | The Oxford Handbook of Topics in Philosophy | Oxford Academic"
[2]: https://iep.utm.edu/middlekn/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Middle Knowledge | Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy"
[3]: https://academic.oup.com/mind/article/131/522/459/6382285?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Molinism: Explaining our Freedom Away | Mind | Oxford Academic"
[4]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/medieval-futcont/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Medieval Theories of Future Contingents (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"



Regarding novelty, as I said, when you can say, you say it. If for you to be able to say it, you need to do a bit of research, do it. And then if nothing is novel, then that's okay. I just want to pursue these ideas further. You have mentioned a few of them in which you say that they are relevant to the model we are building. So let's try to go deeper into those, particularly Molinism. Not sure if that's the true solution, or if it goes in the right direction, but I think that we should try to go further and try to propose solutions to this counterfactuals and to these possibilities, or find alternatives that work better. Particularly when we come to what omniscience is, I think we can probably do something different that is not in any of those alternatives, because if we take classical atemporality, it doesn't seem to be necessary that God knows all actual events timelessly. But I think this is the closest one to what we're looking for. Because if, for instance, we take open theism, then that means that God itself is, let's say, enclosed or limited by time. That feels weird because time is a feature of the universe. And maybe even less so. Maybe it's just a feature of our experience of the universe. So I don't think it's very reasonable to consider that like that. Maybe a different variation. And as we said, Molinism seems to also point in the right direction, but it has some limitations and problems. So maybe we can find something between classical atemporality and Molinism that solves it better. Because I think defining omniscience is not just, okay, we leave it like that. We just don't have a definition. No. Let's find a definition that makes sense. Let's define it in a way that is actually appropriate for what we're building. I also think that all kinds of interventions should be considered. Obviously different theories would maybe just allow one or some types of interventions, but I think we should consider all of them and also push it against our definitions of omnipotency, omniscience, and all of that to see if some of them actually break our model or push it further or are compatible or not. I also like your definition of miracle because miracle seems to be something supernatural, and supernatural, assuming that we would actually know the laws of nature well enough to say what is possible in nature and what is not, which is already a big gap in our knowledge. We don't really know. But even assuming so, it means that it's something that is outside the laws of nature, of these secondary causations, right? So that's a good definition. I think I like it. In terms of goodness, there's something that I think is problematic. If we have an objective notion of goodness that goes even beyond the cosmic agent, then the cosmic agent is not ultimate. It's not necessary in the sense that that thing was ontologically prior to the agent as well, because that existed. So I think it makes more sense to actually accept that good is whatever the cosmic agent chooses, unless you suggest me another approach that makes more sense and that makes this apparent contradiction disappear. Because if there's an objective good, we don't necessarily need to get the same notion, because after all humans have different notions of what is good. I guess there are some things that are more common to be considered good, and maybe 100% of people consider it even. But it's still mostly something that is related to our biology and evolution and to our culture, to our anthropology and everything that keeps us together. And obviously there are things that can go beyond that, and where there's more discussion. So I don't think it's that crazy to just define good as whatever the agent chooses to be good, right? Especially because that solves also so many problems of why bad things happen. I mean, part of that is also all these consequences of things that the agent doesn't necessarily choose, but they are just inevitable consequence of the other things it chooses. But another side is also defining good. But I think that definitely this explanation of this naturalistic-looking explanation, as you call it, is probably the one I'm more comfortable with because, yeah, as you said, in order to make the mind reason in these problems to reason about, and the world gives us all these problems. So if you give everything already done, it seems less likely that they will develop true agency. I mean, if our brains are wired the way they are wired, they would. But it seems natural to us all these problems that challenge us. Maybe I would go back to Jordan Peterson here saying that we are made for maximal challenge. That's obviously all, again, a bit outside of this framework, but it's things that point us in the right direction. Linking that to long-term precisely, I think immediate present experience is precisely something that many philosophers as well have found to be the raw motivation, and there are incredibly many, and I would say it's almost unrefutable arguments that humans work better with sacrifice and with prolonging the time that it takes to get reward, and with seeing beyond their own lives even. So I think we have to integrate that in a way that makes sense, right? I mean, it may not be directly what makes the agent do what it does, but I think it should inform us of what shape or what reasoning this agent could have, or what the choices the agent made happen to have consequences. So yeah, let's take all these ideas to push them further and make a form for this argument or model or whatever we are building, this ontology, anthologyology and so on, and we can extract precisely this synthesis or whatever is interesting and potentially novel, or that has maybe just a different phrasing, a different point of view that can be useful and enlightening. So let's write those down. Let's make a note of those, and whatever survives the process, then it's good. If nothing does, then I've learned something. That's fine for me. So yeah, we can also go further with this thing about atemporality and so on, which is what I suggested before, to actually make it a bit more rigorous. I think I'm not so concerned about whether the atemporal being can become temporal. That's an interesting thought, possibly an interesting part of the model, but I think it's less of an issue for me. Like, we can distinguish situations and see how the agent or the world model or the ontology changes, if it is really significant or if it's not. And depending on what we get, we can follow it up further.




Yes. I think this is the point where we should stop treating the discussion as a tour through existing philosophers and start treating it as a **research program**: formulate the ontology, identify the pressure points, test candidate solutions, and only then compare it with existing literature.

I did a targeted literature check specifically on the pieces we've been combining—Molinism and its grounding problem, atemporal omniscience, divine simplicity, and theological voluntarism. The individual components are very well represented in the literature, but I **did not find, from this initial search, an obvious prior formulation of the exact architecture we are developing**. That is *not* enough to claim originality: we'd need a much more systematic literature review before saying that. But it is enough to justify treating some of our combinations as **potentially distinctive syntheses** rather than assuming we're merely reproducing someone else's argument.

There are two places where I think we can make especially substantial progress now:

1. **construct a better theory of divine knowledge than simply choosing between classical theism, Molinism and open theism;**
2. **reconstruct goodness without making it an independent entity above God, while also avoiding the triviality "good = whatever God happens to want."**

And these two problems turn out to be connected.

---

# I. First: let's put our ontology on paper

I would currently formulate the model like this.

### Level 0 — Logical/metaphysical background

There are truths about what is possible, impossible, necessary, contradictory, etc.

We don't yet decide whether these are:

* Platonic entities,
* propositions,
* structures,
* facts about logical space,
* or something grounded in God.

This is deliberately left open.

### Level 1 — Necessary reality

There exists, or potentially exists, something whose nonexistence is metaphysically impossible.

Call it:

$$
N.
$$

The important point is not merely:

> "N exists in every possible world."

The deeper question is:

> **What makes N necessary?**

This is where O'Connor, Aquinas, Leibniz, divine simplicity, etc., become relevant.

### Level 2 — Agency

Suppose \(N\) is not merely an impersonal structure but an **agent**.

Then it has:

* intentionality,
* reasons,
* alternatives,
* choice,
* perhaps knowledge and purposes.

This is already a major ontological step.

### Level 3 — Creation/actualization

There is a space of possible physical structures:

$$
\mathcal G = \{G_1,G_2,\ldots\}.
$$

A \(G\) is a generating specification sufficient to determine a physical universe, or at least enough of one.

The agent actualizes:

$$
G^*.
$$

### Level 4 — Physical universe

$$
G^* \rightarrow U.
$$

The universe has its own internal causal structure.

This is where physics lives.

### Level 5 — Autonomous agents

The physical structure produces creatures capable of genuine agency.

Potentially:

$$
U \rightarrow A_1,A_2,\ldots
$$

where some \(A_i\) possess libertarian freedom.

And now the interesting problem begins.

---

# II. The Molinist problem is actually *our* problem

Molinism is usually presented as a solution to the apparent conflict between:

> God's providence

and

> libertarian creaturely freedom.

The central idea is that God has **middle knowledge**: knowledge of counterfactuals such as:

> If person \(A\) were in circumstances \(C\), \(A\) would freely choose \(X\).

This allows God to choose a world knowing what free creatures would do in it.

That is extremely close to our universe-selection problem.

But the grounding objection is serious.

Suppose:

$$
C \Box\!\rightarrow X
$$

means:

> "If A were in C, A would freely choose X."

What makes that proposition true?

This is precisely the question Molinism struggles with. The literature identifies the **grounding objection** as one of the central objections to middle knowledge. Proposed answers include brute counterfactual facts, grounding in the future event, grounding in the creature as conceived by God, and other approaches. ([Enciclopedia de Internet de Filosofía][1])

So I think we should *not* simply adopt Molinism.

Instead, let's ask:

> **Can we get what Molinism wants without requiring a mysterious pre-creative catalogue of counterfactual creaturely decisions?**

I think there are at least three promising possibilities.

---

# III. Model A: Molinism

The structure is:

$$
\text{God}
\rightarrow
\boxed{\text{counterfactual knowledge}}
\rightarrow
G
\rightarrow
U.
$$

God knows:

> If this world is created, Alice would freely do X.

Therefore God can select a world partly on the basis of its free history.

### Strength

It gives God extremely rich providential knowledge without deterministically causing free actions.

### Weakness

Where do those counterfactual truths come from?

If they are:

* independent of God → something looks ontologically prior to God;
* determined by God → freedom is threatened;
* determined by the creature → the creature doesn't yet exist;
* brute facts → we have introduced exactly the kind of unexplained fundamental fact we were trying to avoid.

So Molinism may simply **relocate the brute fact**.

That's a very important result for our project.

---

# IV. Model B: Open future

Here:

$$
\text{God knows everything that is true}.
$$

But some future libertarian choices aren't yet true or false.

So:

> "Alice will choose X tomorrow"

doesn't currently have a determinate truth value.

God knows:

* all possibilities;
* all necessary truths;
* all present facts;
* all actual past facts;
* all constraints on the future;
* perhaps probability distributions where those are objective.

But not a fact that doesn't yet exist.

This is coherent.

But, as you say, it makes God appear temporally related to the universe.

That isn't necessarily fatal—one can distinguish **being temporally located** from **knowing temporal reality**—but it is less attractive if our starting point is that time itself is part of the created structure.

---

# V. Model C: Atemporal complete-history knowledge

This is the classical direction.

God isn't at:

$$
t_0
$$

and then looking toward:

$$
t_1.
$$

Rather, God knows the entire temporal structure.

The SEP's current treatment of omniscience describes the Boethian view precisely this way: God's perspective is eternity, in which the whole temporal sequence is possessed "all at once," rather than being predicted from an earlier moment. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

But **here is where I think your proposal can become more interesting than simply saying "classical theism is right."**

You noticed something important:

> **Why should omniscience be defined as knowing every actual event timelessly?**

It doesn't have to be.

The atemporal model gives us the *metaphysical framework* but doesn't yet tell us the correct **epistemic definition**.

---

# VI. A fourth model: structural omniscience

This is the direction I think we should investigate.

Instead of:

> God knows every future event.

or:

> God knows every counterfactual free choice.

we could define omniscience in terms of **complete knowledge of reality's modal-temporal structure**.

Something like:

> **An omniscient being knows every truth concerning every actual, possible and necessary state of affairs, including their logical, causal and modal relations.**

But there's a crucial qualification:

> **It does not follow that every merely possible future is an already-existing future fact.**

So God knows:

$$
\mathcal P = \{\text{all possible histories}\}.
$$

He knows:

$$
H^* = \text{the actual history}.
$$

And he knows the structure connecting:

$$
G \rightarrow \{H_1,H_2,H_3,\ldots\}.
$$

If a libertarian agent makes the actual choice:

$$
H^*
$$

then God knows that actual fact.

But God does not need to have known it **as a future contingent proposition before it became actual**.

---

# VII. This gives us a very interesting definition

I'd tentatively call it:

## **Modal-structural omniscience**

> **Omniscience is complete knowledge of all truths about reality: necessary truths, actual truths, possibilities, impossibilities, and the relations among them.**

The crucial thing is that "future" isn't a special epistemological category.

It's merely:

> a region of temporal structure relative to a creature located at a particular time.

For God, if God is genuinely atemporal, there is no privileged epistemic category called "future."

There is just:

$$
\text{reality as a whole}.
$$

This is much cleaner.

---

# VIII. But then we encounter a subtle problem

Suppose Alice freely chooses X.

Before Alice chooses:

$$
X
$$

is possible and:

$$
Y
$$

is also possible.

After Alice chooses:

$$
X
$$

is actual.

God's knowledge therefore changes?

If God is genuinely immutable and timeless, apparently not.

But then what exactly does God know?

One possibility is:

> God's knowledge contains the complete structure in which X becomes actual.

There isn't a temporal updating of God's knowledge.

The **object of knowledge** is a temporal event, but the act of divine knowing isn't itself temporally updated.

This is very close to classical divine simplicity. On the classical account, God's knowledge isn't something God *has* as a changing mental state; divine attributes are radically different from creaturely properties, and in strong divine simplicity God is identical with the divine attributes rather than merely instantiating them. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][3])

We don't need to accept all of divine simplicity to exploit this insight.

---

# IX. And this lets us distinguish three things that are normally conflated

### Temporal becoming

What happens from the perspective of a creature:

> X isn't actual yet → X becomes actual.

### Atemporal truth

From God's perspective:

> X is an event in the complete structure of reality.

### Epistemic acquisition

A creature learns:

> "X happened."

God doesn't need epistemic acquisition.

This gives us a potentially powerful formulation:

> **Divine omniscience is not infinitely fast prediction; it is non-acquisitional apprehension of reality.**

That's a much better conception than "God knows the future."

And I would mark this as one of our **potentially distinctive formulations**. The underlying idea is unquestionably ancient, but this particular connection to our generating-structure model and libertarian future is worth investigating further.

---

# X. What does this do to Molinism?

Quite a lot.

Molinism says God knows:

$$
C \Box\!\rightarrow X
$$

before creating.

Our model could instead say:

> God knows the complete modal structure of the possible universes.

So God knows:

$$
G_1\rightarrow H_1,H_2,H_3...
$$

and:

$$
G_2\rightarrow H_4,H_5,H_6...
$$

where the branches involving libertarian agents remain genuinely branching.

Then creation actualizes one branch.

God knows the actual branch **because it is actual**, rather than because God possessed a pre-creative deterministic-looking description of every free decision.

This avoids the specific Molinist grounding problem.

But there is a price.

It weakens **pre-creation providential knowledge**.

God knows what can happen and what does happen, but not necessarily a pre-creative truth:

> "Alice would freely choose X in G."

That might be perfectly acceptable.

In fact, I suspect it may be more philosophically economical.

---

# XI. We should therefore distinguish two types of divine providence

### Strong providence

God chooses the world knowing its complete future history.

That's close to Molinism.

### Structural providence

God chooses a world whose **space of possible histories** has desirable properties.

For example:

> "I choose a universe capable of producing autonomous rational agents."

God doesn't need to specify:

> Alice chooses X at 14:32 on Tuesday.

The point is that the universe is structured so that:

* agency exists;
* alternatives exist;
* consequences exist;
* learning exists;
* moral development exists;
* etc.

That actually fits your intuition about why **challenge** might be valuable.

The agent chooses a *space of possibilities*, not a predetermined biography for every person.

That is a substantial conceptual shift.

---

# XII. And now "free will" becomes more interesting

Under this model, God doesn't give Alice freedom by:

> choosing what Alice will do.

God gives Alice freedom by creating a structure in which:

$$
\{X,Y,Z\}
$$

are genuinely available to her.

Then:

$$
A \rightsquigarrow X
$$

is an irreducibly agentive causal relation.

God chooses the **conditions for agency**.

Alice chooses the action.

That is much cleaner than saying God knows in advance exactly what Alice will freely do.

And it fits very naturally with O'Connor-style agent causation, which is why I think your earlier attraction to O'Connor matters here.

---

# XIII. Now let's attack our own model

There is an obvious objection:

> If God knows the complete space of possibilities but doesn't know which free choice will occur, isn't God's knowledge incomplete?

Our answer would be:

**Only if "which free choice will occur" is already a truth before the choice occurs.**

If libertarian freedom is genuinely indeterministic in the strong sense, there may simply be no fact yet corresponding to:

> "Alice will choose X."

So there is nothing for omniscience to lack.

This is an extremely important distinction:

> **Ignorance is failure to know a truth.**

versus:

> **Non-existence of a truth because the relevant contingent event has not yet been settled.**

The latter is not ignorance.

This is essentially where open-future theories have their strongest point, but we're combining it with the atemporal framework rather than making God temporally embedded.

---

# XIV. Now let's turn to your goodness objection

I agree with you that my earlier formulation:

> "There is objective goodness independently of God"

creates a serious problem for the ontology we're constructing.

If goodness is a necessary entity existing independently of the necessary agent, then:

$$
God \quad+\quad Goodness
$$

are two fundamental realities.

That undermines the claim that God is the ultimate explanatory ground.

So we should take your proposal seriously:

> **Goodness is grounded in the agent itself.**

But we need to distinguish two very different versions.

---

# XV. Bad version: arbitrary divine preference

$$
Good(x) \iff God\ wills(x).
$$

Then:

> If God willed torture for its own sake, torture would become good.

That makes goodness seem arbitrary.

And this is exactly the traditional Euthyphro problem.

Contemporary theological voluntarism explicitly deals with versions of this position; importantly, modern theistic theories don't all say that goodness is identical to God's commands or arbitrary will. Some distinguish moral obligation from goodness and ground the latter in God in a different way. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][4])

So we shouldn't stop at simple voluntarism.

---

# XVI. Better version: goodness is grounded in the agent's nature

Suppose the necessary agent doesn't have a collection of arbitrary preferences.

Instead, there is a necessary relation:

$$
N \equiv \text{the agent's nature}.
$$

And its choices necessarily express that nature.

Then:

$$
Good(x)
$$

means something like:

> x realizes or participates in the value inherent in the nature of the ultimate agent.

This is very close to a major classical-theist strategy.

Divine simplicity takes this dramatically far: God isn't a subject who possesses goodness as an external property; God is, in the classical account, identical with goodness itself in the relevant sense. This is explicitly presented as one way of escaping the Euthyphro dilemma without making goodness either external to God or arbitrary. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][3])

But **we don't have to accept classical divine simplicity wholesale**.

We can extract the underlying idea:

> **The standard of goodness is internal to the necessary agent's nature rather than external to it.**

---

# XVII. But this raises your excellent question:

> Why would *that* nature be good?

And here I think we have to be extremely careful.

There are three possibilities.

### A. Goodness is external

Then God isn't ultimate.

Bad for our model.

### B. Goodness is arbitrary preference

Then objective goodness disappears.

Also unsatisfactory.

### C. Goodness is constitutive of the agent's necessary nature

Then:

$$
God = \text{a necessarily good kind of being}.
$$

There is no further entity called Goodness standing above God.

This is the classical solution.

But then the question becomes:

> **Why is the nature of a necessary rational agent necessarily such that it is good?**

And *that* is the difficult question we should investigate.

---

# XVIII. There is another possibility I think fits our project even better

Don't define goodness initially as:

> "whatever God chooses."

Define it as:

> **what realizes the constitutive powers or ends of an agent/nature.**

Then we can distinguish:

### Agent-relative good

Something is good for \(A\) if it fulfills \(A\)'s nature/capacities.

### Intrinsic good

Something is good in itself.

### Divine good

What realizes the nature of the ultimate agent.

### Creaturely good

What realizes the nature of creatures.

This means we don't need:

> one Platonic object called GOOD.

We have a theory of **value grounded in natures and actualization**.

That's basically where Aristotelian/Thomistic theories become relevant, but there are contemporary developments and alternatives.

---

# XIX. And here's where your observation about human goodness becomes important

You're right:

> humans don't agree on everything that is good.

But that doesn't necessarily undermine objective value.

We don't expect disagreement about:

> mathematics

to prove that there are no mathematical truths.

Likewise:

> disagreement about value

doesn't by itself prove that value is subjective.

The real question is:

> **Is there a fact of the matter about what fulfills a given nature?**

That is a much more manageable proposition.

For example, suppose rational agency is genuinely valuable.

Then:

$$
knowledge,\ autonomy,\ rational\ development
$$

might be goods for rational agents.

And:

$$
ignorance,\ coercion,\ irrationality
$$

might be defects *relative to that nature*.

This doesn't require God to issue commands.

---

# XX. And now your "maximal challenge" intuition becomes philosophically interesting

Suppose the agent's reason for creation is not:

> maximize pleasure.

Instead:

> maximize the realization of autonomous rational agency.

Then a universe without adversity might actually be **worse**.

Because:

$$
\text{agency}
\rightarrow
\text{choice}
\rightarrow
\text{risk}
\rightarrow
\text{failure}
\rightarrow
\text{learning}
\rightarrow
\text{development}.
$$

Likewise:

$$
\text{long-term goals}
\rightarrow
\text{delayed gratification}
\rightarrow
\text{sacrifice}
\rightarrow
\text{character}.
$$

Now we have a principled way of explaining why:

> immediate pleasure ≠ maximal goodness.

And this is philosophically much stronger than invoking Peterson or empirical psychology directly.

Psychology can become **evidence about how a universe with rational embodied agents actually realizes these goods**, rather than the foundation of the metaphysics.

---

# XXI. This also gives us a nontrivial account of evil

Suppose:

$$
G = \text{a world permitting autonomous rational agency}.
$$

Then some consequences may be unavoidable:

$$
G \rightarrow
\begin{cases}
\text{freedom}\\
\text{ignorance}\\
\text{risk}\\
\text{failure}\\
\text{suffering}\\
\text{evil}
\end{cases}
$$

The crucial point is:

> **The agent need not choose every consequence individually.**

It chooses \(G\).

Some consequences are structurally entailed by \(G\).

This gives us a potentially elegant distinction:

### Chosen goods

What the agent intentionally actualizes.

### Permitted consequences

What follows from the structure required to realize those goods.

### Unchosen evils

Negative consequences that arise because the chosen structure contains genuine autonomy, contingency, scarcity, etc.

That is already much better than simply saying:

> "God has a mysterious reason for everything."

---

# XXII. And this gives us a candidate architecture of divine action

We can now classify everything.

| Divine relation           | What happens                                                                 |
| ------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Grounding**             | God explains why contingent reality exists                                   |
| **Creation**              | God actualizes a generating structure                                        |
| **Sustenance**            | God grounds its continued existence                                          |
| **Structural providence** | God chooses the possibility-space                                            |
| **Creaturely agency**     | creatures make genuinely free choices                                        |
| **Knowledge**             | God knows the modal/actual structure                                         |
| **Intervention**          | God causes particular events                                                 |
| **Miracle**               | divine causation produces an event outside ordinary secondary causal closure |
| **Moral governance**      | God assigns normative significance                                           |
| **Teleology**             | reality is ordered toward certain ends                                       |

And none of these automatically implies all the others.

That's important.

---

# XXIII. Omnipotence should therefore also be decomposed

I don't think we should define omnipotence merely as:

> "can do anything."

That's logically incoherent because "anything" includes contradictions.

Instead:

> **Omnipotence = ability to actualize any metaphysically possible state of affairs compatible with the agent's own necessary nature.**

Then distinguish:

### Creative omnipotence

Can actualize any possible generating structure.

### Causal omnipotence

Can cause any metaphysically possible event.

### Sustaining omnipotence

Can maintain any contingent reality.

### Providential omnipotence

Can arrange circumstances without destroying creaturely agency.

### Logical limitation

Cannot:

> make \(P\land\neg P\) true.

That's not a limitation in the ordinary sense; it's simply incoherence.

---

# XXIV. And this reveals an important interaction between omniscience and omnipotence

Suppose:

> God can create every possible universe.

But God doesn't know which universes are possible.

Then omnipotence is epistemically useless.

Conversely:

> God knows every possibility.

but can't actualize them.

Then God has omniscience without omnipotence.

So our architecture naturally produces:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Modal knowledge}
+
\text{unbounded causal power}
}
$$

as the core of divine creative capacity.

That is a much more precise foundation for "omnipotence."

---

# XXV. Miracles then fit beautifully

Your definition was good, and I'd sharpen it:

> **A miracle is an event whose sufficient causal explanation includes divine agency rather than being exhausted by the ordinary secondary causal structure of nature.**

This is better than:

> "something impossible according to physics."

Because, as you rightly say, we don't possess a complete catalogue of what nature permits.

And it avoids the crude:

> supernatural = violates physics.

A miracle could instead be:

$$
\text{ordinary causal structure} + \text{divine primary causation}.
$$

The physical event remains physical.

The **ultimate explanation of why that event occurred** includes something outside the physical causal network.

---

# XXVI. Where I think we now have a potentially interesting synthesis

Let me formulate it compactly.

> **The necessary agent does not need to select a complete universe history. It can select a fundamental generating structure whose modal consequences include a space of possible histories. If that structure contains genuinely libertarian agents, then some future events are not fixed prior to their occurrence. Omniscience therefore need not consist in pre-temporal prediction of every future contingent event; it can consist in complete knowledge of necessary truths, modal possibilities, the actual structure, and every determinate truth concerning it. Divine providence can consequently operate primarily through the selection and sustaining of a possibility-space rather than the determination of every event within it.**

**That formulation is the one I would now put in our research notes as a candidate central thesis.**

Again: I am **not claiming that nobody has said this**. Parts of it clearly overlap with Boethian atemporality, Molinism, open-future theories, agent causation, and classical providence. What may be interesting is the **specific synthesis around the generating structure / possibility-space distinction**. Our next literature pass should explicitly search for that.

---

# XXVII. And I think there is a second candidate thesis

On goodness:

> **Rather than postulating an objective good existing independently of the necessary agent, or reducing goodness to arbitrary divine preference, goodness can potentially be understood as grounded in the necessary agent's own nature and the realization of its constitutive ends. Creation can then be evaluated according to the degree to which its possibility-space realizes those ends, while negative consequences may arise necessarily from the conditions required for their realization.**

This has much more precedent—especially classical theism and contemporary theological voluntarism—so I'm **less optimistic about originality** here. Modern theological voluntarists explicitly distinguish views in which moral status depends on divine will from views that ground goodness itself in God without reducing it to arbitrary commands. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][4])

But our application to the **choice of a generating structure** is worth developing.

---

# XXVIII. One thing I would *not* put into the model yet

I would **not yet say**:

> "God wants autonomous rational agents."

I'd put it in the model as a **candidate hypothesis**:

$$
H_A = \text{the necessary agent values autonomous rational agency}.
$$

Then we can ask:

1. Is \(H_A\) coherent?
2. Does it follow from agency itself?
3. Does it require an additional assumption?
4. Does it explain features of our universe?
5. Does it outperform alternative purposes?
6. Does it generate problematic consequences?
7. Does it resemble existing theological doctrines because of genuine convergence or because we're importing them?

That's the scientific/philosophical discipline I think you were asking for.

---

# XXIX. And there is a beautiful symmetry emerging

At the beginning of our discussion we had:

> **Science describes regularities but doesn't ultimately explain why there are those regularities.**

Now our metaphysical model is trying to move one level deeper:

$$
\boxed{
\text{physical laws}
}
$$

↓ generated by

$$
\boxed{
\text{fundamental structure}
}
$$

↓ selected by

$$
\boxed{
\text{reason}
}
$$

↓ belonging to

$$
\boxed{
\text{agent}
}
$$

↓ grounded in

$$
\boxed{
\text{necessary reality}
}
$$

And then the reverse direction:

$$
\boxed{
\text{necessary agent}
}
\rightarrow
\boxed{
\text{possible structures}
}
\rightarrow
\boxed{
\text{physical universe}
}
\rightarrow
\boxed{
\text{autonomous agents}
}
\rightarrow
\boxed{
\text{knowledge, freedom, value}
}
$$

That's beginning to look like an actual **ontology**, rather than simply an argument for "God."

And importantly, it leaves open the possibility that the endpoint isn't exactly the God of classical Christianity. We can ask what attributes are *actually forced* by the architecture and which are merely compatible with it.

---

## Where I'd take it next

I think the next stage should be a deliberately adversarial one rather than another exposition.

**First:** take our proposed **structural/modal omniscience** and attack it from every direction:

* Does an atemporal God genuinely know contingent facts?
* What does "knowing the actual branch" mean if the branch wasn't determined?
* Can an atemporal entity distinguish actual from merely possible?
* Does timeless knowledge entail fatalism?
* Does libertarian freedom require genuinely open future?
* Can God know an open future without being temporal?
* Does our model secretly collapse into Molinism?
* Does it secretly collapse into determinism?
* Does it require a B-theory of time?
* Can it coexist with an A-theory of time?

**Second:** do the same to the goodness theory:

* Can goodness be grounded in God's nature without becoming arbitrary?
* Does "God's nature" itself require a standard?
* Can God's necessary nature include contingent preferences?
* What does it mean for something to "realize" divine nature?
* Can suffering be a necessary cost of autonomous agency?
* Is there a principled upper bound on that claim, or can it excuse literally anything?
* Can we distinguish genuine soul-making from an ad-hoc theodicy?

And **third**, I'd do a proper novelty/precedent map around the resulting propositions, rather than merely saying "this sounds like Molinism" or "this sounds classical." That is where we'll find out whether we're rediscovering something, combining existing ideas in a useful way, or actually producing a formulation that deserves to be developed independently.

[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/sum2024/entries/omniscience/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Omniscience (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy/Summer 2024 Edition)"
[3]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/divine-simplicity/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Divine Simplicity (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[4]: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/fall2025/entries/voluntarism-theological/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Theological Voluntarism (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy/Fall 2025 Edition)"

Yes. I think your correction gives us a better methodological rule: **we're not trying to maximize the number of objections; we're trying to identify the places where the model actually bears philosophical weight, then compare the alternatives seriously.** And we should keep separate what is already well established from what is merely an interesting synthesis we are developing.

The next step, then, is to tighten the architecture rather than add more attributes to God prematurely.

## 1. First correction: "God knows Alice's choice" does not by itself destroy freedom

Your intuition is basically right, but there is a subtle logical distinction we need to make very explicit.

There are three propositions that are often conflated:

1. **Alice freely chooses X.**
2. **God knows that Alice chooses X.**
3. **Alice is causally determined to choose X.**

The first and second do **not logically entail the third**.

The problematic argument is usually:

> If God infallibly knows that Alice will choose X, then Alice cannot choose anything other than X; therefore she isn't free.

But "cannot" is ambiguous.

There is a difference between:

### Epistemic impossibility

> Given God's infallible knowledge, it is impossible that God is wrong about Alice's choice.

and

### Causal/metaphysical necessity

> Given the state of reality prior to Alice's choice, Alice is unable to choose otherwise.

Those are not obviously the same.

If God knows:

> Alice chooses X,

then:

$$
\neg(\text{God knows Alice chooses X})
$$

is impossible *given God's infallibility*.

But it doesn't follow that:

$$
\neg X
$$

was causally impossible for Alice.

The key is the direction of dependence.

We could have:

$$
\text{Alice freely chooses X}
\rightarrow
\text{God knows Alice chooses X}
$$

without:

$$
\text{God's knowing X}
\rightarrow
\text{Alice chooses X}.
$$

This is one of the oldest problems in the philosophy of divine foreknowledge and freedom, so the distinction itself isn't novel. The interesting question for us is whether our **atemporal/modal model gives us a particularly clean way of expressing it.**

---

# 2. But there is a deeper problem: infallibility seems to produce necessity

Suppose, counterfactually:

> Alice could have chosen Y.

If God eternally knows:

> Alice chooses X,

then could Alice actually have chosen Y?

If she did, God's eternal knowledge would have been false.

So we seem to get:

$$
\Box(GK_X)
$$

and therefore:

$$
\Box X?
$$

But that last inference is exactly what needs to be rejected.

A useful distinction is:

> **Necessity of the consequence** vs **necessity of the consequent**.

The fact that:

> necessarily, if God knows X, X occurs

doesn't mean:

> necessarily, X occurs.

Symbolically:

$$
\Box(GK(X)\rightarrow X)
$$

does not entail:

$$
\Box X.
$$

This is a version of the classical response to the "necessity of the past" argument.

The crucial idea is that God's knowledge is not an **independent prior condition** which constrains Alice.

Rather, the truth of God's knowledge tracks the actual event.

---

# 3. The atemporal model makes this cleaner

Imagine the complete history:

$$
H = (E_1,E_2,\ldots,E_n).
$$

Alice's decision is:

$$
E_k = X.
$$

From Alice's perspective:

$$
E_k
$$

hasn't happened yet.

From God's perspective, there isn't a "before" and "after" in the same way.

God apprehends:

$$
H
$$

as a whole.

So we shouldn't say:

> God looked at \(t_0\), discovered what Alice would do at \(t_1\), and therefore fixed \(t_1\).

Instead:

> God knows the actual history including Alice's free action.

That still leaves the hard question:

> **What makes God's knowledge infallible without making Alice's action predetermined?**

And I think the answer has to be:

> **The truth of the divine knowledge depends on the actual free action, rather than the free action depending causally on the divine knowledge.**

That is philosophically defensible, although not universally accepted.

---

# 4. This also clarifies what I meant by "pre-creative knowledge"

I think my previous terminology was unnecessarily confusing.

Let's distinguish three things.

### P1 — Pre-creative modal knowledge

Before creation, the agent knows:

> These universes are possible; those are impossible; these structures entail those consequences.

For example:

$$
G \Rightarrow \text{possible autonomous agents}.
$$

### P2 — Pre-creative counterfactual knowledge

The agent knows:

> If Alice existed under conditions C, she would freely choose X.

That's Molinism's distinctive claim.

### P3 — Knowledge of the actual history

The agent knows:

> Alice actually chooses X.

This is not necessarily available **as an actual fact before the actualization of the universe**.

And that distinction is critical.

Our model can accept:

$$
P1
$$

without accepting:

$$
P2.
$$

Then, after actualization, it has:

$$
P3.
$$

If God is atemporal, however, we shouldn't interpret "after" as God acquiring knowledge at a later moment. "After" describes the **temporal structure of the universe**, not a change in God's epistemic state.

---

# 5. Your objection to P2 is particularly important

You said, essentially:

> If God knows what Alice would choose in every possible universe, doesn't that already determine what Alice will actually choose?

No—not necessarily, but this reveals why P2 is philosophically expensive.

Suppose:

$$
G_1 \rightarrow X
$$

means:

> In universe \(G_1\), Alice freely chooses X.

And:

$$
G_2 \rightarrow Y
$$

means:

> In universe \(G_2\), Alice freely chooses Y.

God can know both.

Then choosing \(G_1\) makes X actual.

But God didn't **cause Alice to choose X by knowing the counterfactual**.

That's the Molinist picture.

The problem is not the causal relation.

The problem is:

> **What makes \(G_1\rightarrow X\) true before either \(G_1\) or X is actual?**

That's the grounding problem.

And this is precisely why I think we should investigate a model that **doesn't require P2**.

---

# 6. Our model can instead make the possibility-space primary

This gives us a useful distinction:

### God's knowledge before creation

$$
K(\mathcal{P})
$$

where \(\mathcal P\) is the complete space of metaphysically possible structures and histories.

### God's creative act

$$
\mathcal P \rightarrow G^*
$$

### Actual history

$$
G^* \rightarrow H^*
$$

### God's knowledge

$$
K(\mathcal P,H^*)
$$

The important thing is that we do **not** need:

$$
K(\text{counterfactual free choices in every }G)
$$

to explain providence.

God can choose:

> "I want a world in which genuine autonomous agency is possible."

rather than:

> "I want a world in which Alice freely chooses X at time T."

That seems much more compatible with your structural-providence intuition.

---

# 7. But now we should admit what this sacrifices

It gives up something traditionally associated with strong providence.

Suppose God chooses a universe containing Alice.

He knows:

* Alice will exist;
* she will have genuine freedom;
* these options will be available;
* these causal circumstances will exist;
* certain consequences are necessary;
* certain other consequences are possible.

But he doesn't necessarily know, prior to creation, **which libertarian choice Alice will actually make**.

That means God cannot guarantee:

> "Alice will eventually choose God."

without somehow determining her choice.

This is a genuine tradeoff.

And I think that's healthy for the model: **we shouldn't define omniscience in such a way that it automatically gives us every theological conclusion we want.**

---

# 8. This suggests a particularly clean definition of omniscience

I'd now distinguish:

### Absolute epistemic completeness

A being knows every proposition that is true.

### Modal completeness

It knows every:

* necessary possibility,
* impossibility,
* possible world,
* causal relation,
* modal relation.

### Temporal completeness

It knows every actual event across the complete temporal structure.

### Counterfactual completeness

It knows what every free agent would do under every counterfactual circumstance.

The last one is **not automatically entailed by the first three**.

And this gives us an important result:

> **Molinism isn't a definition of omniscience; it is a particular theory about one possible component of omniscience.**

That's a useful conceptual separation.

---

# 9. I would therefore provisionally define our omniscience as:

> **The complete apprehension of every determinate truth about reality, including necessary truths, the complete space of metaphysical possibilities, the complete actual history, and all relations among these; where genuinely unsettled future contingents do not constitute missing truths.**

And then leave this open:

> Does complete omniscience additionally require knowledge of counterfactual free choices?

**Open question.**

I currently think our architecture is cleaner without requiring it.

---

# 10. Now goodness needs to be separated into its own project

I strongly agree with your correction here.

We shouldn't sneak goodness into the necessary-agent argument.

Our current progression is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Necessary reality}}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Agent}}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Creation}}
$$

$$
\downarrow
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Autonomous agents}}
$$

and only then:

$$
\boxed{\text{Value / goodness / purpose}}.
$$

That is a **second argument**.

---

# 11. And your distinction between God's goodness and human goodness is extremely important

There are actually at least four things we could mean by "good."

| Concept              | Meaning                                             |
| -------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
| **Good-for-X**       | What benefits/fulfills X's nature                   |
| **Subjective good**  | What X experiences as desirable                     |
| **Human moral good** | What is objectively good for human agents           |
| **Ultimate good**    | What is good relative to the ultimate agent/reality |

These needn't coincide.

For example:

> Pain can be subjectively bad while being instrumentally good for a person.

And:

> Something good for one organism can be bad for another.

So:

$$
\text{experienced badness}
\neq
\text{ultimate badness}.
$$

This gives us considerable room to develop a theodicy later without prematurely declaring suffering "good."

---

# 12. Your "arbitrary preference" proposal deserves more respect

I agree with you that I was too quick to dismiss it.

Suppose God is ultimate.

Then asking:

> "What external standard tells God what is good?"

potentially defeats the purpose of making God ultimate.

So perhaps:

$$
Good(x) \iff R(x,G)
$$

where \(R\) means something like:

> x stands in the appropriate relation to God's nature/purposes.

The obvious objection is:

> Why *that* relation?

But notice what happens.

The naturalist has to answer:

> Why is this fundamental physical/mathematical structure the way it is?

The theist answers:

> Because this is the nature/purpose of the necessary agent.

We then have to decide whether that is a genuine explanation or merely a relabelling.

**That's the actual philosophical battle.**

---

# 13. And there are two radically different versions

### Divine preference

$$
Good(x) \equiv God\ desires(x).
$$

This is straightforward voluntarism.

### Divine nature

$$
Good(x) \equiv x\text{ appropriately realizes/participates in God's nature}.
$$

This is much closer to classical theism.

But there's an important intermediate possibility:

### Divine teleology

$$
Good(x) \equiv x\text{ contributes to the purposes constitutive of God's creative agency}.
$$

This might fit **our** architecture particularly well.

Because our question isn't initially:

> "What commandments did God issue?"

It's:

> **Why did the necessary agent actualize this possibility-space rather than another?**

So goodness becomes connected to **selection criteria**.

---

# 14. This creates a very interesting research question

Suppose there are:

$$
G_1,G_2,G_3,\ldots
$$

possible universes.

The agent chooses:

$$
G^*.
$$

Why?

Possibilities include:

### A. Arbitrary choice

$$
G^*
$$

is simply preferred.

### B. Necessary choice

Only \(G^*\) is compatible with the agent's nature.

### C. Teleological choice

Several worlds are possible, but some better realize the agent's purposes.

### D. Epistemic choice

The agent chooses based on what it knows.

### E. Aesthetic/structural choice

The agent values particular forms of structure.

### F. Agency-oriented choice

The agent values worlds capable of autonomous rational agency.

We shouldn't decide yet.

This is precisely where our metaphysics can branch into competing theories of creation.

---

# 15. And now your point about human goodness becomes useful again

Suppose the agent chooses:

$$
G^*
$$

because it realizes autonomous rational agency.

Then human beings can investigate:

> What actually makes human flourishing possible?

Science, psychology, economics, ethics, anthropology, etc., can become **downstream evidence**.

That is very different from saying:

> "Science proves God's purposes."

Instead:

$$
\text{metaphysical hypothesis}
\rightarrow
\text{prediction about what sort of world we should expect}
$$

and then empirical knowledge tells us whether the resulting picture is plausible.

This is where our earlier discussion about science becomes relevant again.

---

# 16. Your challenge point could therefore become a hypothesis, not a premise

We could hypothesize:

> A universe optimized for autonomous rational agency may require difficulty, uncertainty, delayed reward, scarcity, mortality, and meaningful consequences.

Then we investigate.

If the empirical world shows:

$$
\text{challenge} \rightarrow \text{development}
$$

in many robust domains, that becomes interesting evidence.

But if we discover:

> The same agency could be produced with vastly less suffering and difficulty,

then the hypothesis becomes weaker.

That's exactly how I think we should treat the Peterson-type intuition: **interesting empirical input, not metaphysical authority.**

---

# 17. Now the architecture looks like this

genui{"learning_viz":{"type_id":"COMBINATION_FORMULA","locale_override":"en-US"}}

The visualization isn't particularly useful here, so I would actually ignore it; the ontology is better represented directly:

```text
                 NECESSARY REALITY
                        │
                        ▼
                      AGENT
                        │
             ┌──────────┴──────────┐
             │                     │
      MODAL KNOWLEDGE          NATURE/PURPOSE
             │                     │
             ▼                     ▼
      POSSIBILITY SPACE        THEORY OF GOOD
             │
             ▼
       CREATIVE ACT
             │
             ▼
     FUNDAMENTAL STRUCTURE
             │
             ▼
          UNIVERSE
             │
       ┌─────┴─────┐
       ▼           ▼
   CAUSALITY     AGENCY
                     │
                     ▼
               FREE CHOICE
                     │
                     ▼
               ACTUAL HISTORY
                     │
                     ▼
             DIVINE KNOWLEDGE
```

This is now a genuinely useful map because it tells us **which claims depend on which others**.

---

# 18. And we can now see what remains open

| Question                                                  | Current status                                              |
| --------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| Is there necessary reality?                               | **Open argument**                                           |
| Is necessary reality an agent?                            | **Promising; needs adversarial testing**                    |
| Can an agent actualize a universe?                        | **Conceptually coherent**                                   |
| Must it create rather than select?                        | **Open**                                                    |
| What exactly is selected?                                 | **Candidate: generating structure**                         |
| Must the agent know all consequences?                     | **No; depends on its reason for selection**                 |
| Must it know counterfactual free choices?                 | **Not obviously**                                           |
| Does divine foreknowledge destroy freedom?                | **Not if causal and epistemic necessity are distinguished** |
| Is omniscience temporal?                                  | **Our model leans strongly toward atemporality**            |
| Does atemporality require classical divine simplicity?    | **No**                                                      |
| Is omniscience = knowing every future event?              | **No; definition needs refinement**                         |
| Is goodness external to God?                              | **Probably undesirable for our ontology**                   |
| Is goodness arbitrary divine preference?                  | **Viable; needs development**                               |
| Is goodness grounded in divine nature?                    | **Promising; needs development**                            |
| Is human goodness identical to ultimate goodness?         | **Not assumed**                                             |
| Are evil/suffering necessarily contrary to ultimate good? | **Not assumed**                                             |
| Does God intervene temporally?                            | **Open**                                                    |
| Does omnipotence follow?                                  | **Not yet**                                                 |
| Does omnipresence follow?                                 | **Not yet**                                                 |

---

# 19. One particularly important methodological point

I think we've now reached a stage where we should **stop trying to get to "God" as quickly as possible**.

Instead, we should ask what each layer actually buys us.

For example:

$$
N
$$

doesn't automatically give us:

$$
Agent.
$$

And:

$$
Agent
$$

doesn't automatically give us:

$$
Goodness.
$$

And:

$$
Goodness
$$

doesn't automatically give us:

$$
Omnipotence.
$$

And:

$$
Omniscience
$$

doesn't automatically give us:

$$
Providence.
$$

The argument becomes much stronger if we can show each transition rather than importing the classical package.

---

# 20. The three big questions I think we should attack next

### **A. Necessary agent → creative reason**

This is probably the deepest unresolved part of the ontology.

If the agent is necessary, why does it create anything?

And if it creates, why this possibility-space?

We need to distinguish:

$$
\text{capacity to create}
$$

from

$$
\text{reason to create}.
$$

This is where your idea that **autonomous agency may itself constitute a reason for creation** becomes potentially important.

---

### **B. Modal-structural omniscience → classical omniscience**

We need to rigorously test whether our definition is genuinely sufficient.

Especially:

> Can a being know the actual free choice without making it necessary?

> Can it know the whole temporal structure without itself becoming temporal?

> Can it know all possibilities without possessing Molinist counterfactuals?

> Does "complete apprehension" merely hide the problem in a new word?

That last question is particularly important. We shouldn't let elegant terminology substitute for an argument.

---

### **C. Agent → goodness**

This is the completely separate second thesis.

We need to investigate whether:

$$
Good = \text{God's preference}
$$

is actually coherent and philosophically respectable, and compare it with:

$$
Good = \text{God's nature}
$$

and:

$$
Good = \text{realization of objective teleological structure}.
$$

And then ask whether these three are genuinely different or ultimately equivalent.

---

## And one last point on originality

I would now explicitly keep a **"provenance" column** in our notes.

For every significant proposition:

* **Ancient/classical:** clearly present in Aristotle/Aquinas/Boethius/etc.
* **Modern established:** developed extensively in contemporary philosophy.
* **Synthesis:** existing ingredients combined in a potentially interesting way.
* **Potentially distinctive:** I can't readily identify the same formulation and it deserves literature checking.
* **Actually novel:** only after serious literature review.

At the moment, I would put **modal-structural omniscience + generating-structure providence + libertarian agency** in *Synthesis / potentially distinctive formulation*, **not** "novel." The grounding problem for Molinism and the distinction between divine knowledge and temporal acquisition are absolutely established territory. The work for us is to see whether our particular architecture genuinely solves something rather than merely renaming it.

And I think that is exactly the right standard for what we're doing: **if an idea survives the adversarial process, it stays; if it turns out to be an old idea, that's completely fine; if it collapses, we learn where the ontology fails.**

The next natural move is therefore to take **necessary agent → reason for creation → possibility-space → autonomous agency** and push that chain as hard as possible, while simultaneously testing the proposed definition of omniscience against the strongest foreknowledge/free-will objections. That will tell us whether the architecture is actually holding together or whether we've merely made it sound coherent.
