Yes. I think your latest formulation exposes the exact point where we need to stop using loose language like "God knows the future" and specify **what counts as a truth, when contingent truths become determinate, and what kind of knowledge we're attributing to the agent**.

And I agree with your intuition: the cleanest version of our model is very close to the idea that **an undetermined future does not yet contain a fact about which branch will be actualized**. That is much cleaner than saying God somehow knows a determinate future while insisting that the future is not determined.

There is also an important historical point: this is not an eccentric idea we're inventing. Aristotle's treatment of future contingents, later medieval discussions, open theism, and some modern branching-time approaches all grapple with versions of it. The Boethian alternative instead says that God timelessly apprehends the entire history. ([plato.stanford.edu][1])

But we can now formulate **our own version more precisely**.

---

# 1. The key distinction: possibility, actuality, and truth

Let's use Alice.

At \(t_0\):

> Alice will choose X or Y at \(t_1\).

Suppose both are genuinely possible.

Then we can represent the situation as:

$$
H_0
$$

with two possible continuations:

$$
H_X
\qquad
H_Y
$$

At \(t_0\), there is a fact:

> X is possible.

And another:

> Y is possible.

But there is not necessarily yet a fact:

> Alice chooses X.

Nor:

> Alice chooses Y.

There is a crucial distinction between:

$$
\text{Possibly}(X)
$$

and:

$$
X.
$$

And this is exactly where your intuition about "there is nothing determinate yet" comes in.

---

# 2. This gives us a very clean answer to "what does God know?"

Before the free choice, God knows:

$$
K(\Diamond X)
$$

and:

$$
K(\Diamond Y).
$$

He also knows all the relevant conditions, dispositions, causal structures, etc.

But if Alice's choice is genuinely libertarian and not already determined, there is no proposition:

$$
K(X)
$$

or:

$$
K(Y)
$$

for God to know **as an actual contingent fact**.

This is not ignorance in the ordinary sense.

It is more like:

> **There is no fact there yet to be known.**

That is very close to the position you were already finding compelling.

---

# 3. Then what happens at \(t_1\)?

Alice freely chooses X.

Now the history branches:

$$
H_0
$$

becomes:

$$
H_X.
$$

At this point:

$$
X
$$

is true.

God knows:

$$
K(X).
$$

So we have:

### Before Alice chooses

$$
K(\Diamond X)
$$

$$
K(\Diamond Y)
$$

but not:

$$
K(X)
$$

or:

$$
K(Y).
$$

### After Alice chooses

$$
K(X)
$$

and:

$$
\neg K(Y).
$$

This is precisely the structure you were reaching toward.

And it has a very important consequence:

> **God's knowledge of the actual history does not make Alice's choice free; Alice's free choice makes one branch of the previously open history actual, and divine knowledge tracks that actuality.**

---

# 4. But then you asked the crucial question: "Can the truth change?"

Yes—but we have to be extremely careful about what "truth" means.

There are two competing theories.

### Theory A: Eternal bivalence

Every proposition is eternally either true or false.

Then even:

> "Alice chooses X"

was already true before Alice chose X.

This is roughly the sort of framework in which classical divine foreknowledge becomes difficult.

### Theory B: Open future

Some future-contingent propositions aren't yet true or false.

At \(t_0\):

> "Alice chooses X"

is not yet true.

At \(t_1\):

> "Alice chooses X"

becomes true.

This is close to the Aristotelian/open-future family of solutions. Aristotle famously argued that propositions about some future contingents need not already possess a determinate truth value; contemporary discussions distinguish several sophisticated versions of this idea. ([plato.stanford.edu][2])

**Our model currently wants Theory B.**

---

# 5. And that means something important about "God's knowledge changing"

If God is genuinely atemporal, we should **not** say:

> God didn't know X, then later learned X.

That would make God's cognition temporal.

Instead, we can say:

> At \(t_0\), X was not an actual truth.
>
> At \(t_1\), X became an actual truth.
>
> Divine cognition corresponds to the complete structure of reality, including its actualization.

If God is atemporal, God's relation to the whole history is not itself a sequence:

$$
K_0 \rightarrow K_1 \rightarrow K_2.
$$

Rather, the temporal sequence belongs to the universe:

$$
H_0 \rightarrow H_1 \rightarrow H_2.
$$

God's knowledge is a complete apprehension of whatever determinate reality exists.

This is very close in spirit to the classical Boethian solution, except that **we are refusing to make all future contingents already determinate**. Boethius and Aquinas instead hold that God timelessly apprehends temporal events, so events future for us are present to God's eternal cognition. ([plato.stanford.edu][3])

That is an important difference.

---

# 6. So we have actually separated two questions that were previously mixed

### Question 1: Is God outside time?

Our model:

> **Probably yes, or at least ontologically prior to temporal succession.**

### Question 2: Are future free choices already determinate?

Our model:

> **No.**

Those aren't contradictory.

God can be atemporal while reality itself contains an **open temporal dimension**.

That's a very important conceptual possibility.

---

# 7. This also clarifies why "God knows the actual history" isn't circular

You were rightly suspicious of my previous formulation.

Suppose:

$$
A = \text{Alice freely chooses X}.
$$

We say:

> God's knowledge depends on A.

That does **not** mean:

$$
God's knowledge \rightarrow A.
$$

It means:

$$
A \rightarrow \text{truth of "Alice chooses X"}
\rightarrow K(A).
$$

The epistemic relation follows the truth.

The causal relation does not run backward from knowledge to choice.

A human analogy is imperfect but useful:

Suppose I watch a recording of Alice making a free choice.

My knowledge that she chose X depends on her having chosen X.

My knowledge doesn't cause her choice.

Now remove the temporal limitations of my observation and imagine a cognition that apprehends the entire history directly.

That's roughly the structure we're proposing.

The difference is that God doesn't **discover** the event by watching it happen; the event is simply part of the reality God apprehends.

---

# 8. But there's an important objection

Someone could say:

> "Fine, but if God timelessly knows the whole history, then the whole history exists as a complete object. Doesn't that make Alice's choice fixed?"

This is where we need another distinction:

$$
\textbf{determinate}
\neq
\textbf{determined}.
$$

Once Alice has chosen X, the complete history contains:

$$
X.
$$

So the history is determinate.

But it doesn't follow that Alice was **determined** to choose X.

This is exactly analogous to the difference between:

> "There is a fact about what Alice did."

and:

> "Something forced Alice to do it."

The first doesn't entail the second.

The difficult question is whether an atemporal complete history makes the second secretly true.

That's the genuinely hard metaphysical question.

Our answer would be:

> **No, provided the branching structure is ontologically prior to the actualization and Alice's agency selects among genuinely open alternatives.**

But that needs an actual theory of libertarian agency behind it. We cannot simply declare it.

---

# 9. And this makes our model somewhat different from classical Boethianism

The classical picture is approximately:

$$
\text{complete temporal history}
$$

is eternally present to God.

Our picture is closer to:

$$
\text{possibility structure}
\rightarrow
\text{actualization}
\rightarrow
\text{complete actual history}.
$$

God knows the possibility structure completely.

The actual history is determined progressively through free actions.

But because God is outside temporal succession, he doesn't undergo a temporal process of learning.

This is why I think your instinct that we are somewhere **between classical atemporality and open theism** is right.

Open theism usually gives God changing knowledge as the world develops. ([plato.stanford.edu][1])

Classical Boethianism gives God timeless knowledge of the entire determinate history. ([plato.stanford.edu][3])

Our proposal is:

> **atemporal divine cognition + genuinely open temporal actuality.**

That's a useful formulation.

---

# 10. And now intervention becomes much more interesting

This is where I think your observation is genuinely important for the architecture.

Suppose the universe has developed to:

$$
H_t.
$$

Alice hasn't yet made some free choice.

God intervenes.

There are several possibilities.

### Intervention A — Causal intervention

God modifies some physical condition:

$$
C \rightarrow C'.
$$

That changes the subsequent possibility structure.

For example:

$$
H_t \rightarrow
\begin{cases}
H_X\\
H_Y
\end{cases}
$$

becomes:

$$
H_t' \rightarrow
\begin{cases}
H_Z\\
H_W
\end{cases}
$$

The intervention changes what possibilities are available.

---

### Intervention B — Informational intervention

God gives Alice information.

Now:

$$
I \rightarrow
\text{different epistemic state}
\rightarrow
\text{different possible choices}.
$$

Crucially, this doesn't necessarily determine her choice.

It changes the **reasons available to her**.

This seems particularly interesting for our agency-oriented model.

---

### Intervention C — Environmental intervention

God changes circumstances without directly determining the choice.

For example:

$$
C_1 \rightarrow C_2.
$$

Alice still chooses freely within the new circumstances.

---

### Intervention D — Direct agency intervention

God directly changes a person's will.

This is much more problematic for our libertarian model.

If:

$$
God \rightarrow Alice's choice
$$

in the relevant causal sense, then Alice's libertarian freedom may disappear.

So our model probably wants to distinguish:

$$
\text{influence}
\neq
\text{determination}.
$$

---

### Intervention E — Actualization intervention

The most radical possibility:

God changes which branch of reality is actualized without altering the internal causal laws.

This is metaphysically much stranger, but interesting because it fits the atemporal architecture.

---

# 11. And your point about intervention implying ignorance is exactly right—but only sometimes

Suppose God intervenes because:

> "I want to find out what happens."

Then yes, this suggests epistemic incompleteness.

But:

> "I know exactly what will happen, but I want it to happen."

doesn't.

And:

> "I know every possible outcome but want to actualize this one."

also doesn't.

So intervention does **not** imply ignorance.

It can imply:

* curiosity,
* experimentation,
* play,
* aesthetic preference,
* teleological purpose,
* compassion,
* justice,
* relationship,
* communication,
* actualization of a value,
* or simply preference for one possible history over another.

This gives us a very rich space to explore without prematurely importing Christian theology.

---

# 12. Now let's formalize our preferred omniscience

I think our current definition can be improved.

Instead of:

> "God knows every truth."

—which immediately creates the future-contingent problem—

I'd formulate it as:

> **Omniscience is complete cognitive access to the modal and actual structure of reality: all necessary truths, all metaphysical possibilities, and every determinate fact of the actual history, including each free choice once that choice becomes actual.**

Then:

### Necessary truths

$$
K(\Box P)
$$

### Possibilities

$$
K(\Diamond P)
$$

### Actual determinate facts

$$
K(P)
$$

### Undetermined future contingent

Neither:

$$
K(P)
$$

nor:

$$
K(\neg P)
$$

because neither is yet an actual fact.

This is much more precise.

---

# 13. What we are explicitly rejecting

We are **not** claiming:

$$
Omniscience = \text{knowing every proposition that could ever be formulated}.
$$

Nor:

$$
Omniscience = \text{knowing every future event before it becomes determinate}.
$$

Nor:

$$
Omniscience = \text{Molinist knowledge of every counterfactual free choice}.
$$

Nor:

$$
Omniscience = \text{knowledge acquired sequentially through time}.
$$

And that is actually quite a substantial philosophical position.

---

# 14. Your question about whether all three "reasons for creation" are just relabelling

Yes. I think you're right.

We need to distinguish **levels of description** from genuinely competing explanations.

For example:

> God creates a universe because of God's nature.

could mean:

> God has a nature that generates certain values.

And:

> God creates because it is good.

could mean:

> Those values constitute the good.

And:

> God creates because it serves a purpose.

could mean:

> That purpose expresses those values.

Those may be three descriptions of one underlying structure:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Nature}
\rightarrow
\text{Value}
\rightarrow
\text{Purpose}
\rightarrow
\text{Choice}
}
$$

rather than four independent explanations.

I think **this is the direction we should pursue**.

---

# 15. The genuinely different question is whether the relation is necessary or contingent

This is where your objection about agency becomes important.

Suppose:

> God's nature necessarily entails that God chooses universe \(U\).

Then:

$$
Nature(G) \rightarrow \Box U.
$$

There isn't much choice left.

But suppose:

$$
Nature(G) \rightarrow
\{\text{values/preferences/purposes}\}
$$

and then:

$$
\{\text{values}\} \rightarrow
\{U_1,U_2,U_3,\ldots\}
$$

where several universes satisfy the purpose.

Then God genuinely chooses among them.

That seems much closer to the model you want.

So we can have:

$$
\boxed{\text{necessary nature}}
$$

without:

$$
\boxed{\text{necessary particular choice}}.
$$

That is a very useful distinction.

---

# 16. This also solves part of your concern about "necessary agent" becoming naturalistic

Exactly.

We don't want:

> "God necessarily has property X, therefore God necessarily creates universe Y."

Instead:

> God necessarily has a particular nature, but that nature permits a range of intentional acts.

This gives us:

$$
\text{necessary existence}
+
\text{necessary nature}
+
\text{contingent action}.
$$

That's perfectly coherent in principle.

A human analogy:

> You necessarily have your nature as a person, but your nature doesn't uniquely determine every decision you make.

The analogy obviously doesn't establish divine metaphysics, but it illustrates the logical distinction.

---

# 17. This gives us a much better architecture

I'd now write the model as:

$$
\boxed{\text{Necessary Reality}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Necessary Agent/Nature}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Values/Purposes}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Possibility Space}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Contingent Creative Choice}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Fundamental Structure}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Temporal Universe}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Open Future}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Free Agency}}
$$

↓

$$
\boxed{\text{Actual History}}
$$

while divine cognition encompasses:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Necessary truths}
+
\text{Possibilities}
+
\text{Actual history}
}
$$

without requiring:

$$
\boxed{\text{pre-actual knowledge of future free choices}}.
$$

This is becoming much more internally coherent.

---

# 18. There is also a nice consequence for providence

Providence can now mean:

> **The intentional structuring and, where appropriate, intervention in the unfolding possibility-space without eliminating the genuine agency of creatures.**

That is much richer than:

> God already knows exactly everything that will happen and therefore simply executes a predetermined plan.

It permits:

* creation,
* initial conditions,
* laws,
* constraints,
* opportunities,
* information,
* persuasion,
* revelation,
* environmental changes,
* miracles,
* responses to creaturely choices,

while leaving genuinely free choices genuinely open.

And this gives us a very interesting model of divine-human interaction:

$$
God \leftrightarrow
\text{open history}
\leftrightarrow
human agency.
$$

---

# 19. One warning: we haven't solved the deepest problem yet

We've made the model **coherent enough to investigate**, but we haven't proven it.

The adversary will now say:

> "You are simply redefining omniscience so that God doesn't have to know future contingents."

And that's a serious objection.

Our response cannot be:

> "That's just our preferred definition."

We need an argument that:

1. future free contingents genuinely aren't determinate before their actualization;
2. knowledge requires a truth to be available to be known;
3. therefore there is no epistemic deficiency in failing to know a nonexistent determinate fact;
4. complete knowledge of all possibilities and all actual facts is sufficient for a defensible conception of omniscience.

That is the next philosophical battle.

And interestingly, contemporary philosophy explicitly discusses versions of this position: some open-future theories say future contingents lack truth values, while others allow them to have truth values in more complicated ways. ([plato.stanford.edu][1])

---

# 20. Where this leaves us

I would therefore **lock in** the following provisional commitments:

| Claim                                                                                  | Status                             |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| Future free choices are genuinely open                                                 | **Preferred**                      |
| An open future need not contain determinate truths about which choice occurs           | **Preferred**                      |
| God knows all possibilities                                                            | **Preferred**                      |
| God need not know which free branch will actualize before it does                      | **Preferred**                      |
| God knows the actual choice once actualized                                            | **Preferred**                      |
| Divine knowledge does not causally determine the choice                                | **Preferred**                      |
| God is not temporally ignorant and then temporally informed                            | **Preferred**                      |
| God can be atemporal while temporal reality remains dynamically open                   | **Working hypothesis**             |
| Molinist middle knowledge is unnecessary                                               | **Preferred, but not established** |
| Classical Boethian timeless foreknowledge is insufficient for our model                | **Preferred**                      |
| Providence can involve temporal intervention                                           | **Open and promising**             |
| Intervention need not imply ignorance                                                  | **Established conceptually**       |
| Divine nature, value, purpose and preference may be different aspects of one structure | **Working hypothesis**             |
| Necessary nature need not determine one unique contingent creative act                 | **Preferred**                      |

And I think the most important conceptual sentence to keep is:

> **God's knowledge does not make a free act necessary; rather, the free act determines which contingent fact becomes actual, and divine knowledge perfectly apprehends that actual fact once it exists.**

That is substantially more precise than "God knows the future but doesn't cause it."

And it also makes your original intuition—the strongest part of your objection—central rather than something we have to explain away:

> **If a genuinely free future choice is not yet determinate, there is nothing determinate there for even an omniscient being to know.**

The price is real: we give up exhaustive pre-actual foreknowledge of libertarian choices. But, as you said, **that is a trade-off rather than a defect**, and it may actually give us a much more coherent account of freedom and divine intervention. The philosophical literature treats exactly this trade-off as one of the central alternatives to classical foreknowledge. ([plato.stanford.edu][1])

And I think this is now the point where we should move on rather than keep circling foreknowledge: **take this provisional model as fixed and analyze what kinds of divine intervention are logically available under it**, because that will tell us whether our conception of omniscience, agency, providence, and atemporality actually works together.

[1]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/free-will-foreknowledge/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Foreknowledge and Free Will (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/future-contingents/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Future Contingents (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"
[3]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/medieval-futcont/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Medieval Theories of Future Contingents (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"

And I think we can answer it, which is: while these things are possible, or maybe the question itself is poorly formed for now. I think probably, because the question already I had was: while these things are possible, these two choices to say, does God know all the possibilities? So does God have knowledge of all the possible outcomes of every possible choice? And that seems like a simple question, but again, actually it may not make sense, because God is temporal, right? For God, the knowledge is not like, I know this, and then I know that, and then I know the possibilities, and then I know what is actual. He still may know the possibilities. So I guess the question should be formulated as: does God know all the possibilities, with a distinguishable one as this is the actual, regardless of when it was possible and when it was not? I'm not sure if it makes sense to keep all this reasoning in the prompt. I'm going to keep it, but I don't want it to become so relevant, in the sense that I've found myself reasoning temporally when I shouldn't. So maybe that's useful in some way, but precisely I don't want this argument to be temporal. I want the question and the answer to be consistent with the atemporality nature of the God that we are defining. When I say God, I mean the cosmic agent. I think we can use it interchangeably for now. At some point we will have to formalize everything, and yeah, probably make sense anyway. And that also links to the branching in point eight, because I think we definitely do need a theory of libertarian agency being handed, but I think we are pointed in the right direction. So I think the answer is definitely the one I would give. I find very amusing and interesting your theory and your types of intervention. I think this definitely needs more care and more development. I believe that this is even before goodness. So if we think of the sections or the thesis that we would have, one would be about the nature of this necessary agent. Then the next one would be about the nature of these interventions, if they are possible, what kind there are, which ones are consistent, or which ones we prefer to consider, which ones we're going to study, and so on, and the consequences, the metaphysical properties and consequences, and explanations. And then goodness is probably on top of that, because goodness would be related to how it intervenes, or when, or why it does or it does not intervene. It's related to all interventions, including the creation itself, which could be considered the intervention zero, so to speak. I like your definition of omniscience. It's good that we also separate it from what we are not defining to distinguish ourselves from other positions. So I would accept this one, and possibly this one can lead us to the other two definitions that we are looking for of omnipotence, and then later, when we have omnipotence right, omnipresence as well. Also we are pursuing the direction of the three description with one underline structure. So let's push that underline structure further. Including that aspect of the choice of universe. And also about the necessarily properties to distinguish from that naturalistic point of view. Then very good point on Iting. Honestly, I think at the end of the day, if we go far and deep enough, everything is just a preferred definition and a preferred option. But obviously we can make better arguments, and we should. So I think that is definitely the battle that we should fight. So if we can even build on existing philosophy, that's good, but we need to make it for our own position. So don't be afraid of going further and argument properly. As long as your arguments are actually valid and true metaphysical arguments, that's all right. So thanks for updating the table and some of the definition and conceptual references and sentences, because that will be very helpful to then write it up. For now, I would say you can continue.




Yes. I think we can now make the architecture substantially more precise, and there is an important correction to the way we were previously talking about “before” and “after.”

The central idea I would now propose is:

> **The cosmic agent need not be temporally ignorant of a future choice; rather, it can know the complete actual history without that knowledge being the ground or cause of the free choices contained in that history.**

That sounds paradoxical at first, but I think it becomes much clearer if we stop treating divine knowledge as a sequence of informational updates.

There is also a useful contemporary connection here: the literature distinguishes several positions—Molinism, open theism, Ockhamism, eternalism, etc.—but our developing model does not have to coincide exactly with any one of them. In particular, Molinism gives us a very useful piece—the distinction between possible histories and the actual history—but its strong commitment to pre-creative counterfactuals of creaturely freedom is precisely what we have been reluctant to accept. ([OUP Academic][1])

## 1. First, let's remove the temporal confusion

Suppose there is an agent \(G\), and a universe \(U\).

From Alice's perspective:

$$
t_0 < t_1
$$

At \(t_0\), Alice has not yet chosen. At \(t_1\), she chooses \(X\).

If we ask:

> “Did Alice's choice already have a truth value at \(t_0\)?”

there are two radically different answers available.

### Open-future answer

No.

At \(t_0\),

> “Alice chooses X at \(t_1\)”

is not yet true or false.

There are several genuinely possible continuations:

$$
H_X,\ H_Y,\ H_Z,\ldots
$$

and Alice's free action is what selects which continuation becomes actual.

This is close to the intuition you have been developing.

### Eternalist / atemporal answer

There is a complete spacetime history \(H\), and the proposition

> “Alice chooses X at \(t_1\)”

is simply true in \(H\).

It doesn't become true *when God learns it*. It is true because it is a fact about the actual history.

God's knowledge therefore doesn't have to look like:

$$
\text{ignorance}\rightarrow\text{learning}\rightarrow\text{knowledge}.
$$

Instead:

$$
G \text{ knows } H
$$

as a whole.

This is the classical direction, but it immediately raises the free-will problem you correctly identified.

---

# 2. The crucial distinction: truth, determination and knowledge

I think this is the conceptual key to the whole model.

There are three different relations:

### A. Truth

$$
P \text{ is true}
$$

### B. Causal determination

$$
C \rightarrow P
$$

where \(C\) makes \(P\) inevitable.

### C. Knowledge

$$
K_G(P)
$$

where \(G\) knows \(P\).

These are not equivalent.

In particular:

$$
K_G(P) \not\Rightarrow G\text{ causes }P.
$$

But the more difficult claim is:

$$
K_G(P) \not\Rightarrow P\text{ is causally determined}.
$$

That is exactly what the traditional foreknowledge/free-will debate disputes. The SEP still presents theological fatalism as the central challenge: if an infallible being knew yesterday that you would do X, does that make X unavoidable? ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

The classical response is roughly:

> **Necessity of the truth is not the same thing as necessity of the event.**

If God knows that Alice freely chooses X, then it is necessarily true that:

> If God's knowledge is infallible, Alice chooses X.

But this does **not** automatically mean:

> Alice is causally compelled to choose X.

The difficult part is making that distinction metaphysically robust rather than merely asserting it.

And I think our model can improve on the usual formulation.

---

# 3. The model I think we are converging toward

I would formulate the ontology in terms of **possible histories**.

Let:

$$
\mathcal H = \{H_1,H_2,H_3,\ldots\}
$$

be the set of metaphysically possible complete histories.

One of these is actual:

$$
H^* \in \mathcal H.
$$

The cosmic agent knows:

1. the space of possible histories \(\mathcal H\);
2. the structural laws/constraints defining each history;
3. the actual history \(H^*\).

But importantly:

> **Knowing \(H^*\) does not mean that \(G\)'s knowledge selected every event within \(H^*\).**

We can therefore distinguish:

$$
\boxed{\text{possible histories}}
$$

from

$$
\boxed{\text{actual history}}
$$

and from

$$
\boxed{\text{causal determination within the actual history}}.
$$

These are three different things.

That distinction is extremely important.

---

# 4. Then what happens to Alice?

Suppose two possible histories differ only in Alice's choice:

$$
H_X = \ldots A\text{ chooses }X\ldots
$$

$$
H_Y = \ldots A\text{ chooses }Y\ldots
$$

Both are metaphysically possible.

Now suppose \(H_X\) is actual.

Then God knows:

$$
H_X.
$$

But we should **not** say that God knew, prior to Alice's free act, a proposition of the form:

> “Alice will choose X rather than Y”

in the Molinist sense.

That would reintroduce precisely the problem you were objecting to.

Instead, we can say:

> God knows the entire actual history, including the fact that Alice freely chooses X.

There is no requirement that the knowledge be temporally acquired.

The crucial distinction is:

$$
\boxed{\text{God's knowledge depends epistemically on the actual history}}
$$

without

$$
\boxed{\text{the actual history depending causally on God's knowledge}.}
$$

This is the point I think you were struggling to grasp earlier.

“Depends on” here is **not causal dependence**.

It is closer to a truthmaker relation:

> The fact that Alice chooses X makes the proposition “Alice chooses X” true; God's knowledge tracks that truth.

The direction is therefore:

$$
\text{Alice's actual free action}
\rightarrow
\text{truth}
\rightarrow
\text{divine knowledge}
$$

rather than:

$$
\text{divine knowledge}
\rightarrow
\text{Alice's action}.
$$

But because \(G\) is atemporal, we shouldn't imagine this as a chronological sequence.

It is an **ontological/explanatory ordering**, not a temporal one.

---

# 5. And this answers your earlier question about “does God know all possibilities?”

Yes—under the architecture we're developing, I think the answer should be **yes**, but with a very specific meaning.

God knows:

$$
\boxed{\text{all metaphysically possible histories}}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{\text{which one is actual}}.
$$

But he does **not** necessarily know, independently of actuality, a truth-value for every future free decision.

This gives us an interesting hybrid:

### Possibility knowledge

God knows:

> Alice could choose X.

> Alice could choose Y.

> Alice could choose Z.

### Actuality knowledge

God knows:

> In the actual history, Alice chooses Y.

### Counterfactual knowledge

We **do not automatically assume**:

> If Alice had been in circumstance C, she would freely have chosen X.

That third category is precisely the controversial Molinist addition. Molinism says such counterfactuals are true independently of God's will and available to God prior to creation. ([OUP Academic][1])

Our model does not need that.

And that is a substantial simplification.

---

# 6. This gives us a very interesting alternative to Molinism

We can now formulate the difference quite sharply.

### Molinism

$$
\text{Possible worlds}
+
\text{counterfactual free choices}
\rightarrow
\text{world selection}
\rightarrow
\text{actual history}
$$

God knows:

> “If Alice were in C, she would freely choose X.”

before deciding which world to actualize.

That gives God extremely strong providential control.

But it creates the notorious **grounding problem**:

> What makes the counterfactual true?

Why is it true that Alice *would freely choose X* rather than Y?

And if something prior to her action already determines that truth, does she really have libertarian freedom?

This remains one of the central objections to Molinism. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

### Our model

Instead:

$$
\text{Possible worlds}
\rightarrow
\text{creative selection}
\rightarrow
\text{free actual choices}
\rightarrow
\text{actual history}
$$

and divine knowledge encompasses the resulting actual history.

This sacrifices something:

> God cannot necessarily guarantee every future outcome through knowledge of counterfactual creaturely choices.

But we gain something:

> We don't need unexplained pre-existing truths about what free agents *would* do.

That is a real philosophical trade-off, not merely a relabeling.

---

# 7. But then what exactly does creation select?

This brings us directly back to your earlier point about the **minimal generating set**.

Suppose a universe is characterized by some fundamental structure:

$$
S = \{L,C,I,\ldots\}
$$

where, schematically:

* \(L\) = fundamental laws;
* \(C\) = fundamental constants;
* \(I\) = initial/boundary conditions;
* perhaps additional ontological primitives.

The cosmic agent can choose \(S\).

Then deterministic consequences follow:

$$
S\Rightarrow D_1,D_2,D_3,\ldots
$$

while libertarian choices introduce additional branching:

$$
D_i \Rightarrow
\begin{cases}
A\\
B
\end{cases}
$$

depending on free agency.

So the universe's complete history isn't necessarily something God chooses directly.

Rather:

$$
\boxed{
\text{God chooses the generative structure}
+
\text{creatures generate some contingent history}
}
$$

This is much more compatible with the kind of autonomous agency you have been trying to preserve.

And it also makes our distinction between **creation** and **providence** much cleaner.

---

# 8. We can now classify divine interventions

This is the next section I think we should formalize.

There are at least six radically different things that could be called “intervention”:

| Type                                    | What God does                             | Does it affect free agency? |
| --------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| **1. Actualization**                    | Brings a possible universe into existence | No                          |
| **2. Structural creation**              | Selects laws/constants/initial structure  | Indirectly                  |
| **3. Sustenance**                       | Keeps the universe existent               | Not necessarily             |
| **4. Constraint/boundary intervention** | Changes physical conditions               | Potentially                 |
| **5. Event intervention**               | Causes a particular physical event        | Potentially                 |
| **6. Agency intervention**              | Directly affects a free agent's decision  | Highly problematic          |

And there is an even more interesting seventh:

### 7. Historical intervention

God changes something at \(t_2\), but because the entire history is being considered as a four-dimensional object, the intervention is part of the complete actual history.

This is where your intuition about atemporality becomes particularly interesting.

From inside the universe:

$$
t_1\rightarrow t_2\rightarrow t_3.
$$

From the atemporal perspective:

$$
H =
\{t_1,t_2,t_3,\ldots\}
$$

is simply one complete object.

So an intervention at \(t_2\) isn't necessarily God “changing the past.”

It is God actualizing a history containing that intervention.

That is conceptually much cleaner.

---

# 9. But there is a very important limitation

We must not use this trick to make everything disappear.

If God chooses the entire completed history—including Alice's free decision—then we have simply smuggled determinism back in.

So our model needs a restriction:

> **God's creative act cannot causally determine libertarian choices merely by selecting the complete history containing them.**

Instead, the complete history must contain genuine causal contributions from created agents.

That gives us a rather unusual metaphysical picture:

$$
\boxed{
H^*
=
G\text{-generated structure}
+
\text{created-agent causation}
+
\text{ordinary physical causation}
}
$$

The actual history is therefore **co-produced ontologically**, but not symmetrically.

God provides the ontological framework; created agents contribute genuine originating causal acts.

That fits remarkably well with the agent-causation framework we've been using from O'Connor. O'Connor's work is explicitly concerned with agent causation and the possibility that free choices are not reducible to event-causal chains. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

---

# 10. This also changes what “omniscience” means

I would now propose a more precise definition for our working theory:

> **Omniscience is complete knowledge of every metaphysically possible structure and of the complete actuality of reality, including all truths concerning its contingent history, without requiring that every contingent future proposition possess a determinate truth-value independently of its actualization.**

That is deliberately **not**:

> knowledge of everything that will happen before it happens.

And it is not:

> knowledge of every counterfactual free choice.

It is:

$$
\boxed{
K_G(\mathcal P)
+
K_G(H^*)
}
$$

where:

* \(\mathcal P\) = the complete space of metaphysical possibilities;
* \(H^*\) = the actual history.

This is what I would call, provisionally:

### **Actuality-complete omniscience**

or perhaps

### **modal-complete, actuality-indexed omniscience.**

I prefer the second because it emphasizes the two components.

---

# 11. The surprising consequence

This means God can know something that **we cannot know** without violating libertarian freedom.

We cannot know Alice's future free decision.

But God can know the complete actual history.

Why?

Because our epistemic limitation is temporal and perspectival.

God's isn't.

This is analogous—not identical—to the difference between:

> “What will happen at \(t_2\)?”

and

> “What is the complete history of the universe?”

The second contains the first as a component.

The crucial metaphysical claim is that the second does not therefore **cause** the first.

This is where an eternalist conception of time becomes useful, although we should be careful: eternalism itself does not establish theism, and eternalism plus divine foreknowledge does not automatically solve the free-will problem. It merely gives us the metaphysical architecture in which the problem can be formulated differently.

---

# 12. Now the really interesting question: providence

I think this moves us to the question you wanted us to reach:

> **If God doesn't need Molinist middle knowledge, how can there be providence?**

Our answer can be weaker and, I think, more coherent:

### Providence is not necessarily prediction.

It can be:

$$
\boxed{\text{selection + sustaining + intervention + response}}
$$

rather than:

$$
\boxed{\text{complete pre-programming}}
$$

God can choose a universe whose structure makes certain kinds of agency possible.

He can sustain that universe.

He can intervene under certain conditions.

He can respond to actual free choices.

And he can know the complete actual history.

None of this requires that he have known, independently of those choices, exactly which free choice Alice would make.

This makes providence **interactive rather than deterministic**.

And importantly, that doesn't require open theism's stronger conclusion that God himself is temporally located and discovers reality sequentially. Open theism typically makes the future partly epistemically open to God; our model instead keeps God atemporal while denying that all future free propositions must already have determinate truth values.

That is a fairly distinctive combination.

---

# 13. Where I think our model currently stands

I would freeze the ontology here before adding goodness.

### Layer I — Necessary reality

There exists a necessary ontological ground.

### Layer II — Agenthood

That ground is not merely an impersonal necessary structure but an agent capable of originating contingent effects.

### Layer III — Creation

The agent actualizes a contingent universe/structure from among metaphysically possible structures.

### Layer IV — Generative structure

The created universe contains laws, constants, boundary conditions, etc., from which enormous amounts of its history follow.

### Layer V — Libertarian agency

At least some created agents possess genuine agent-causal powers that are not reducible to deterministic physical causation.

### Layer VI — Possible histories

The complete space of possible histories contains multiple branches corresponding to different free actions.

### Layer VII — Actuality

One complete history is actual.

### Layer VIII — Divine knowledge

The cosmic agent knows both:

$$
\text{the space of possibilities}
$$

and

$$
\text{the actual history}.
$$

But it does not need Molinist counterfactual truths about every free choice.

### Layer IX — Providence

Divine action can include:

* creation,
* sustaining,
* structural intervention,
* physical intervention,
* historical intervention,
* possibly interaction with agents,

without requiring causal determination of their libertarian choices.

### Layer X — Goodness

**Still deliberately open.**

We should not insert goodness yet.

---

# 14. And this exposes the next genuinely difficult question

I think the next question is no longer:

> “Can God know the future and humans be free?”

We have a candidate architecture for that.

The harder question is:

> **What exactly does it mean for God to know the space of possibilities?**

Because there are several increasingly strong notions:

$$
P_1 = \text{know which histories are metaphysically possible}
$$

$$
P_2 = \text{know every possible history in complete detail}
$$

$$
P_3 = \text{know every possible consequence of every possible intervention}
$$

$$
P_4 = \text{know every counterfactual involving free agents}
$$

$$
P_5 = \text{know every logically possible world}
$$

These are **not equivalent**.

And I think our model should probably accept something like \(P_2\), perhaps \(P_3\), while deliberately refusing to assume \(P_4\) unless we find an argument for it.

That is where the Molinist debate becomes genuinely useful rather than something we simply need to “solve.” The contemporary literature continues to dispute whether counterfactuals of creaturely freedom are genuine truths, whether they are grounded, and whether they are compatible with libertarian freedom. ([OUP Academic][3])

---

## One potentially important conceptual contribution

I would **not** yet call this original. There are too many nearby ideas in eternalism, open-future theories, Ockhamism, Molinism, agent causation, and contemporary philosophy of religion.

But there is a synthesis here that is worth preserving:

> **Possibility-complete but counterfactually noncommittal atemporal omniscience.**

That is, roughly:

$$
\boxed{
\text{God knows every possible history}
+
\text{God knows which history is actual}
-
\text{God need not know pre-actual counterfactuals of free choice}
}
$$

combined with:

$$
\boxed{
\text{creation determines the generative structure}
+
\text{created agents genuinely determine some contingent events}
}
$$

This seems to me considerably closer to the architecture you've been intuitively constructing than either classical exhaustive foreknowledge or Molinism.

And it gives us a very clean next task:

**We should now formalize the modal structure itself**—possible universes, possible histories, actualization, free branching, divine intervention, and the exact relation between *possible*, *actual*, *necessary*, *determined*, *known*, and *caused*. Once those are formalized, we can test whether our proposed omniscience really is coherent, rather than relying on intuitions about “knowing the future.” That is also the point at which we can start deriving a coherent definition of **omnipotence** from the same architecture rather than importing the traditional definition.

Really good. Honestly, up to point eight, I like everything and we should keep it In 10 I like actuality complete omniscience better as a name because it sounds better, but it can be also defined more precisely as more complete actuality-indexed omniscience.
