Yes. I think your clarification changes how I would organize the next stage. We should **freeze the parts that are now working**, and then formalize the model rather than continually adding traditional theological attributes.

And I agree with your point about §9: we should not turn a deliberately unexercised possibility into a “problem.” The question is not whether God *could* override agency; it is what follows if we assume that the actual creative choice is to establish a world containing genuine autonomous agents.

There is also a useful contemporary confirmation of the direction we're taking: Katherin Rogers published a 2025 paper specifically combining libertarian free will, divine eternity, and an eternalist/isotemporalist conception of time, arguing that divine providence can be substantial without being absolute. So the combination itself is very much a live philosophical position, even though our particular synthesis need not reproduce hers. ([ojs.uclouvain.be][1])

## 1. First correction: §9 is not a problem for the model

I would now formulate it this way.

We have:

$$
G \rightarrow S
$$

where \(G\) is the cosmic agent and \(S\) is the generative structure of the universe.

Then:

$$
S \rightarrow \text{physical history}
$$

but some parts of the history contain genuine agent-causal events:

$$
A_i \rightarrow \text{choice}_i
$$

where \(A_i\) is an autonomous agent.

So the history is:

$$
\boxed{
H =
H_{\text{God}}
+
H_{\text{physical}}
+
H_{\text{creaturely agency}}
}
$$

but **not as three equivalent causal sources**.

God is the ultimate ontological source of the created reality; physical processes are secondary causes; autonomous agents are genuine originating causes within that reality.

This is essentially the asymmetrical co-production you liked.

And importantly:

> **God could presumably have created a world in which some apparent agents were not genuinely autonomous.**

There is no contradiction in that.

Likewise, God could presumably cause a particular event directly rather than allowing an agent to determine it.

The claim we are currently making is much weaker:

> **The selected generative architecture includes genuine autonomous agency, and therefore not every contingent event is directly determined by God.**

That's a design choice of the model, not an inability of God.

This distinction is important because otherwise we accidentally turn:

> “God chooses to create autonomous agents”

into:

> “God is metaphysically incapable of determining an agent's action.”

We don't need the latter.

---

# 2. So why choose autonomous agency at all?

This is now one of the places where our earlier discussion about the nature of the creative choice becomes important.

We don't know that autonomy was God's reason.

But we have already identified a family of possible reasons:

* agency itself may be intrinsically valuable;
* autonomous agents can generate genuinely novel contingent histories;
* relationships between agents cannot be fully replaced by predetermined states;
* knowledge, deliberation, discovery and moral development require some degree of openness;
* a world containing agents capable of choosing has a fundamentally different ontology from a world consisting exclusively of predetermined processes.

And this gives us a much stronger interpretation of your earlier intuition about “creating beings in God's image.”

It needn't mean:

> God makes little copies of himself.

It could mean:

> **The universe contains entities possessing, at a creaturely level, some structural feature analogous to the divine agent's own agency.**

That is a much more philosophically interesting claim.

We should not yet call it *goodness*. We can postpone that.

---

# 3. The crucial distinction: generative completeness vs historical completeness

I think this is the next refinement our model needs.

Previously we said:

> God chooses the generating set.

But we need to distinguish two kinds of completeness.

### Generative completeness

A set \(S\) is generatively complete if, together with the relevant metaphysical rules, it determines the space of possible histories.

Schematically:

$$
S \Rightarrow \mathcal H
$$

where \(\mathcal H\) is the set of possible histories.

### Historical completeness

A particular history is:

$$
H^* \in \mathcal H.
$$

God knows \(H^*\).

But **knowing the actual history is not the same thing as having selected every event in it**.

That distinction is absolutely central.

So:

$$
\boxed{
G \text{ determines } \mathcal H
}
$$

does not imply:

$$
\boxed{
G \text{ determines } H^*
}
$$

provided that \(\mathcal H\) contains genuine branching produced by autonomous agents.

This is probably the cleanest formulation yet of what we have been trying to say.

---

# 4. And this makes “actuality-complete omniscience” much clearer

I agree with your terminology.

I'd use:

> **Actuality-complete omniscience**

as the name.

And then define it more technically as:

> **Complete actuality-indexed omniscience:** knowledge of every relevant metaphysical possibility together with complete knowledge of which contingent possibilities are actual, without requiring every future libertarian proposition to possess a determinate truth value independently of its actualization.

The short name is much better for prose.

The technical definition prevents misunderstandings.

So our God knows:

$$
\mathcal H
$$

and:

$$
H^*.
$$

But we don't yet require:

$$
\forall H \in \mathcal H,\quad
K_G(\text{what Alice would freely do in }H).
$$

That last step is Molinism.

And this is exactly where we depart from Molinism.

Molinism introduces counterfactuals of creaturely freedom as truths available to God prior to creation; the contemporary literature continues to debate whether those counterfactuals have an adequate grounding and whether they genuinely preserve libertarian freedom. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

Our architecture doesn't need them.

---

# 5. Now: why doesn't God simply choose the whole history?

This is the most important objection to our current formulation.

Suppose:

$$
H^* = \text{the complete actual history}.
$$

Why doesn't God simply say:

> “I choose \(H^*\).”

If God chooses every detail, then Alice's “choice” looks suspiciously like something God chose.

So we need a more precise distinction between:

### Actualization

God selects which **possible generative structure** exists.

and

### Determination

God directly fixes the outcome of a contingent event.

Those are not the same.

Our model therefore says:

$$
G \text{ actualizes } S
$$

rather than:

$$
G \text{ directly actualizes every event }e_i.
$$

Then:

$$
S \Rightarrow \mathcal H
$$

and autonomous agents contribute to determining which member of \(\mathcal H\) becomes actual.

This gives us:

$$
\boxed{
S
\rightarrow
\mathcal H
\rightarrow
H^*
}
$$

where the second arrow is **not exclusively God's causal action**.

That's the sense in which the history is co-produced.

---

# 6. This gives us a very useful notion of “creative sovereignty”

We can now distinguish two kinds of sovereignty:

### Exhaustive sovereignty

God determines every fact.

$$
G \rightarrow \forall e_i
$$

### Generative sovereignty

God determines the fundamental ontological conditions under which contingent events occur.

$$
G \rightarrow S \rightarrow \mathcal H
$$

while some events inside \(H^*\) arise through secondary causes and autonomous agency.

Our model wants the second.

This is actually quite close to the direction of several contemporary attempts to reconcile providence with libertarian agency, although they differ substantially in how much control they give God. The recent Rogers paper, for example, explicitly describes divine providence as substantial but not absolute because created agents have genuine input into how things unfold. ([ojs.uclouvain.be][1])

---

# 7. Now providence becomes less mysterious

I think I see why you found my previous treatment disconnected.

I treated:

> intervention

and

> providence

as two parallel topics.

They shouldn't be.

**Providence is a higher-order concept describing the relationship between divine intention and the unfolding of the created history.**

Interventions are *one possible mechanism of providence*.

So:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Providence}
=
\text{divine relation to the unfolding history}
}
$$

and potentially:

$$
\text{Providence}
=
\begin{cases}
\text{creation}\\
\text{sustenance}\\
\text{non-intervention}\\
\text{structural intervention}\\
\text{event intervention}\\
\text{response to agents}\\
\text{etc.}
\end{cases}
$$

This is much cleaner.

And now we don't actually have to commit to “God has providence” as an axiom.

We can ask:

> **If the cosmic agent has reasons for creating a universe, what relationships between that agent and the resulting history are metaphysically available?**

Providence emerges as one possible family of answers.

---

# 8. Intervention therefore needs its own ontology

I agree with you that we haven't developed this enough.

We need to distinguish at least four dimensions.

### A. What is affected?

* physical state;
* law/structure;
* biological system;
* mental state;
* agent;
* entire history.

### B. How is it affected?

* causal production;
* constraint;
* removal of a constraint;
* selection between possibilities;
* sustenance;
* information;
* direct agency.

### C. When does the intervention exist?

* ontologically prior to the universe;
* atemporally related to the whole history;
* temporally localized within the universe.

### D. What does it change?

* what is possible;
* what is probable;
* what is actual;
* what an agent can do;
* what an agent chooses;
* what consequences follow from a choice.

This gives us a much richer space than simply:

> “God intervenes / doesn't intervene.”

---

# 9. And miracles fit naturally into this

Your earlier definition was good:

> A miracle is an event whose proximate causal structure cannot be accounted for by the ordinary laws/secondary causes of nature, assuming those laws are sufficiently known.

But our framework gives us an additional distinction.

A miracle doesn't necessarily mean:

$$
G \rightarrow \text{violation of reality}.
$$

It could mean:

$$
G \rightarrow \text{event}
$$

where the event has a causal source not contained within the ordinary secondary causal structure.

So the universe remains internally coherent.

We have:

$$
\text{primary causation}
$$

and

$$
\text{secondary causation}.
$$

The miracle is an instance where the former produces an effect not reducible to the latter.

This is much closer to classical metaphysics than the popular conception of “God breaking the laws of physics.”

---

# 10. There is another important distinction: God can determine possibilities without determining actualities

This is perhaps the deepest point we've reached.

Suppose Alice has two possible actions:

$$
A,\ B.
$$

God determines the environment \(E\).

Then:

$$
E \Rightarrow \{A,B\}.
$$

God therefore determines:

> **the available possibility space.**

But Alice determines:

$$
\{A,B\}\rightarrow A
$$

or

$$
\{A,B\}\rightarrow B.
$$

This doesn't mean God is powerless.

He could have chosen a different \(E'\):

$$
E' \Rightarrow \{A,C\}.
$$

So God has enormous **modal control** without necessarily having exhaustive **event control**.

That is a very useful distinction for our model:

$$
\boxed{\text{modal control} \neq \text{event determination}}
$$

And it gives us a possible foundation for a later definition of omnipotence.

---

# 11. It also clarifies your question about non-agentic creatures

Suppose there are three kinds of entities:

### Type A

Fully autonomous agents.

### Type B

Partially autonomous systems.

### Type C

Purely physical processes.

Then God can create a world containing all three.

There is no reason that:

$$
\text{created} \Rightarrow \text{autonomous}.
$$

The interesting claim is merely:

$$
\exists x\;(\text{x is genuinely autonomous}).
$$

So our model doesn't require that God maximize autonomy.

He might create:

$$
\text{stars + rocks + plants + animals + humans + other agents}.
$$

Some are agents, some aren't.

And even an autonomous agent might temporarily cease to exercise autonomy in a particular domain if God directly causes something.

So the architecture doesn't require an absolute autonomy principle.

It requires only that **some genuine agent-causal events exist**.

---

# 12. This gives us a very useful three-level causal architecture

I think we should make this one of the core diagrams of the eventual essay.

### Level 1 — Primary causation

$$
G \rightarrow \text{existence and fundamental structure}
$$

### Level 2 — Secondary natural causation

$$
S \rightarrow \text{physical processes}
$$

### Level 3 — Agent causation

$$
A \rightarrow \text{free action}
$$

with interactions:

$$
S \leftrightarrow A
$$

because agents exist within the physical world and their choices have physical consequences.

So:

$$
\boxed{
G
\rightarrow
S
\rightarrow
\begin{cases}
\text{natural processes}\\
\text{agent environments}
\end{cases}
}
$$

and then:

$$
\boxed{
A
\rightarrow
\text{new contingent events}
}
$$

This is much more precise than simply saying “God creates everything.”

---

# 13. Now we can formulate the exact remaining problem

The remaining question is no longer:

> “How can God create a universe containing free agents?”

We have a coherent candidate answer.

Nor is it:

> “How can God know the actual history?”

We have our actuality-complete omniscience.

The important unresolved question is:

> **What is the metaphysical relationship between the space of possible histories and the actual history?**

More specifically:

$$
\boxed{
\text{What makes }H^*\text{ actual rather than }H_1,H_2,\ldots?
}
$$

There are several possibilities.

### A. God's creative choice

$$
G \rightarrow H^*
$$

But this risks determining free actions.

### B. Creaturely choices alone

$$
\text{agents} \rightarrow H^*
$$

But this cannot explain the initial physical structure.

### C. Joint actualization

$$
G + A_1+A_2+\ldots \rightarrow H^*
$$

This is closest to our current model.

But then we have to explain **how joint actualization works metaphysically**.

And I think that's where we should go next.

---

# 14. A particularly promising idea: history as recursively generated

Instead of imagining God selecting an already-complete history, we can think of:

$$
S_0
$$

as the initial generative structure.

Then:

$$
S_0 \rightarrow S_1
$$

through natural causation and agency,

$$
S_1 \rightarrow S_2
$$

and so forth.

But from God's atemporal perspective, the complete history

$$
H^*=\{S_0,S_1,S_2,\ldots\}
$$

is known as one object.

Thus:

> **God does not need to choose the complete history in order to know the complete history.**

The history is generated internally through multiple levels of causation.

That seems to me the cleanest way of preserving all three things simultaneously:

$$
\boxed{
\text{divine creation}
+
\text{genuine creaturely agency}
+
\text{actuality-complete omniscience}
}
$$

without introducing Molinist counterfactuals.

---

# 15. One more thing I would change in our terminology

I think we should stop saying:

> “God chooses the universe.”

That's now too coarse.

I'd use:

> **God actualizes a generative structure.**

Then later we can ask whether that structure includes:

* laws;
* constants;
* initial conditions;
* dimensionality;
* fields;
* matter;
* consciousness;
* agency;
* other ontological primitives.

And only after that do we ask:

> What histories can emerge from that structure?

That is much closer to the generating-set idea you introduced earlier.

It also avoids the misleading picture of God sitting before a catalogue of fully formed universes and picking Universe #17.

---

## Current architecture

| Component                      | Current position                                                             |
| ------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Necessary reality              | Accepted as working hypothesis                                               |
| Agenthood                      | Accepted as working hypothesis                                               |
| Creation                       | Actualization of a generative structure                                      |
| Generative structure           | Determines possibility space, not necessarily every event                    |
| Possible histories             | Exist as modal alternatives                                                  |
| Actual history                 | One history is actual                                                        |
| Libertarian agency             | Genuine agent-causal contribution                                            |
| Divine knowledge               | **Actuality-complete omniscience**                                           |
| Counterfactual free knowledge  | Not required                                                                 |
| Molinism                       | Useful comparison, not adopted                                               |
| Eternalism                     | Useful temporal/metaphysical architecture, not adopted wholesale             |
| Providence                     | Higher-order relation between divine intention and history; not yet an axiom |
| Intervention                   | Separate metaphysical theory still to be developed                           |
| Omnipotence                    | Deferred, but modal/event distinction will be important                      |
| Omnipresence                   | Deferred                                                                     |
| Goodness                       | Separate subsequent thesis                                                   |
| The reason for creation        | Still open                                                                   |
| Why this generative structure? | Still open                                                                   |

And I think this is a much better stopping point than rushing toward “God.”

The **next thing I would do is formalize the modal/causal architecture mathematically**: define \(S\), \(\mathcal H(S)\), actualization, agent branches, necessary/contingent events, divine knowledge, and intervention. That will let us test whether “generative sovereignty + genuine agency + actuality-complete omniscience” is actually internally coherent, and it will give us the machinery we need later to derive rather than stipulate **omnipotence** and **omnipresence**.

[1]: https://ojs.uclouvain.be/index.php/theologica/article/view/85953?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Can Libertarian Free Will be Reconciled with Divine Providence? | TheoLogica: An International Journal for Philosophy of Religion and Philosophical Theology"
[2]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/free-will-foreknowledge/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Foreknowledge and Free Will (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"



Yeah, these summaries that you're making now sound really clean and clear. I think those are good for a write-up that we'll do next. So, good to keep them like that. And definitely agree on 14 with explain how joint actualization works metaphysically, because I think this can be done. So why not? Sorry, 14 is the answer to this. But I guess you get to have the question of: is there any sort of temporal sequence in a way? Because we say something like God creates the universe, something becomes actual, God intervenes, what's actual will change usually from that intervention. So a question I will leave maybe in the back, because it's more something surrounding a question around how things are actually, and maybe not so fundamental. I'll let you decide that. Is there a sense of temporal events outside our time dimension? Or is just our way to conceptualize because we are time beings and therefore we need to explain that in a sequence. Because that will also change the way God's knowledge is acquired. Again, I don't want this to blurry up the blur, the theory that we are creating that looks pretty neat at the moment and makes sense. Agree on the terminology for 15 as well. Thanks for the table again. So priority agreed of formalizing this model of causal architecture mathematically. And then we can continue with everything else.



Yes. I think this is the right moment to stop adding theological attributes and **formalize the causal architecture we have actually converged on**. That will also clarify the remaining questions about omniscience, free agency, intervention, and temporal sequence.

I also checked the contemporary literature around agent causation and divine foreknowledge, because some of what we are constructing overlaps with existing positions, while some of the particular combinations are less standard. O'Connor's account is especially relevant because he explicitly treats agent causation as an irreducible exercise of causal power rather than as an event being determined by prior events. ([OUP Academic][1])

# I. The model we have so far

Let me state it as a model rather than as an argument for God.

We posit a **necessary cosmic agent** \(G\), and a contingent universe \(U\).

The important point is that \(G\)'s relation to the universe is not initially specified as "God pushes physical events around." We distinguish several logically different forms of dependence.

### 1. Ontological dependence

The universe exists because of \(G\):

$$
G \;\longrightarrow_{\text{ontological}}\; U
$$

This is not ordinary temporal causation.

It means roughly:

> \(U\) would not exist without the causal/creative activity of \(G\).

This is the **creation relation**.

---

### 2. Law-generating dependence

The universe has some fundamental structure:

$$
L = \{L_1,L_2,\ldots,L_n\}
$$

where \(L\) includes whatever constitutes the generating structure of the physical universe: laws, constants, initial/global constraints, perhaps boundary conditions, etc.

We previously introduced the idea that the universe's enormous amount of contingent detail might be generated by a much smaller **generating set**.

So instead of:

$$
G \text{ chooses every event}
$$

we have something much more economical:

$$
G \rightsquigarrow L
$$

and then

$$
L \rightsquigarrow \text{vast physical history}.
$$

That is important because it preserves the asymmetry we wanted:

> **God does not have to determine everything in order to create the universe.**

---

### 3. Ordinary physical causation

Once the physical structure exists, ordinary physical events occur according to whatever laws constitute \(L\):

$$
E_t \rightarrow E_{t+1}
$$

or, more realistically,

$$
S_{t+1}=F_L(S_t).
$$

The function \(F_L\) represents ordinary physical evolution.

But this is not necessarily the whole causal architecture.

---

# II. Agent causation is a different causal primitive

This is where O'Connor becomes particularly useful.

Suppose Alice reaches a genuine libertarian choice between \(A\) and \(B\).

A deterministic event-causal model might look like:

$$
R + B + D + H \rightarrow A
$$

where reasons, beliefs, desires, prior history, etc. determine the action.

A probabilistic event-causal model might instead give:

$$
R,B,D,H \rightarrow P(A)=0.7,\quad P(B)=0.3.
$$

But neither gives Alice quite the kind of authorship we are looking for.

O'Connor's agent-causal picture instead says, schematically:

$$
\boxed{\text{Alice} \rightarrow A}
$$

where Alice herself exercises a causal power that is **not reducible to a prior event determining the choice**. O'Connor explicitly characterizes the difference in terms of how causal capacities are exercised: ordinary event causation produces effects "as a matter of course," whereas an agent's causal capacity enables the agent to determine an outcome within a range of possibilities. ([OUP Academic][1])

This is precisely the structure we have been looking for.

And importantly, contemporary libertarian literature still treats agent-causal libertarianism as a live position, although heavily disputed. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][2])

So our universe is not simply:

$$
L \rightarrow \text{everything}.
$$

It is more like:

$$
\boxed{
L + \text{agent powers} \rightarrow \text{history}
}
$$

with some parts of history determined by ordinary physical causation and some choices originating at the level of agents.

---

# III. The crucial concept: joint actualization

This gives us the concept that I think should become central to the model.

Suppose:

$$
L
$$

determines the physical structure, but Alice possesses genuine agent-causal alternatives.

Then the actual history is neither:

$$
H=F(L)
$$

nor:

$$
H=F(G).
$$

Rather:

$$
\boxed{H = F(L,A_1,A_2,\ldots,A_n)}
$$

where \(A_i\) are actual exercises of agent powers.

But that notation still makes the agents look like ordinary input variables.

So a better formulation is:

$$
\boxed{
H \in \mathcal H(L,\mathcal A)
}
$$

where:

* \(L\) specifies the physical-generative structure;
* \(\mathcal A\) specifies the available agent powers;
* \(\mathcal H\) is the set of histories compatible with both.

The actual history is:

$$
\boxed{H^* \in \mathcal H(L,\mathcal A)}
$$

and is jointly determined by:

1. the physical-generative structure,
2. the causal powers of agents,
3. the actual exercise of those powers.

This is what I mean by **joint actualization**.

And it is asymmetric.

The universe does not choose itself in the same way Alice chooses.

Nor does God determine Alice's choice merely by creating the conditions under which she exists.

We therefore have three distinct forms of determination:

| Relation           | What determines what?                          |
| ------------------ | ---------------------------------------------- |
| Physical causation | Events/states → later events/states            |
| Agent causation    | Agent → self-determined action                 |
| Cosmic creation    | Cosmic agent → existence/structure of universe |

That three-level distinction is becoming one of the central pieces of our architecture.

---

# IV. Now the difficult part: God's knowledge

This is where I think we can finally make the previous discussion considerably more precise.

There are **three different propositions** that tend to get conflated:

1. Alice chooses \(A\).
2. God knows that Alice chooses \(A\).
3. Alice's choosing \(A\) is caused by God's knowing that she chooses \(A\).

The first two can coexist.

The third does **not** follow.

And this can be made mathematically precise.

## The modal mistake

Suppose our actual world is \(w^*\).

In \(w^*\):

$$
A(w^*)=\text{Alice chooses }X.
$$

God knows this:

$$
K_G(A=X).
$$

Now consider another metaphysically possible world \(w'\):

$$
A(w')=\text{Alice chooses }Y.
$$

Then:

$$
K_G(A=X)
$$

is true in \(w^*\), while:

$$
K_G(A=Y)
$$

would be true in \(w'\).

This is crucial.

The proposition

$$
K_G(A=X)
$$

does **not** mean:

$$
\Box(A=X).
$$

It means:

$$
\boxed{K_G(A=X)\text{ is true in the actual world because }A=X\text{ is actual.}}
$$

The modal fallacy would be:

$$
K_G(A=X)
$$

therefore

$$
\Box(A=X).
$$

That inference is invalid.

The correct relationship is:

$$
\boxed{
A=X \rightarrow K_G(A=X)
}
$$

given God's infallibility,

not:

$$
K_G(A=X)\rightarrow A=X
$$

as a causal determination relation.

More precisely, infallibility gives us the truth condition:

$$
K_G(p)\Rightarrow p.
$$

But that does not mean:

$$
K_G(p)\Rightarrow \Box p.
$$

That distinction is absolutely fundamental.

---

# V. But what does God actually know?

Here I think we should preserve your preferred terminology:

## **Actuality-Complete Omniscience**

with the more precise technical formulation:

> **actuality-indexed omniscience**

Let the total possibility space be:

$$
\Omega.
$$

Each possible world is:

$$
w\in\Omega.
$$

The actual world is:

$$
w^*\in\Omega.
$$

God's knowledge contains:

### A. Necessary truths

$$
N
$$

such as logical and mathematical truths, whatever metaphysical necessities we ultimately accept.

### B. Possibilities

$$
\mathcal P(\Omega)
$$

God knows which histories are possible, which are impossible, which structures are compatible with which laws, etc.

### C. Actuality

$$
w^*
$$

God knows which possibility is actual.

So we can represent the knowledge state as:

$$
\boxed{
K_G=(N,\Omega,w^*)
}
$$

with the understanding that \(N\) and \(\Omega\) are themselves richly structured.

This is considerably stronger than ordinary omniscience in one respect and weaker in another.

God knows:

> **everything that is actual, everything necessary, and the relevant space of possibilities.**

But we do **not** require:

> every possible free choice to be eternally fixed by a pre-creative counterfactual fact.

That is exactly where we depart from classical Molinism.

Molinism posits true counterfactuals such as:

$$
C(S,C)\rightarrow X
$$

meaning:

> If person \(S\) were in circumstance \(C\), \(S\) would freely choose \(X\).

And God supposedly knows these counterfactuals prior to the creative decision. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][3])

Our model does **not** need that.

---

# VI. This answers your earlier "was it true yet?" intuition

There are actually **two versions** of our model.

### Version A — temporal/open-future semantics

Before Alice chooses:

$$
A=X
$$

is not yet a determinate truth.

So:

$$
K_G(A=X)
$$

is unavailable.

After Alice chooses:

$$
A=X
$$

becomes true and God knows it.

This is very close to your original intuition:

> **There was nothing determinate there to know.**

It is philosophically coherent, but it makes God in some sense temporally related to the unfolding of reality.

---

### Version B — our preferred atemporal model

We do **not** say that the truth "comes into existence" temporally.

Instead, the complete history is an atemporal object:

$$
H^*.
$$

Alice's choice is one event within that history.

God knows:

$$
H^*.
$$

But the grounding relation is:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Alice's actual choice grounds the truth of the corresponding proposition}
}
$$

rather than:

$$
\boxed{
\text{God's knowledge grounds Alice's choice}.
}
$$

This is much closer to the Boethian architecture, where God does not acquire knowledge sequentially but has a complete grasp of temporal reality. Contemporary discussions of the Boethian solution explicitly characterize it this way. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][4])

So I would now **drop "God didn't know before Alice chose" as a fundamental claim**.

It is useful intuition, but it is too temporal for the architecture we are building.

The more rigorous statement is:

> **Alice's choice is not ontologically determined by God's knowledge of it, even though God's knowledge is infallibly indexed to the actual complete history containing that choice.**

That is cleaner.

---

# VII. Why this does not make Alice's choice less free

We can formulate the asymmetry very sharply.

Consider two possible worlds:

$$
w_1: Alice\rightarrow X
$$

$$
w_2: Alice\rightarrow Y.
$$

Then:

$$
K_G^{w_1}(Alice=X)
$$

while:

$$
K_G^{w_2}(Alice=Y).
$$

God's knowledge **tracks the actual world**.

It does not select the world by being knowledge.

The causal direction is:

$$
\boxed{
Alice\text{'s agency}\rightarrow \text{actual history}\rightarrow \text{truth of proposition}\rightarrow G\text{'s knowledge}
}
$$

not:

$$
G\text{'s knowledge}\rightarrow Alice\text{'s decision}.
$$

But note something subtle:

This is not a temporal causal chain.

The arrows after the actualization relation are partly **grounding/semantic relations**, not temporal causes.

That distinction will become important later.

---

# VIII. So what does "before creation" mean?

This also resolves the problem you raised about pre-creation knowledge.

If \(G\) is genuinely atemporal, then asking:

> "What did God know before creation?"

is potentially malformed.

There is no divine chronological sequence:

$$
K_0\rightarrow K_1\rightarrow K_2.
$$

Instead:

$$
\boxed{
K_G=(N,\Omega,w^*)
}
$$

is a single complete cognitive state.

But \(w^*\) itself is not selected by God's knowledge in the way a person selects an item from a menu.

This is a crucial distinction between:

### **epistemic determination**

and

### **causal determination.**

Knowing which branch is actual does not cause the branch.

---

# IX. This gives us a very interesting definition of providence

Now I think we can finally explain why **providence** belongs in the model rather than being an arbitrary theological addition.

Providence is not identical to creation.

It is the relation between:

$$
G
$$

and the actual history:

$$
H^*.
$$

Creation establishes the generative structure.

Providence concerns the ways in which \(G\) may participate in the realization of particular histories.

So:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Creation} = \text{establishing the possibility-space/generative structure}
}
$$

whereas:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Providence} = \text{relation of }G\text{ to the realization of particular history}
}
$$

This distinction is much cleaner than simply calling everything "God's causation."

---

# X. The intervention taxonomy

We can now formalize the different types of intervention we have been circling around.

Let:

$$
H^*
$$

be the actual history.

An intervention is a modification of the actualization structure.

### Type 0 — Creation

$$
\varnothing \rightarrow L
$$

and ultimately:

$$
L\rightarrow \mathcal H.
$$

Creation establishes the possibility structure itself.

---

### Type 1 — Law-level intervention

Modify the generative laws:

$$
L\rightarrow L'.
$$

This is an enormous intervention because it changes the space of possible physical histories.

---

### Type 2 — Boundary/initial-condition intervention

Keep \(L\) fixed but alter:

$$
I\rightarrow I'.
$$

Then:

$$
H=F_L(I)
$$

becomes:

$$
H'=F_L(I').
$$

This is the "God sets the initial conditions" model.

---

### Type 3 — Event-level physical intervention

At some point \(t\):

$$
S_t\rightarrow S'_t
$$

without changing the fundamental laws.

That is essentially the classical miracle structure.

---

### Type 4 — Agent-level intervention

God modifies an agent's causal situation or causal powers:

$$
A_i\rightarrow A'_i.
$$

For example:

* changing what an agent perceives;
* changing which reasons are available;
* incapacitating an agent;
* enabling an otherwise unavailable action.

This is particularly important because it need **not** violate the agent's autonomy unless God directly determines the choice.

---

### Type 5 — Direct agent determination

God causes:

$$
Alice\rightarrow X
$$

to become effectively:

$$
G\rightarrow X.
$$

Then Alice's agency regarding \(X\) has been bypassed.

This is logically possible in our architecture, but it means that Alice is not the autonomous agent **with respect to that particular action**.

That matches your observation exactly.

There is no contradiction in saying:

> Alice is normally an autonomous agent, but God can intervene in such a way that she does not autonomously produce a particular event.

The interesting question is whether God *chooses* to do this.

---

# XI. Intervention therefore has a measurable metaphysical cost

This gives us a very useful principle.

The more deeply God intervenes, the more of the co-produced history he determines.

We can roughly order the interventions:

$$
\text{creation}
<
\text{law}
<
\text{initial condition}
<
\text{physical event}
<
\text{agent's circumstances}
<
\text{agent's decision}
$$

in terms of how directly they constrain creaturely agency.

This isn't yet a theorem. It is a structural ordering.

And it gives us something very useful later when we develop **goodness and providence**.

We can ask:

> Why would a maximally powerful agent normally prefer lower-intervention strategies?

One possible answer—which we should investigate rather than assume—is:

$$
\boxed{
\text{preserving autonomous agency may itself be a reason for limiting intervention.}
}
$$

That would connect the agency thesis to the later goodness thesis without smuggling goodness into the basic metaphysics.

---

# XII. The deeper mathematical architecture

I think our model can now be written approximately as:

$$
\boxed{
G
\;\rightsquigarrow\;
L
\;\rightsquigarrow\;
\mathcal H
}
$$

where:

* \(G\) = necessary cosmic agent;
* \(L\) = chosen generative structure;
* \(\mathcal H\) = space of physically admissible histories.

Then:

$$
\boxed{
H^*\in\mathcal H
}
$$

is actualized through:

$$
\boxed{
H^*
=
\operatorname{JointAct}(L,\mathcal A,\mathcal I)
}
$$

where:

* \(\mathcal A\) = autonomous-agent powers;
* \(\mathcal I\) = divine interventions, if any.

The crucial point is that:

$$
\operatorname{JointAct}
$$

is **not simply a deterministic function**.

For autonomous agents, it contains primitive agent-causal actualizations.

So we could write:

$$
A_i:
R_i,C_i\rightsquigarrow a_i
$$

where:

* \(R_i\) = reasons/mental state;
* \(C_i\) = circumstances;
* \(a_i\) = actual choice.

The arrow \(\rightsquigarrow\) is intentionally different from ordinary:

$$
\rightarrow
$$

because the latter represents event causation while the former represents **agentive actualization**.

---

# XIII. The complete model in one diagram

I think this is now the cleanest picture:

```text
                 NECESSARY COSMIC AGENT
                          G
                          │
                 ┌────────┴────────┐
                 │                 │
          Generative choice     Intervention
                 │                 │
                 ▼                 │
          Fundamental structure L  │
                 │                 │
                 ▼                 │
        Physical possibility space │
                 │                 │
                 └──────┬──────────┘
                        ▼
                 ┌─────────────┐
                 │   HISTORY   │
                 │     H*      │
                 └─────────────┘
                    ▲       ▲
                    │       │
             physical       │
             causation      │
                    │       │
                    │   agent causation
                    │       │
                    └───┬───┘
                        │
                  AUTONOMOUS AGENTS
```

And epistemically:

```text
                 POSSIBILITY SPACE Ω
                         │
                         │
                         ▼
                   ACTUAL HISTORY H*
                         │
                         │ truth/grounding
                         ▼
                DIVINE KNOWLEDGE K_G
```

The two diagrams are deliberately different.

**Causation** goes one way.

**Knowledge** tracks actuality.

That prevents us from confusing:

> "God knows what happens"

with:

> "God makes what happens happen."

---

# XIV. Where this leaves omniscience

I would now make our working definition:

> **Actuality-complete omniscience:** the cosmic agent possesses complete and infallible knowledge of all necessary truths, the complete relevant modal structure of reality, and the complete actual history, including the outcomes of genuinely free agent-causal acts, without those acts being causally determined by the divine knowledge that represents them.

This is stronger than simply saying "God knows everything."

It tells us **what everything means**.

And it deliberately does *not* require:

* foreknowledge as temporal prediction;
* Molinist middle knowledge;
* determination of libertarian choices;
* knowledge being acquired sequentially;
* every possible counterfactual of freedom having a determinate truth value.

Molinism remains extremely relevant as a comparison because it solves the providence problem by giving God counterfactual knowledge of free choices, but our architecture deliberately avoids making those counterfactuals primitive. ([Enciclopedia de Filosofía de Stanford][3])

---

# XV. The big remaining question

And I think this reveals the **real** next philosophical problem.

We have essentially solved one problem:

> **How can an atemporal God know an actual free choice without causing it?**

Answer:

$$
\boxed{
\text{knowledge is actuality-indexed, not choice-determining.}
}
$$

But we haven't yet solved:

> **What exactly is the modal structure that God knows?**

In other words:

$$
\boxed{\text{What is } \Omega\text{?}}
$$

Are all metaphysically possible histories genuinely possible?

Are possible worlds fundamental objects?

Are they merely descriptions?

Are possibilities grounded in the natures/powers of things?

Does God know:

$$
\{w_1,w_2,w_3,\ldots\}
$$

as an abstract space?

Or does God know possibilities **structurally**, through the powers and constraints that generate them?

That question matters enormously because it connects directly to our earlier discussion of **mathematical structure and naturalism**.

I think our model should probably avoid reifying possible universes unnecessarily. We can instead try to construct possibility structurally:

$$
\boxed{
\Omega = \text{the space of histories permitted by }(L,\mathcal A,\text{metaphysical constraints})
}
$$

rather than saying that God somehow has a warehouse containing infinitely many pre-existing universes.

That is a major simplification.

---

## Current architecture

| Component                 | Working position                      | Status                        |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| Necessary reality         | Cosmic agent \(G\)                    | **Adopted provisionally**     |
| Creation                  | Establishment of generative structure | **Strong**                    |
| Fundamental laws          | Generating set \(L\)                  | **Strong/provisional**        |
| Physical causation        | Event/state causation                 | **Adopted**                   |
| Libertarian agency        | Agent causation                       | **Preferred**                 |
| Actual history            | Jointly actualized                    | **Preferred**                 |
| Divine knowledge          | Actuality-complete                    | **Adopted provisionally**     |
| Divine knowledge → choice | Not causally determining              | **Core principle**            |
| Possibilities             | Structured modal space \(\Omega\)     | **Open**                      |
| Providence                | Relation of \(G\) to actualization    | **Useful, needs development** |
| Intervention              | Multiple levels                       | **Needs development**         |
| Omnipotence               | Not yet defined                       | **Open**                      |
| Omnipresence              | Not yet defined                       | **Open**                      |
| Goodness                  | Separate thesis                       | **Open**                      |
| Traditional attributes    | To be derived, not assumed            | **Open**                      |

And importantly, **this is not yet "classical theism."** We have deliberately not assumed omnipotence, perfect goodness, omnipresence, or even the classical doctrine of divine simplicity. We are constructing a candidate metaphysical architecture from the bottom up.

The next natural step, therefore, is exactly the one you suggested: **formalize \(\Omega\), \(L\), agent-causal actualization, and intervention as one coherent causal/modal system**. Once that is done, we can test whether omnipotence and omnipresence actually follow naturally from the architecture—or whether they require additional substantive assumptions. That will also put us in a much better position to tackle goodness without using it prematurely to explain providence.
