[3]: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/fall2022/entries/free-will-foreknowledge/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Foreknowledge and Free Will (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy/Fall 2022 Edition)"
[4]: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/free-will-foreknowledge/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Foreknowledge and Free Will (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)"




I'm not sure this remaining question. So I think that's also important to solve, or at least to leave it in the table as something that we need to solve. Because I'm not sure if the next formalization will solve it or not. So I'll let you choose what you do next, but I think one of the purposes of the table is precisely that we know what we have to work on next. So yeah, follow on that.




Exactly. I would **not treat the modal-space question as something that automatically disappears once we formalize the causal architecture**. It is a separate foundational question, although the formalization will constrain the answer.

So I would revise the roadmap rather than simply moving on.

## 1. What we should formalize first

I suggest doing the next step in two layers:

### Layer A — causal architecture

We formalize:

$$
G,\quad L,\quad \mathcal A,\quad H,\quad I
$$

where:

* \(G\): necessary cosmic agent;
* \(L\): fundamental generative structure;
* \(\mathcal A\): agent-causal powers;
* \(H\): histories;
* \(I\): possible divine interventions.

The aim is to answer:

> **How can a universe containing both ordinary physical causation and genuinely autonomous agent causation constitute one coherent causal history?**

This should give us a rigorous account of **joint actualization**.

### Layer B — modal architecture

Separately, we formalize:

$$
\Omega
$$

the space of possibilities.

But rather than immediately deciding that \(\Omega\) is a collection of "possible universes," we ask:

> **What makes a possibility a possibility?**

That is the deeper question.

---

# 2. I think there are actually three different questions hiding in \(\Omega\)

This is worth separating because otherwise we risk treating them as one problem.

### Q1. What is metaphysically possible?

For example:

> Could the electron have had a different charge?

> Could the laws of physics have been different?

> Could Alice have chosen differently?

These are questions about **modal truth**.

---

### Q2. What constitutes a possible history?

Suppose we have:

$$
L+\mathcal A.
$$

Does that determine a space:

$$
\Omega(L,\mathcal A)
$$

of possible histories?

And if so, how?

For example, perhaps:

$$
\Omega(L,\mathcal A)
=
\{H: H\text{ is compatible with }L\text{ and the causal powers of the agents}\}.
$$

This would make possibilities **structural**, rather than requiring possible worlds to exist as entities.

---

### Q3. What does God know when God knows a possibility?

This is an epistemological/ontological question.

If God knows:

> Alice could choose X or Y,

what exactly is present in divine knowledge?

Is God contemplating two possible universes?

Or does God simply know the causal/modal structure that makes both actions possible?

The second option is considerably more attractive to me for our model.

---

# 3. This may actually simplify the problem considerably

I think we should resist saying:

> There are infinitely many universes, and God knows all of them.

We don't need that.

Consider an ordinary chess position.

There are enormous numbers of possible future games, but we don't normally say that all those games have to **exist** somewhere.

There is a **structure of possibilities** generated by:

* the current position,
* the rules,
* the legal moves.

Likewise, perhaps:

$$
\boxed{
\text{possibility} \neq \text{existing possible object}.
}
$$

A possible history could instead mean:

$$
\boxed{
\text{a history that can be generated without violating the relevant constraints}.
}
$$

That gives us a much more parsimonious ontology.

And it fits your earlier criticism of Platonism: we don't need to populate reality with an additional realm of mathematical or possible objects merely because we can reason about them.

---

# 4. But then we have a very interesting question about libertarian agency

Suppose Alice can choose:

$$
X\quad\text{or}\quad Y.
$$

We don't necessarily need two pre-existing histories.

We can say:

$$
\mathcal A_{\text{Alice}}
\vdash
\{X,Y\}
$$

meaning that Alice's causal powers make both outcomes genuinely available.

Then her actual exercise of agency produces:

$$
X.
$$

So:

$$
\boxed{
\text{possibility is grounded in causal power}
}
$$

rather than:

$$
\boxed{
\text{causal power is grounded in a pre-existing possible world}.
}
$$

That is potentially a very important move for our architecture.

It also makes the O'Connor-style agent causation fit much more naturally.

---

# 5. And it gives us a possible hierarchy of modality

We might eventually distinguish:

| Level        | Possibility                                        |
| ------------ | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Logical      | Doesn't contradict logic                           |
| Metaphysical | Doesn't contradict the nature/structure of reality |
| Physical     | Compatible with \(L\)                              |
| Agentive     | Compatible with the causal powers of an agent      |
| Historical   | Compatible with the complete preceding structure   |

So, for example:

> Alice choosing to fly unaided

might be logically conceivable but physically impossible.

> Alice choosing X instead of Y

might be physically possible and agentively possible.

> Alice choosing X in a history where she never existed

is historically impossible.

This gives us something much richer than simply:

$$
\Omega=\{\text{possible universes}\}.
$$

---

# 6. This also changes how we should think about God's knowledge

Our preferred formulation could eventually become something like:

$$
K_G =
\big(
\text{necessary structure},
\text{modal structure},
\text{actual structure}
\big).
$$

But "modal structure" need not mean:

> God knows every possible universe individually.

It could mean:

> God knows completely the generative powers, constraints, and relations that constitute what can and cannot happen.

Then actuality is distinguished within that structure.

So:

$$
\boxed{
\text{possibility} \quad+\quad \text{actuality}
}
$$

rather than:

$$
\boxed{
\text{possible universes} \quad+\quad \text{one selected universe}.
}
$$

That is, I think, philosophically cleaner.

---

# 7. But there is an important adversarial objection

We shouldn't declare victory yet.

A naturalist can ask:

> Why does this "modal structure" exist at all?

And here we encounter exactly the question we've been postponing.

Perhaps modal truths are simply brute facts.

Perhaps they are grounded in logical structure.

Perhaps they are grounded in mathematical structure.

Perhaps they are grounded in the natures of things.

Perhaps they are merely descriptions of what would happen under counterfactual conditions.

Perhaps there is no additional ontology at all.

And the theist cannot simply say:

> "God knows the modal structure."

because then we've merely renamed the problem.

We eventually need to ask:

$$
\boxed{
\text{What grounds modality?}
}
$$

And that belongs explicitly in our table.

---

# 8. Updated roadmap

I would therefore change our table to this:

| Component                         | Working position                                           | Status                          |
| --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| Necessary reality                 | Cosmic agent \(G\)                                         | **Preferred provisional model** |
| Creation                          | Establishment of generative structure                      | **Strong**                      |
| Fundamental structure \(L\)       | Generating set of the universe                             | **Preferred**                   |
| Physical causation                | Ordinary event/state causation                             | **Adopted**                     |
| Agent causation                   | Irreducible autonomous causation                           | **Preferred**                   |
| Joint actualization               | Physical + agent causal production of \(H^*\)              | **Needs formalization**         |
| Divine intervention               | Multiple causal levels                                     | **Needs formalization**         |
| **Possibility**                   | Structured rather than necessarily reified                 | **Open**                        |
| **Ground of modality**            | What makes possibilities possible?                         | **Open — foundational**         |
| Possible histories \(\Omega\)     | To be derived from structure/powers                        | **Open**                        |
| Divine knowledge                  | Actuality-complete omniscience                             | **Preferred**                   |
| Divine knowledge of possibilities | Structural/modal knowledge                                 | **Open**                        |
| Free will + divine knowledge      | Non-causal actuality-indexing                              | **Strong provisional solution** |
| Providence                        | Divine relation to actualization                           | **Useful; needs integration**   |
| Omnipotence                       | To derive after causal architecture                        | **Open**                        |
| Omnipresence                      | To derive after ontology                                   | **Open**                        |
| Goodness                          | Separate thesis                                            | **Open**                        |
| Divine nature                     | Why this necessary agent rather than impersonal necessity? | **Foundational open question**  |
| Traditional theism                | To be derived, not assumed                                 | **Open**                        |

And I would add one more column in the eventual write-up:

**Origin / provenance of the claim** — whether it comes from logical necessity, empirical observation, metaphysical inference, a modeling choice, theological tradition, or an explicitly adopted preference.

That will help us avoid precisely the problem you've repeatedly pointed out: accidentally turning a convenient modeling decision into something that looks like a deduction.

---

## 9. So the immediate sequence I'd use

I would **not** jump straight to omnipotence.

I'd do:

### A. Formal causal architecture

Define \(G,L,\mathcal A,H,I\) and joint actualization.

### B. Formal modal architecture

Ask what \(\Omega\) actually is and whether it can be generated from \(L+\mathcal A\), rather than assumed as a realm of possible worlds.

### C. Ground of modality

Attack the naturalist and theist alternatives:

* brute modal facts;
* logicalism;
* mathematical structuralism;
* dispositionalism/powers;
* modal realism;
* divine conceptualism;
* divine grounding;
* combinations.

This is where our earlier **"why is necessary mathematical structure metaphysically sufficient?"** question properly belongs.

### D. Revisit actuality-complete omniscience

Once \(\Omega\) is defined, we can state exactly what it means for \(G\) to know:

$$
\Omega + H^*
$$

without accidentally smuggling temporal succession into an atemporal being.

### E. Intervention

Then ask what modifications of \(H^*\) are metaphysically possible, and what each does to \(L\), agency, and actuality.

### F. Omnipotence

Now we can define the maximal scope of \(G\)'s causal powers relative to this architecture.

### G. Omnipresence

Only after we know what kind of entity \(G\) is and what "location" means for it.

### H. Goodness

Then, separately, ask why the actualization/intervention policy of \(G\) should count as good.

---

I think this ordering is substantially better than the previous one because **possibility is no longer an unexplained box called \(\Omega\)**. We explicitly acknowledge that we have to explain both *what possibilities are* and *what grounds them*. And importantly, we don't assume that solving that question will necessarily vindicate either naturalism or theism.

The next step should therefore be the **formal causal architecture and the modal architecture together**, because they constrain each other: agent powers help determine what possibilities mean, while the modal structure tells us what an agent could actually do. Once we have that, we can attack the grounding of modality as its own philosophical battle rather than hiding it inside the notation.





Okay, I think the information is definitely cleaner, but I would not rule out the possibility of God knowing the actual possibilities. So, our position is that it does know possibility in actuality, but I wouldn't necessarily rule out that he knows more than that, because that seems to be reasonable. I don't know. It doesn't contradict our notion of omniscience, I think. So let's keep that, like an extra possibility to explore if it doesn't contradict it or doesn't add too much extra ontology. Just not as necessarily. I would say your point six is, like, the clean minimum that we need. I think we already discussed that it was relevant to add this column of origin and provenance, so it should have been added already. Other than that, happy for you to continue in order for the corpus to have a consistent growth and metaphysically coherent structure.




Yes. I think we have reached the point where **formalizing the causal architecture is more valuable than adding another philosophical layer**. It will also tell us whether the remaining questions about sequence, intervention, agency, and omniscience are genuine problems or merely artifacts of temporal language.

I would therefore freeze the current conceptual commitments provisionally and build the model from them.

## 1. The minimal architecture we have actually committed to

Let me distinguish what we **need**, what we **prefer**, and what remains open.

| Component             | Current formulation                                                                               | Status                    | Provenance                          |
| --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------- | ----------------------------------- |
| Reality               | There is a fundamental reality underlying contingent physical reality                             | Working assumption        | Cosmological/metaphysical argument  |
| Necessity             | The fundamental reality is necessary in the relevant metaphysical sense                           | Preferred                 | O'Connor + cosmological tradition   |
| Agency                | This necessary reality has genuine agent-causal capacity                                          | Preferred/argued          | O'Connor + libertarian agency       |
| Creation              | The agent can actualize a contingent universe/world-structure                                     | Working hypothesis        | Extension of agent-causation        |
| Generating structure  | The actual universe is determined by some generating set of fundamental conditions/laws/structure | Strong working hypothesis | Our causal analysis                 |
| Autonomy              | Some creatures can possess genuine libertarian agency                                             | Preferred                 | Agent-causal model                  |
| Actuality             | One possible history is actual                                                                    | Required                  | Basic modal architecture            |
| Possibility           | Non-actual possibilities need not be actual entities                                              | Preferred                 | Ontological economy                 |
| Divine knowledge      | At minimum, the agent knows the complete actual history                                           | **Accepted minimum**      | Our development                     |
| Possibility knowledge | The agent may know more than the actual possibilities                                             | **Open**                  | Your latest qualification           |
| Divine intervention   | Intervention can modify what becomes actual without requiring ignorance                           | Working hypothesis        | Our model                           |
| Providence            | To be investigated as a structural relation between divine agency and creaturely agency           | Open/secondary            | Classical theism + our architecture |
| Goodness              | Separate thesis concerning the nature/reasons of divine choice                                    | Deferred                  | Our methodological decision         |
| Omnipotence           | To be derived from the causal architecture                                                        | Deferred                  | Classical theology + our model      |
| Omnipresence          | To be derived after the preceding concepts                                                        | Deferred                  | Classical theology + our model      |

The important change is exactly the one you just made:

> **Actuality-complete omniscience is the minimum requirement, not the maximum.**

So our model does **not** say:

> God knows only what is actual.

It says:

> God necessarily knows everything that is actual; whether divine knowledge additionally encompasses some or all non-actual possibilities remains open.

That is a much cleaner position.

---

# 2. The causal architecture

I suggest we stop thinking initially in terms of "God chooses universe A, then Alice chooses X, then God learns X."

That language is useful psychologically but risks smuggling temporal succession into the ontology.

Instead, let's represent reality structurally.

Let:

$$
\mathcal W
$$

be the space of metaphysically possible worlds.

A world \(w\in\mathcal W\) contains, among other things:

$$
w=(L,C,H,A,\ldots)
$$

where:

* \(L\) = fundamental laws/structural principles,
* \(C\) = initial or boundary conditions,
* \(H\) = the resulting history,
* \(A\) = the actions of autonomous agents.

This is deliberately schematic. The important point is that **a world is not merely its initial conditions**.

That addresses one of the questions you raised earlier.

If God selects only \(L\), then the subsequent history may not be uniquely fixed because autonomous agency introduces genuine branching.

So we shouldn't say:

> God chooses the universe.

without qualification.

We should distinguish at least:

### Creation/actualization of structure

God actualizes some fundamental structure:

$$
G \rightarrow S
$$

where \(S\) might include laws, constants, topology, dimensional structure, boundary conditions, etc.

But then:

$$
S \not\Rightarrow H
$$

in the strong deterministic sense if libertarian agents exist.

Rather:

$$
S + \text{agent choices} \rightarrow H
$$

The actual history is therefore **jointly constituted**.

This gives us the phrase we have already been converging toward:

> **The history is co-produced, but not symmetrically.**

God supplies the ontological framework and potentially intervenes within it; autonomous creatures contribute genuinely underived choices.

---

# 3. A more rigorous notion of branching

Suppose Alice reaches a genuine libertarian choice.

At some point in the structure we have:

$$
A_0 \leadsto
\begin{cases}
A_1\\
A_2\\
A_3\\
\vdots
\end{cases}
$$

These are not necessarily different *universes* in the ontological sense.

They are **possible continuations of the same history up to the choice-point**.

Let:

$$
H_{\leq t}
$$

be everything prior to Alice's choice.

Then there is a set:

$$
\mathrm{Cont}(H_{\leq t})
$$

of possible continuations.

Alice's agency selects one:

$$
a:\mathrm{Cont}(H_{\leq t})\rightarrow H
$$

more precisely, her exercise of agency is part of what determines which continuation becomes actual.

The key point is:

$$
\text{possible}(X) \neq \text{actual}(X)
$$

and:

$$
\text{knowing that }X\text{ is possible}
\neq
\text{knowing that }X\text{ will become actual}.
$$

That distinction is going to be absolutely central to our conception of omniscience.

---

# 4. Now we can state the knowledge problem much more cleanly

Your original difficulty was essentially:

> If God knows beforehand that Alice will choose X, how can Alice genuinely choose X?

There are actually **three different propositions** hiding inside that sentence.

### P1 — Possibility

> Alice can choose X.

Formally:

$$
\Diamond(Alice=X)
$$

### P2 — Actuality

> Alice chooses X.

$$
Actual(Alice=X)
$$

### P3 — Divine knowledge

> God knows that Alice chooses X.

$$
K_G(Actual(Alice=X))
$$

The problematic inference is:

$$
K_G(P2)\Rightarrow \neg Free(Alice)
$$

But that inference does **not** follow merely from the semantics of knowledge.

The real question is:

> What makes P2 true?

If the answer is:

> God's prior causal determination makes P2 true,

then yes, libertarian freedom disappears.

But our model instead says:

> Alice's free action is what makes P2 true within the actual history.

So:

$$
Free(Alice=X)\rightarrow Actual(Alice=X)
$$

and therefore:

$$
Actual(Alice=X)\rightarrow K_G(Actual(Alice=X)).
$$

The direction of metaphysical dependence is crucial.

God's knowledge tracks actuality; it does not constitute actuality.

---

# 5. But what about atemporality?

This is where I think your latest formulation is particularly useful.

We should **not** say:

> God first knows the possibilities, then Alice chooses, then God learns what Alice chose.

That would give God a temporal epistemic process, which we don't currently want.

Instead, we can formulate divine knowledge as an **indexing relation over actuality**.

There is a total actual history:

$$
H^*
$$

and God has complete knowledge of:

$$
H^*.
$$

That does not imply that God's knowledge is acquired sequentially.

From the divine standpoint, we can simply have:

$$
K_G(H^*).
$$

Human beings experience:

$$
t_1<t_2<t_3<\cdots
$$

and therefore describe:

> "Alice hasn't chosen yet."

But from the atemporal perspective, the relevant proposition is simply:

$$
Alice(H^*)=X.
$$

There is no requirement that God's knowledge itself undergo:

$$
K_1\rightarrow K_2\rightarrow K_3.
$$

This is one reason **actuality-complete omniscience** is a good name.

It doesn't say:

> God knows only what humans would call "presently actual."

It says:

> God's knowledge completely indexes the actual reality, without requiring the knowledge itself to be temporally updated.

---

# 6. And this gives us a nice solution to the "truth before existence" issue

Your earlier intuition—

> "It isn't known because it isn't true yet"

—is actually very useful, provided we are careful about what "yet" means.

From the creature's temporal perspective:

$$
t<t_A
$$

and Alice's future choice isn't yet actual.

So there is no fact:

$$
Alice(t_A)=X
$$

*within the temporal history available at \(t\)*.

But from the atemporal divine perspective, the entire actual history can be represented as:

$$
H^*=\{A(t_1),A(t_2),...,A(t_A),...\}.
$$

God doesn't have to **wait** until \(t_A\) to acquire the information.

Rather, God's knowledge is indexed to the entire actual history.

This gives us an important distinction:

### Temporal epistemology

Knowledge can be acquired as events occur.

### Atemporal divine knowledge

Knowledge can correspond to the entire actual structure without being an event of acquisition.

That is considerably cleaner than saying:

> God knew beforehand.

"Beforehand" is precisely the misleading word.

---

# 7. But now we get your genuinely difficult question

And I think this is the next major issue we should formally attack.

Suppose God knows all possible continuations.

For Alice:

$$
\{X,Y,Z\}.
$$

Suppose God also knows the actual history:

$$
X.
$$

Then:

$$
K_G(\Diamond X)
$$

and

$$
K_G(\Diamond Y)
$$

and

$$
K_G(\Diamond Z)
$$

but also:

$$
K_G(Actual(X)).
$$

Does that make \(X\) necessary?

No.

Because:

$$
\Diamond X
$$

does not mean:

$$
\Box X.
$$

And divine knowledge doesn't change the modal status.

The proposition can be:

$$
\Diamond X \land Actual(X) \land K_G(X)
$$

while remaining:

$$
\neg\Box X.
$$

In plain language:

> God knows that Alice actually chooses X, but X was not metaphysically necessary.

This is the precise version of the intuition you were defending.

The fact that a proposition is necessarily known by a perfect knower does **not** automatically make the proposition itself necessary.

The difficult argument is instead:

> If God couldn't have been wrong, and God knew X, then Alice couldn't have done otherwise.

That is the famous **modal/fixity problem**.

And here we have to be very careful: our model has not yet *proved* that libertarian freedom survives that argument. We have only isolated exactly where the argument lives.

That is good progress, because now we can attack it directly rather than talking vaguely about "foreknowledge."

---

# 8. Molinism now becomes much easier to compare

Molinism introduces something like:

> Given circumstances C and a free agent A, A would freely choose X.

These are the famous **counterfactuals of creaturely freedom**.

Schematically:

$$
C \square\!\!\rightarrow (A=X)
$$

meaning approximately:

> If C were the case, Alice would freely choose X.

God can then choose a world knowing which free choices would occur under different circumstances.

That gives God enormous providential control without directly determining Alice's choices.

But it creates a major metaphysical question:

> **What makes these counterfactuals true?**

They cannot simply be God's decision, because then God's knowledge would determine the truth.

And they cannot straightforwardly be Alice's actual decision, because we're talking about counterfactual situations that may never become actual.

This is the famous **grounding problem for middle knowledge**.

Our model currently does **not** need Molinism.

That's important.

We can retain:

$$
K_G(\text{all relevant possibilities})
$$

without requiring:

$$
K_G(\text{every counterfactual free choice}).
$$

Whether we *want* to add the latter remains an open question.

And that fits your preference perfectly: **don't add ontology until the architecture requires it.**

---

# 9. Intervention becomes much more interesting now

We can now distinguish several fundamentally different kinds of divine action.

### Type I — Structural actualization

God determines/actualizes:

$$
S
$$

the fundamental structure.

This is "creation" in the strongest sense.

---

### Type II — Boundary-condition intervention

God determines something about the initial/boundary state:

$$
S+C.
$$

This can radically influence subsequent history without determining every event.

---

### Type III — Law-level intervention

God modifies the ordinary causal structure:

$$
L\rightarrow L'
$$

or temporarily suspends its operation.

This is a candidate definition of a **miracle**:

> an event whose causal sufficient conditions are not exhausted by the ordinary secondary causal structure of the physical world.

This is much better than "something scientifically impossible," because science never has epistemic certainty about the complete laws of nature.

---

### Type IV — State intervention

God changes some physical state:

$$
X_t\rightarrow X'_t.
$$

This is intervention within the existing laws/structure.

---

### Type V — Agent-directed intervention

God changes the circumstances of an agent without determining the agent's choice:

$$
C\rightarrow C'
$$

while preserving:

$$
Free(A).
$$

This is particularly interesting for our model.

God could influence Alice without choosing for Alice.

---

### Type VI — Agency intervention

God directly determines what Alice does:

$$
Free(A)\rightarrow \neg Free(A)
$$

for that particular action.

We have already established that this is **metaphysically conceivable** within our framework.

It is simply incompatible with the stronger claim:

> Alice is autonomous with respect to this particular decision.

So it isn't a contradiction in the ontology.

It's a change in the causal status of the creature.

---

# 10. This also clarifies providence

I think your earlier feeling that I had somewhat artificially connected providence and intervention was justified.

We can now define the relationship more precisely.

**Intervention** is a type of causal relation.

**Providence** is a higher-level property of the overall relationship between divine agency and the actual history.

Something like:

$$
Providence =
\text{the structured relation between divine actualization/intervention and the resulting history}.
$$

So providence doesn't need to be a separate causal mechanism.

It is an **architectural description** of the whole system.

That makes it much less ad hoc.

---

# 11. And now the remaining temporal question becomes sharply defined

You asked:

> Is there a temporal sequence outside our time?

I think we should **not answer this yet**.

We can instead distinguish three notions:

### 1. Logical/metaphysical priority

$$
G\prec S\prec H
$$

meaning that God's existence/agency is ontologically prior to the universe.

This does **not** imply temporal priority.

### 2. Temporal order inside the universe

$$
t_1<t_2<t_3.
$$

This is the ordinary causal sequence experienced by creatures.

### 3. Divine explanatory order

We may have:

$$
\text{divine agency}
\rightarrow
\text{actual world}
$$

without there being a cosmic clock on which:

> God exists at 12:00, creates the universe at 12:01.

That distinction is extremely useful.

So our current ontology requires **priority**, but does not yet require **a second temporal dimension**.

And that is precisely the question we should keep in the table rather than prematurely resolving.

---

# 12. Our current model in one diagram

I would currently represent it like this:

```text
                 NECESSARY REALITY
                         │
                         │ agent-causal capacity
                         ▼
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ Fundamental         │
              │ structure S         │
              │ laws / constants /  │
              │ boundary structure  │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         │
              ┌──────────▼──────────┐
              │ Possible histories  │
              │ / continuations     │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         │
              autonomous choices
                         │
                         ▼
                  ACTUAL HISTORY H*
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          │              │              │
     physical events   agents       interventions
          │              │              │
          └──────────────┴──────────────┘
                         │
                         ▼
                DIVINE KNOWLEDGE
                         │
          ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
          │                             │
  actuality-complete              possibility knowledge
   omniscience                     (OPEN EXTENSION)
```

The important asymmetry is:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Divine knowledge tracks actuality;
it does not constitute creaturely actuality.}
}
$$

while:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Actual history is jointly produced by
divine and creaturely causation.}
}
$$

but:

$$
\boxed{
\text{the two kinds of agency are not symmetrical.}
}
$$

That is becoming the central causal architecture of our model.

---

# 13. One important refinement to our earlier "generating set" idea

I think your insight about the generating set deserves to remain prominent.

If we have a sufficiently complete fundamental specification:

$$
G=\{g_1,g_2,\ldots,g_n\}
$$

then the physical universe can be generated from \(G\), at least modulo genuinely autonomous choices.

So:

$$
G + A_1+A_2+\cdots \rightarrow H.
$$

This gives us a useful distinction:

> **The universe need not be specified by God event-by-event.**

God could specify/actualize a relatively small generating structure, while the enormous complexity of the resulting world emerges from:

1. mathematical/physical consequences,
2. contingent initial conditions,
3. autonomous agency,
4. and possibly divine interventions.

That is substantially more economical than imagining God as manually determining every event.

And it fits your earlier objection about mathematical simplicity: **a compact description need not correspond to a simple underlying reality.** A small generating set can generate extraordinarily complex histories.

---

# 14. Where I think we should go next

I would now make the next stage quite formal.

### A. Formal causal model

Define precisely:

* necessary vs contingent,
* possible world,
* actual world,
* generating structure,
* causal determination,
* agent causation,
* intervention,
* free choice,
* counterfactual possibility.

Then we can ask mathematically:

$$
\text{What exactly does it mean for Alice's choice to be free?}
$$

and:

$$
\text{What exactly does it mean for God to know it?}
$$

That will let us attack the **foreknowledge/freedom problem** rather than merely describing it.

### B. Then formalize omniscience

Our provisional definition:

> **Actuality-complete omniscience:** knowledge of every truth concerning the complete actual reality, without requiring temporal acquisition or updating.

Then investigate possible extensions:

$$
\text{ACO}
\subseteq
\text{ACO + modal knowledge}
\subseteq
\text{ACO + counterfactual knowledge}
$$

and determine which extensions add genuine metaphysical commitments.

### C. Then intervention

Construct the complete taxonomy above and ask:

> Which interventions are compatible with which forms of agency, omniscience and atemporality?

### D. Then goodness

Only after the causal architecture is stable should we ask:

> Why would the necessary agent actualize *this* structure rather than another?

That is where your proposed conception of goodness becomes particularly interesting, because we can then distinguish:

$$
\text{what the agent chooses}
$$

from

$$
\text{why the agent chooses it}
$$

from

$$
\text{whether "good" is identical to the latter}.
$$

And we can finally confront the deeper question you have been circling:

> **Does an agent's nature constitute its reasons, or are reasons/goods metaphysically prior to the agent?**

That is where the theology of goodness will become genuinely nontrivial.

---

## Current research map

| Question                                 | Current position                            |                Confidence |
| ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------: |
| Necessary foundation?                    | Yes, provisionally                          |         High within model |
| Personal/agent foundation?               | Yes, provisionally                          |                  Moderate |
| Genuine libertarian agency?              | Yes, preferred                              | Open philosophical battle |
| Actuality-complete omniscience?          | Yes                                         |        Strongly preferred |
| Knowledge of possibilities?              | Likely compatible; not required             |                      Open |
| Counterfactual knowledge?                | Not required                                |                      Open |
| Divine timelessness?                     | Preferred                                   |              Open details |
| Temporal sequence outside universe?      | **Unresolved**                              |                      Open |
| Creation = determination of every event? | No                                          |                  Rejected |
| Joint divine/creaturely actualization?   | Yes                                         |              Core feature |
| Intervention possible?                   | Yes, several types                          |           Core hypothesis |
| Intervention destroys autonomy?          | Only if it determines an autonomous action  |                    Strong |
| Providence                               | Global architecture, not separate mechanism |        Working definition |
| Goodness                                 | Separate thesis                             |                  Deferred |
| Omnipotence                              | To derive                                   |                  Deferred |
| Omnipresence                             | To derive                                   |                  Deferred |

And I would explicitly mark one methodological principle alongside the table:

> **We are not trying to recover classical theism by definition. We are constructing the strongest coherent ontology available from the arguments we find compelling, and only subsequently asking how closely its necessary agent resembles the God of classical theism.**

That protects the project from exactly the bias you have repeatedly warned against: adding *God-like* properties merely because we want the conclusion to be God.

The next genuinely difficult step, therefore, is **not another argument for God**. It is to make the causal model sufficiently precise that we can determine whether an atemporal necessary agent + genuine libertarian agents + actuality-complete omniscience is internally coherent, and exactly what additional assumptions are required to get from that architecture toward the stronger traditional attributes.
